# Air Line Pilots Association v. Miller — neutral preflight

Prepared for a fresh independent model. This preparation supplies no vote, disposition, or finding. Read with the frozen May 21 Current-Term Neutral Projection and the chunk-9 entering-law slice. No same-matter Supreme Court disposition, private position, or comparator was used. Research cutoff: May 26, 1998; source retrieval occurred October 24, 2026.

## Event, channel, questions, and corrected posture

No. 97-428; argument March 23, 1998; scheduled decision May 26, 1998. Review under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) of *Miller v. Air Line Pilots Association*, **108 F.3d 1415 (D.C. Cir. March 14, 1997), No. 96-7033**. The supplied packet incorrectly identifies the Sixth Circuit. This is ordinary review of the D.C. Circuit's reversal and remand of the district court's agency-fee judgment; it is not review of a Sixth Circuit judgment.

The authorized question is whether nonmember employees must exhaust the union's impartial arbitration procedure before litigating their compulsory-fee objections in federal court when they have not agreed to arbitrate. This is an analytical question formulation, not a purported verbatim grant order. ALPA seeks reversal of the rejection of mandatory exhaustion; the pilots seek affirmance and independent judicial adjudication. The original packet's employee argument was not the petitioner's requested change. The amount ultimately chargeable, each challenged expenditure, the audit, and discovery disputes remain distinct from the exhaustion question.

The dated argument and event anchors are supplied objective procedural facts. No additional grant event, timetable, mandate, or filing is reconstructed. The frozen roster supplies Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. No case-specific nonparticipation is established; validate at submission and decision without inventing a recusal.

## Supported record and lower judgment

ALPA represents Delta pilots under the Railway Labor Act. Its 1991 agreement with Delta established an agency shop effective January 1992. Nonmembers were required to contribute to collective representation; their objection is to the chargeable proportion, not a claimed agreement to arbitrate. ALPA initially discounted fees about 8% using 1990 expenses, then about 17% using 1991 expenses, and finally determined that 19% of 1992 expenses were nongermane. It credited or rebated objectors with interest. The 153 plaintiffs disputed ALPA's calculations and procedures.

ALPA treated protests as requests for arbitration under its own Policies and Procedures, over the litigating pilots' objection. AAA appointed an arbitrator from a labor-relations panel. Some pilots had already sued before the agency shop became effective. The district court refused to enjoin arbitration; the arbitrator refused to postpone it, and counsel entered a conditional appearance. The lower opinion reports 158 arbitration participants, of whom 91 became parties to this suit; 67 participants did not challenge the award. Another 62 plaintiffs did not participate. Thus the litigants before the court were nonparticipants or participants under protest. The sanitized objective extract reports 174 timely objectors. Those figures concern different groups—objectors and participants—and are not treated as a factual conflict or silently made identical.

The arbitrator largely sustained ALPA's allocations and declined requested discovery. In court ALPA resisted a request for documents concerning 20 sample projects, offering its entire 1992 expense records instead. The district court granted judgment largely for ALPA and ruled that arbitration had to be exhausted, relying on the arbitrator's findings on recordkeeping and overhead.

The D.C. Circuit reversed the exhaustion requirement. It distinguished an impartial arbitrator from the final internal authority of a governmental agency or benefit plan; it found no assent or statutory basis to defer to the arbitrator. It rejected ALPA's attempt to distinguish the circuit's NLRA decision in *Abrams* from this RLA case. Its separate rulings included: political government-relations expenditures were to be separated from safety-related collective bargaining; the 1990/1991 notice challenge lacked injury on the reported facts; auditors need not decide legal germaneness; professional audit adequacy remained contestable; and recordkeeping and overhead required independent findings. It left discovery management below. These are lower-court rulings, not Supreme Court holdings or a reason to expand the authorized question.

## Entering law and competing paths

**Surviving received authority.** *Hudson*, 475 U.S. 292 (1986), requires an adequate fee explanation, a reasonably prompt opportunity to challenge the fee before an impartial decisionmaker, and escrow for reasonably disputed amounts. It does not identify arbitration as the exclusive impartial forum. Its majority expressly denies preclusive effect to the arbitrator's decision in a subsequent §1983 action. White's concurrence, joined by Burger, favors exhaustion when a union supplies compliant arbitration. That is a separate writing, not the majority's rule. *Hanson*, *Street*, *Allen*, *Abood*, *Ellis*, *Beck*, and pre-divergence *Lehnert* supply the received union-security, chargeability, and objector-protection background at their actual scope. The parties' agreement that Hudson safeguards apply to this RLA arrangement is reported below; it is not an independent new holding about every private union or the NLRA.

*AT&T Technologies*, 475 U.S. 643 (1986), and *Gateway Coal*, 414 U.S. 368 (1974), supply the agreement basis of contractual arbitration. The frozen Standards module **Independent determination and delegation of arbitrability**, effective May 22, 1995, requires judicial determination of assent absent clear and unmistakable delegation under ordinary contract law. Appearing solely to object does not itself establish assent. Breyer's antecedent-assent rule has all nine joins; the eight Associates also join its conditional actual-delegation review proposition. Its arbitration-review rule does not itself answer whether a distinct exhaustion obligation can arise by law.

**Judicial exhaustion.** Simulated *McCarthy v. Madigan*, March 4, 1992, preserves statutory primacy and sound judicial discretion consistent with the statutory scheme when no command applies. Its actual agency-authority, expertise, correction, fact-development, efficiency, available-relief and access considerations matter; it does not create an automatic no-exhaustion rule outside its damages-only BOP setting. Its governing framework has Blackmun, Stone, White, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy and Souter; Scalia and Thomas supported the particular judgment only on the unavailable-compensation ground. The Standards module **Judicial exhaustion and damages-only federal prison medical-care claims** retains that framework outside a controlling statutory command; the PLRA amendment concerns confined-prisoner litigation, not these pilots.

**ALPA's supported path.** Hudson compels provision of an impartial expert procedure; a reasonable exhaustion requirement could allow that procedure to narrow facts, correct errors and reduce duplicative litigation while preserving later judicial review. White's concurrence and the reported *Lancaster* circuit position support the argument. Its strongest obstacle is source and fit: a compelled protective opportunity is not necessarily a duty imposed on nonconsenting claimants, and this neutral arbitrator is neither the union's senior policymaking official nor a statutory administrative tribunal. Efficiency alone does not identify the scope of lawful compulsion or deference. An exhaustion path must specify whether judicial review remains independent and what lawful source imposes the prior step; it cannot assume a binding award or consent from protest.

**Pilots' supported path.** They never assented, the reported statutory framework establishes no compulsory-arbitration gate for this fee dispute, and Hudson's protection gives a prompt option without surrender of the judicial forum. The strongest counterargument is that assent principles address contractual binding arbitration, while courts sometimes impose nonbinding exhaustion as judicial administration. The model must address that distinction through McCarthy and the actual procedure, not dismiss it by relabeling exhaustion as contractual waiver. The limited question does not require deciding every exhaustion doctrine, consenting party, future fee amount, or fee-calculation claim.

**Remedy.** Affirmance on exhaustion would preserve independent judicial treatment and the lower court's remaining bounded directions; it would establish no particular fee refund, discovery plan, or substantive chargeability result. Reversal must identify the exact consequence for already completed arbitration and those who did not participate, without inventing forfeiture, assent, statutory deference or a new award. A narrower correction/remand is available if an impermissible deference rule is distinct from a lawful preliminary process. No path automatically binds absent objectors or creates a class action.

## Sources, chronology, and readiness

Read the entire lower opinion, including both footnotes: [CAP judicial text](https://static.case.law/f3d/108/html/1415-01.html), saved as [air_line_pilots_cap.txt](../research/lower/air_line_pilots_cap.txt) with original HTML adjacent. Factual identifiers and argument date are independently checked against the sanitized [objective source extract](../runtime/OT_1997CHUNK9_OBJECTIVE_SOURCES_MAY26.md), ALPA block only. The source is an objective extraction ending before the same-matter Supreme Court holding, not an invitation to open the full opinion.

Exact entering-law modules are mechanically copied in [LATER_STANDARDS](../entering-law/OT_1997CHUNK9_LATER_STANDARDS.md); McCarthy's complete entry is copied in [LATER_EXTRACT](../entering-law/OT_1997CHUNK9_LATER_EXTRACT.md). The full authorized slice and frozen neutral projection control over this navigation copy. They also provide the published judicial coalitions and qualifications for independent assessment; no historical authorship in this matter is imported.

Original arbitration papers, complete merits briefs and appendix were not independently recovered. The published lower opinion supplies the procedure, nonconsent, preserved dispute and remand limits necessary for the authorized exhaustion issue. No material premise presently requires an archive search. A proposed ruling on an unquoted policy term, actual consent by a particular pilot, preservation outside the reported issues, or the final fee amount requires additional source work; missing research is not a party default.

This May 26 event uses the common pre-group May 21 baseline. No uncoordinated May 26 case governs it merely because it is processed earlier. Ready for independent modeling on the stated question and supported record; preparation supplies no completed Court action.

## Calderon v. Ashmus — No. 97-391

### Identity, vehicle and questions

Argued March 24, 1998; proposed decision May 26, 1998. Certiorari under §1254(1) to the Ninth Circuit, 123 F.3d 1199 (August 18, 1997). The circuit reviewed the preliminary injunction under §1292(a)(1), not a final declaratory judgment under §1291: other district-court claims remained pending. It reviewed the declaration's premises as bound up with the injunction, affirmed the injunction with a clarification, and denied appellate §1988 fees because it had reached no merits of the §1983 claim.

**Corrected scope:** The granted issues concern the Eleventh Amendment and the First Amendment objection to an injunction prohibiting state officials from invoking chapter 154. Article III is an antecedent question that the Court must resolve when disputed; it is not inaccurately recast here as the sole question actually granted. If Article III is satisfied, the granted immunity and speech/petitioning questions remain live. California's actual statutory qualification is the premise of the lower injunction, but a merits decision on every qualification defect is not automatically within the grant. The precise formal grant text is not separately recovered; this description follows the sanitized official source recitation.

### Record and the judgment below

Ashmus filed on April 24, 1996, for a class of California death-sentenced prisoners awaiting execution while pursuing state and federal challenges, except those whose convictions and sentences had been affirmed on direct review before June 6, 1989. California asserted that its unitary-review system qualified under §2265 and threatened to invoke chapter 154. The lower court found that more than 130 class members lacked appointed counsel and that appointment could take years. The class also includes prisoners with counsel. These are reported lower-court findings and descriptions, not proof of each class member's personal filing deadline.

The district court declared California unqualified and enjoined the officials from seeking chapter 154 benefits in proceedings involving class members. The circuit clarified the injunction to address the current state of California law in capital habeas cases; it permitted later modification if events changed. It treated the threatened application as a present pressure to file an inadequately developed petition or risk the shorter period, and held that prospective officer relief was available. It rejected the First Amendment objection because officials remained free to criticize the ruling; their effort to claim the adjudicated litigation benefit was restrained. The circuit did not reach the §1983 merits.

The circuit identified separate qualification deficiencies: nonbinding recommended competency standards rather than a highest-court rule; inadequate compensation for investigation beyond the appellate record; and failure actually to appoint counsel after an indigent prisoner's acceptance. It expressly rejected an automatic requirement of prior habeas experience for appointed counsel. These findings must not become a Supreme Court merits holding merely because review of the injunction presents them. The lower opinion's paraphrase suggesting any late first petition becomes successive is not the statutory text and is not adopted as entering law.

### Entering snapshots

1. **Article III and declaratory relief.** Seminole Tribe (March 27, 1996), Part I, has seven direct joins—O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer—for conclusive adjudication of legal relations despite later administrative steps. An immediate coercive decree is not necessary to judicial power. Raines (June 26, 1997) requires concrete personal injury but decides an individual-legislator claim, not all anticipatory litigation. Steel Co. (June 2, 1997), with its exact exception and sequencing limits, requires Article III before contested statutory merits. Current Texas (March 31, 1998) and Ohio Forestry (May 18, 1998), both unanimous, examine fitness and actual present hardship; neither requires physical injury or violation of law before review. Neither decides the different concrete pressure asserted here. The Declaratory Judgment Act, §§2201–2202, supplies a remedy in a controversy otherwise within federal jurisdiction; §1331 does not remove Article III.
2. **State officials and remedy.** Seminole Tribe's officer holding (Souter, Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer) permits relief against a connected officer for an ongoing federal duty within that statute's complete remedial limits; it supplies no universal statutory exception. Coeur d'Alene (June 23, 1997; the same five) rejects a categorical sovereign-lands exception but requires an actual ongoing federal violation, a connected official, and proper prospective relief. Green v. Mansour, 474 U.S. 64 (1985), preserves the absence-of-ongoing-violation objection. Neither a federal litigation position nor a future federal court's possible error is automatically an officer's present violation. No IGRA burden, mediation step or contempt limit is transplanted to AEDPA.
3. **AEDPA.** The annex supplies the exact modules “Qualifying State counsel systems for special capital habeas procedures” and “Capital habeas filing and claim scope under chapter 154,” both effective April 24, 1996. The unitary route's transcript deferral, express counsel-continuity request, appointment order, compensation and competency predicates are separate. The 180-day period and each tolling provision are preserved; no prisoner's clock is calculated here. Chapter 154's applicability to pending cases does not prove California's qualification. Chapter 153's one-year limitation remains a separate regime.

### Meaningful lawful alternatives and limits

**Article III path A:** A standalone declaration selects a procedural rule for future custody claims without adjudicating their substantive entitlement. Aetna Life v. Haworth, 300 U.S. 227 (1937), requires a definite dispute and conclusive relief; Coffman v. Breeze Corporations, 323 U.S. 316 (1945), and Public Service Commission v. Wycoff, 344 U.S. 237 (1952), support testing whether an isolated defensive issue is an advisory fragment. The strongest objection is the present forced filing choice, counsel delay and potentially irreversible loss asserted below: a declaration may settle a concrete legal relation even though habeas merits remain. Seminole forecloses dismissal merely because the relief is declaratory. If jurisdiction fails, vacate the operative injunction/declaration as appropriate and remand for jurisdictional treatment; do not pronounce California qualified or decide the underlying convictions.

**Article III path B:** The common qualification issue determines an immediate statutory filing burden and can be conclusively settled between adverse parties. Texas and Ohio Forestry permit review of established present burdens even if other proceedings follow. The strongest objection is that qualification can be litigated in an actual habeas proceeding and this suit attempts to pre-adjudicate only one component. A model supporting jurisdiction must identify the legal relation settled and whose concrete injury is redressed, rather than rest on expense or aggregate efficiency.

**If jurisdiction exists:** Compare the connected-officer/ongoing-violation route with the argument that asserting a disputed procedural entitlement for another court to resolve is no present violation of federal law. A speech-based invalidation requires distinguishing a legitimate injunction enforcing an actual adjudication from a prohibition on seeking judicial consideration of a disputed legal argument. A narrower remedy could remove an impermissible anti-assertion injunction while preserving a declaration, but only if that declaration independently satisfies jurisdiction, immunity and statutory remedy. The speech objection cannot supply jurisdiction or silently eliminate the granted immunity issue. No general censorship holding or statewide finding of bad faith is supplied.

### Justice-specific source anchors, without commitments

| Justice | Own date-eligible position that must be tested |
|---|---|
| Stevens | Author of unanimous Texas; his serious judicial-function doubt in Seminole is not a join to its Part I. He joined its actual officer route and Coeur d'Alene. His standing concern must be tested against the concrete pressure here. |
| O'Connor | Joined Seminole Part I's conclusive-declaration rule but not its officer result; joined Raines and both current ripeness opinions. Her Steel Co. join reserves other legitimate exceptional sequences. |
| Scalia | Author of Raines and current Ohio Forestry; joined Seminole Part I but rejected its officer result. Concrete present consequences and absence of an automatic execution requirement both matter. |
| Kennedy | Joined Seminole Part I, Raines and current ripeness holdings; did not join the Seminole officer result and reserved exceptional sequencing with O'Connor in Steel Co. |
| Souter | Author of Seminole's conclusive-adjudication and bounded officer holdings; joined current Texas/Ohio Forestry. A viable declaration does not itself establish this action's actual controversy. |
| Thomas | Joined Seminole Part I, Raines, Steel Co.'s relevant rule and both current ripeness decisions; no automatic rejection of declaratory jurisdiction may be attributed to him. |
| Ginsburg | Joined Seminole's judicial-function and officer holdings, Coeur d'Alene, Raines and both current ripeness decisions. Concrete injury and proper officer relief remain independent requirements. |
| Breyer | Joined Seminole and Coeur d'Alene, and the current unanimous ripeness holdings. His current Spencer opinion separately insists on actual continuing Article III injury while preserving procedural predicates. |

**Sources:** complete [Ninth Circuit opinion](../research/lower/ashmus_v_calderon_cap.txt), originally 123 F.3d 1199; [CAP primary case text](https://static.case.law/f3d/123/html/1199-01.html); the objective official extract; and the law annex. Post-divergence Supreme Court authorities quoted by the lower court are controlling only in their actual inherited form, especially Coeur d'Alene and Seminole. No later Supreme Court Ashmus analysis is an input.

## County of Sacramento v. Lewis — No. 96-1337

### Identity, channel and preserved case

Argued December 9, 1997; proposed decision May 26, 1998. Certiorari under §1254(1) to the Ninth Circuit, 98 F.3d 434 (October 9, 1996). The lower appeal followed final summary judgment under §1291, rather than an interlocutory evidentiary-sufficiency appeal. The question concerns the culpability required for the substantive-due-process claim from this pursuit; individual qualified immunity is an independently available, potentially dispositive ground. The circuit reversed Smith's individual immunity judgment but affirmed the municipal judgments. Plaintiffs did not appeal the state-law dispositions. No cross-petition enlarging those municipal or state components is established. The County's appearance in the caption does not reopen its favorable judgment.

### Record, assumed facts and lower rationale

On May 22, 1990, Smith and Stapp had finished responding to a fight when a motorcycle driven by minor Brian Willard, with sixteen-year-old Philip Lewis as passenger, passed. Neither boy was involved in the fight. On the plaintiff-favorable account, Smith could not hear Stapp's command; the motorcycle passed slowly between the patrol cars and then accelerated. Smith made a three-point turn and pursued. The chase lasted about seventy-five seconds over 1.3 miles, averaged about sixty miles per hour, reached approximately one hundred, and involved residential roads, traffic lights and turns. Neither rider wore a helmet. At points Smith followed 100–150 feet behind despite a substantially longer stopping distance. Two cars and a bicyclist were forced from the road in the account credited for summary judgment.

The motorcycle stopped after a crest and turn; whether Lewis remained seated or dismounted is unclear. Smith braked but struck Lewis, who died. The lower opinion expressly reports it undisputed that Smith did not intend the collision. Plaintiffs asserted neither a Fourth nor Eighth Amendment claim. Smith's motives cannot be newly characterized as a purpose to injure merely from dangerous speed, and the account cannot be silently converted into a finding that the boys were known dangerous felons.

The departmental order required evaluating seriousness, necessity and danger, broadcasting specified pursuit information, and aborting when the hazards outweighed immediate apprehension. The lower opinion reports noncommunication conceded and other violations alleged/supported on the favorable view. Those rules may inform evidence of disregard; their breach is neither a federal constitutional rule nor the relevant law that establishes federal notice. The district court assumed a violation and granted individual immunity. The circuit used deliberate indifference/reckless disregard, found a triable issue and treated pre-May-1990 cases as adequate notice. It relied on Wood II, Fargo as modified, Roach, Jones, Checki, Garner and Brower, preserving their respective facts and outcomes.

### Entering snapshots

1. **Constitutional source and underlying wrong.** Albright (January 24, 1994) has no controlling rationale; its seven-vote judgment is not a general displacement rule. Graham v. Connor, 490 U.S. 386 (1989), and actual unanimous Lanier (March 31, 1997) require use of a specific provision where it covers the conduct, but do not exclude all noncustodial official wrongdoing. Brower v. County of Inyo, 489 U.S. 593 (1989), requires termination through intentionally applied means for seizure. An unintended collision and failed command to stop do not automatically establish that predicate. Daniels, 474 U.S. 327 (1986), excludes negligence while reserving recklessness/gross-negligence questions; Rochin, 342 U.S. 165 (1952), supplies the conscience-shocking official-abuse tradition.
2. **Context and culpability.** Collins (February 26, 1992) separates a substantive wrong from municipal attribution, and its eight-Justice alternative arbitrariness holding is confined to the pleaded voluntary-workplace omissions. Pinder (May 21, 1996) distinguishes affirmative deprivation from an omitted protection duty; it reserves the former's merits and culpability. Uhlrig (February 18, 1997) likewise decides conduct-time notice without adopting the lower court's five-part test. Farmer (June 6, 1994) requires actual awareness and unreasonable disregard for the prison-safety claim, with reasonable-response and permissible-inference limits. It is an analogy, not an already controlling police-pursuit test. Doe v. Taylor (October 16, 1995) concerns actual supervisory knowledge, authority, reasonable opportunity and causation in deliberate sexual abuse; its five-vote rule is not a universal emergency standard.
3. **Emergency analogy.** O'Connor's Whitley v. Albers, 475 U.S. 312 (1986), distinguishes competing emergency obligations from ordinary deliberate-indifference settings, while expressly reserving the Fourteenth Amendment culpability question outside prison security. Stevens joined Marshall's dissent except its final due-process footnote, emphasizing wantonness and preservation of disputed emergency facts. Whitley therefore supplies both a serious analogy and a material limit; neither its holding nor the dissent already resolves this civilian pursuit.
4. **Immunity and remedy.** Harlow/Anderson, actual Elder, Pinder, Uhlrig and Lanier require all relevant law and particularized apparent unlawfulness, without a factual twin or numerical consensus. The notice cutoff is May 22, 1990. Later clarity cannot supply earlier notice. Current Smith v. Mattox (January 12, 1998) permits obvious applications on complete assumed facts, rather than injury alone, and retains immunity separately from underlying liability. It supplies no Fourth Amendment merits rule for this nonseizure claim. The annex includes the relevant exact qualified-immunity module and each authority limit.

### Meaningful alternatives and objections

**Path A—emergency-sensitive constitutional rule:** Require a culpability showing that distinguishes genuine abuse from even grave bad judgment during a rapidly developing pursuit. Whitley's competing-obligation analysis supports asking whether a harmful purpose unrelated to a legitimate enforcement aim or comparably arbitrary abuse is present. Its strongest objection is that pursuit was voluntary, no violent offense justified immediate capture on the favorable record, and the officer allegedly created and prolonged the danger; no custody or riot analogy makes all such choices immune. Any purpose-based rule must explain its precise trigger and what changes if there was time for actual deliberation. A model cannot invent proof of good faith as an adjudicated fact.

**Path B—conscious disregard may suffice on this account:** Apply a serious actual-awareness rule where an officer creates life-threatening danger without a supported necessity and has a meaningful opportunity to desist. Farmer, Doe, the direct-deprivation distinction and the preconduct circuit sources support examining culpability rather than categorical custody. Its strongest objection is that those settings supply different duties, time for reflection and causal structures; subjective awareness of pursuit danger is not necessarily arbitrary abuse. The model must identify whether the summary-judgment account permits the requisite inference, without making policy violation negligence per se under the Constitution or importing Canton’s municipal attribution standard as the underlying right.

**Path C—notice-only disposition:** Even if the assumed conduct could support a constitutional violation, the May 1990 law may not have made this pursuit's unlawfulness apparent. This route preserves the culpability merits and reverses only the individual immunity denial. The strongest objection is that Checki, Wood II and the existing deadly-force principles may have supplied particularized warning or obvious coverage. Roach and Jones rejected liability on their actual facts; their cautionary language is not a judgment sustaining this claim. A finding that no exact pursuit precedent exists is insufficient. Any affirmance of immunity denial still leaves actual culpability and causation for ordinary proceedings; no damages award or automatic trial command follows.

### Individual source anchors

| Justice | Material own authorities and limits |
|---|---|
| Stevens | Authored Collins, including its alternative arbitrariness holding; joined Whitley's dissent except the due-process footnote; joined Farmer and Doe's affirmative supervisory rule and dissented from Pinder's immunity disposition. These do not remove his join to Uhlrig's notice-only holding. |
| O'Connor | Authored Whitley and Pinder; joined Farmer's actual-awareness framework and all eight Associates in Uhlrig. Test emergency analogy and conduct-time notice separately. |
| Scalia | Authored Brower's intentional-means seizure rule and current Smith v. Mattox; joined Collins, Farmer, Pinder's notice disposition and Uhlrig. His obvious-force application cannot be treated as automatic immunity or automatic nonseizure liability. |
| Kennedy | Joined Collins, Farmer, Pinder's notice result and Uhlrig; his Albright separate ground was not the specific-source plurality. The actual unanimous Lanier join controls its narrower propositions. |
| Souter | Authored Farmer; joined Collins; dissented from Pinder's notice disposition, but joined Uhlrig. His Albright additional-injury ground supplies no Court rule. |
| Thomas | Joined Collins, Pinder's notice result, Uhlrig and Lanier; Farmer was judgment-only for him. Do not attribute Farmer's full subjective-culpability reasoning to Thomas through the judgment. |
| Ginsburg | Authored Elder and Doe; joined Farmer, dissented from Pinder's immunity disposition and joined Uhlrig. Direct and supervisory wrongdoing remain distinct from omitting protection. |
| Breyer | Authored Uhlrig's bounded notice disposition; joined Doe, Pinder's dissent and Lanier. He was not on the Court for Farmer or Collins, so their other Justices' joins cannot be imputed to him. |

**Sources:** complete [lower opinion](../research/lower/lewis_v_sacramento_cap.txt), [CAP primary text](https://static.case.law/f3d/98/html/0434-01.html); official objective extract; [Whitley official opinion](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-475/pdf/USREPORTS-475-312.pdf), downloaded and fully read in [local text](../research/chunk9_neutral_may26/whitley.txt); and exact law annex. Lower citations to later Supreme Court opinions are used only in their actual simulated form. The lower opinion contains typographical/OCR errors; they are not new factual propositions.

## Kiowa Tribe of Oklahoma v. Manufacturing Technologies — No. 96-1037

### Identity, posture and material record

Argued January 12, 1998; proposed decision May 26, 1998. Certiorari under §1257 to the Oklahoma Court of Civil Appeals, First Division, following state high-court refusal of review confirmed by control. The supplied packet's attribution of the merits holding to the Oklahoma Supreme Court is corrected. The trial court denied the Tribe's immunity motion and entered judgment for Manufacturing Technologies; the intermediate court affirmed on an off-reservation commercial-contract exception. The Tribe seeks reversal on immunity. No individual officer, separate corporation or independently pleaded enforcement route is substituted for the Tribe.

The federally recognized Tribe's industrial development commission agreed to purchase stock from Manufacturing Technologies. Its business-committee chairman signed a $285,000 note plus interest in the Tribe's name. The note recites Carnegie, where the Tribe has a trust-land complex, as execution place; the creditor asserts execution and delivery in Oklahoma City and payment there. Location remains disputed. The note lacks a governing-law clause and expressly preserves sovereign rights. Default and the monetary judgment supply a concrete controversy. The present question is whether federal tribal immunity permits this direct contract action assuming the off-reservation commercial setting asserted below, absent waiver or congressional authorization. A categorical immunity rule could make the location dispute immaterial; a geographic limitation could make it indispensable.

### Entering snapshots

**Tribal immunity is distinct from state immunity and substantive regulation.** Potawatomi, 498 U.S. 505 (February 26, 1991), unanimously preserved immunity absent clear tribal waiver or congressional abrogation, rejected waiver merely from initiating suit and rejected a commercial-business limitation for the trust-land cigarette operation. It distinguished a substantive state tax obligation from an action against the Tribe. Its majority treated trust land as Indian country and distinguished Mescalero's off-reservation setting. It did not directly decide this off-reservation commercial contract.

Stevens's separate Potawatomi writing accepted the established damages-immunity rule but expressly questioned whether it extended to off-territory commerce and distinguished prospective equitable relief. His Puyallup III opinion, 433 U.S. 165 (1977), applied tribal immunity to the Tribe while preserving properly obtained jurisdiction over individual members; an order addressed to the Tribe required its own lawful basis. Participation representing members did not waive immunity. Santa Clara Pueblo, 436 U.S. 49, 58–59 (1978), supplies the requirement of an unequivocal waiver and preserves congressional responsibility. Mescalero Apache Tribe v. Jones, 411 U.S. 145 (1973), concerns applicable nondiscriminatory law beyond reservation borders; applicability is not itself a holding about a direct damages remedy.

Actual Seminole Tribe concerns State/connected-officer amenability under IGRA and establishes no reciprocal tribal consent, common immunity rule for every sovereign or direct holding about private tribal contracts. Actual Chickasaw Nation separates member residence, legal tax incidence and express authorization. Current Alaska v. Venetie (February 25, 1998) separates political status from land classification; it does not make tribal recognition disappear off Indian country or decide immunity. Current Montana v. Crow Tribe (May 18, 1998) separates invalid state taxation from a tribe's entitlement to another payer's money; neither automatic tribal recovery nor automatic private recovery follows from it.

No statutory abrogation or actual waiver is established in the neutral materials. That is a limit on this claim as presented, not an invented finding that no law or document could ever support one. Contract liability, power to make the contract, immunity from suit and execution against particular assets are separate questions.

### Supported alternative paths

**Path A—preserve immunity absent authorized exception.** The Tribe is the defendant and the sought relief is a money judgment. The established status-based immunity, unequivocal-waiver requirement, congressional treatment and distinction between substantive obligation and amenability support rejecting a new commercial/geographic exception. Strongest counterargument: the cited holdings largely arose on tribal lands or involved collective tribal rights, and an off-territory commercial actor imposes risk on nonmembers outside its internal government. A rule preserving immunity must confront that scope argument rather than pretend Potawatomi already adjudicated these facts. It may leave the geographic dispute unresolved because neither asserted location changes its legal result.

**Path B—confine the common-law immunity in off-reservation commerce.** Stevens's Potawatomi reservation, Mescalero's ordinary-state-law principle and the limited factual reach of earlier immunity cases support examining whether immunity follows the tribe into this market transaction. Strongest counterargument: substantive regulation and forum amenability have been deliberately separated; a judicial exception may undermine an established federal protection on which Congress legislates. A model must specify whether it distinguishes or limits precedent and why congressional authority does not foreclose the common-law refinement. If the exception depends on off-reservation execution/performance, the contested location cannot be found against the Tribe without the actual lower factual resolution; a bounded remand is available. A broad commercial exception, including reservation activity, faces Potawatomi directly.

**Path C—waiver or different defendant:** Not presently supported. Signing a payment obligation, defaulting or promising payment in a city is not itself an unequivocal consent to suit; the express sovereign-rights clause points the other way. No independent official, entity or in-rem claim is invented to circumvent the actual defendant. A request to rely on such a path requires the particular document, authorization and preservation source first.

### Individual source anchors

Stevens must be assessed from both his Puyallup immunity opinion and his explicit Potawatomi off-territory reservation. O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter joined the unanimous Potawatomi opinion, preserving its trust-land scope and congressional concerns; none joined Stevens's separate reservation. O'Connor's Sac and Fox opinion and Ginsburg's Chickasaw opinion distinguish territorial substantive-tax predicates, rather than decide amenability. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas joined Ginsburg's Chickasaw off-country-income portion; that specific join cannot be generalized into a tribe-suit exception. Ginsburg and Breyer joined the actual Seminole officer holding and current unanimous Alaska and Crow decisions; Thomas joined the current unanimous Alaska and Crow holdings. All three later appointees require direct application of the inherited tribal cases; no Potawatomi join is falsely attributed to them. The fresh model must test the same two paths for each Justice, and identify additional personal authority if it is necessary to justify a material exception.

**Sources:** the sanitized official objective extract; [Potawatomi official opinion](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-498/pdf/USREPORTS-498-505.pdf) and [local full text](../research/chunk9_neutral_may26/potawatomi.txt); [Puyallup III official opinion](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-433/pdf/USREPORTS-433-165.pdf) and [local full text](../research/chunk9_neutral_may26/puyallup.txt), both downloaded and fully read; actual inherited/current law annex. No same-matter historical Supreme Court reasoning was researched.



<!-- frozen-source: OT_1997CHUNK9_NEUTRAL_MAY26_LAW.md -->

# May 26, 1998 — exact selected entering-law source annex

This is a mechanically copied reading annex to NEUTRAL_MAY26. It is authority only through its identified source. No same-day May 26 decision enters another. The underlying source files remain controlling.

Source SHA-256: entering-law 0ccf978132798bdec6ec2299d0b2287843c0e61cc31f525d54c154b1909fe088; neutral projection 92026c6446a07a02a0c9698b01d33aa98ebd7310da651b25b88eb14c6375f8fa.

## Inherited Holdings — verbatim selected sections

#### Mississippi v. Louisiana, 506 U.S. 73 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-1158.

**Decided:** December 14, 1992.

##### An interstate complaint belongs to the exclusive original forum

**Holding:** Section 1251(a) denies a federal district court jurisdiction to grant relief on an actual controversy between two states, even when the complaint is joined to private litigation and this Court previously declined an original action. The appellate judgment is reversed insofar as it adjudicates Louisiana’s complaint against Mississippi, with instructions to direct dismissal of that complaint for want of jurisdiction.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone-Zsela’s opinion, joined by the other eight Justices; unanimous controlling holding.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- California v. Arizona, 440 U.S. 59 — applied; this Court is the exclusive federal forum for a state’s claim against another state.
- Illinois v. City of Milwaukee, 406 U.S. 91 — distinguished as to different defendants and applied to preserve original-forum discretion.
- Ohio v. Wyandotte Chemicals Corp., 401 U.S. 493 — preserved; discretion does not grant another court exclusive interstate jurisdiction.
- Texas v. New Mexico, 462 U.S. 554 — preserved; case-specific discretion does not negate §1251(a).
- Wyoming v. Oklahoma, January 22, 1992 — distinguished; its original-forum exercise and separate objections supply no district-court authority here.

##### Private title questions remain distinct from binding interstate relief

**Holding:** Section 1251(a) does not bar an otherwise lawful private-title action merely because deciding title requires locating a state boundary; such adjudication does not bind the states on their boundary. Louisiana’s intervention that seeks no relief against Mississippi is unaffected, and the remaining title claims return for a determination whether the existing record permits fair resolution or additional proceedings are necessary.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone-Zsela’s opinion, joined by the other eight Justices; unanimous controlling holding and remand limitation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Durfee v. Duke, 375 U.S. 106 — applied; adjudication between private parties does not bind the states on their boundary.
- California v. Arizona, 440 U.S. 59 — applied to the actual interstate controversy; it does not withdraw otherwise lawful private-title jurisdiction.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court decides no interstate boundary, private title, accretion, avulsion or sovereign acquiescence issue. Party agreement or convenience cannot create district-court jurisdiction over the interstate complaint.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the interstate adjudication and direct dismissal of Louisiana’s complaint against Mississippi for want of jurisdiction. Remand the remaining title claims to determine whether the record permits fair resolution or further district proceedings are needed; Louisiana’s nonadverse intervention is unaffected.

#### Kokkonen v. Guardian Life Insurance Co. of America, 511 U.S. 375

**Docket or dockets:** No. 93-263.

**Decided:** 1994-05-16.

##### Postdismissal contract enforcement requires a jurisdictional basis

**Holding:** After a Rule 41(a)(1)(ii) stipulated dismissal that neither incorporates settlement obligations nor retains jurisdiction, a court has no ancillary or inherent jurisdiction to enforce the separate settlement merely because it ended a federal action; an independent jurisdictional basis is required. Neither factual interdependence nor authority to manage proceedings supports jurisdiction when the settlement-breach facts are separate from the former claim and breach violates no obligation in the dismissal order; judicial awareness or approval of the agreement is insufficient.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's Opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg; all nine adopt the rule, application, and reversal with remand.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Willy v. Coastal Corp., 503 U.S. 131 (1992): distinguished; collateral Rule 11 authority does not create jurisdiction over a new settlement contract.
- Julian v. Central Trust Co., 193 U.S. 93 (1904): distinguished by its express reservation concerning judicially conveyed property; broad language is not general settlement-enforcement authority.
- Moore v. New York Cotton Exchange, 270 U.S. 593 (1926): its factual-interdependence basis is inapplicable to these distinct controversies.
- Chambers v. NASCO, Inc., 501 U.S. 32 (1991): its authority over litigation misconduct does not convert every settlement breach into interference with judicial proceedings.
- McNutt v. General Motors Acceptance Corp., 298 U.S. 178 (1936): applied; the party asserting federal jurisdiction bears the burden of establishing it.

##### A lawful dismissal order may make settlement compliance enforceable

**Holding:** A court may enforce settlement obligations through ancillary jurisdiction when, within its lawful dismissal authority, its order incorporates those obligations or expressly retains jurisdiction over the settlement, making breach a violation of the judicial disposition. Under Rule 41(a)(2) the court may impose such terms in its discretion; under Rule 41(a)(1)(ii) it may incorporate or retain jurisdiction if the parties agree, but private consent alone, awareness of the bargain, or an attempted later addition after jurisdiction has ended does not create jurisdiction.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg; all nine adopt this qualification defining the boundary of the jurisdictional holding.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Julian's actual reservation-based holding remains effective; Rule 41's distinct mechanisms are applied without creating a mandatory retention rule. Rule 60(b) reopening is reserved and is not treated as contract enforcement.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court decides jurisdiction, not breach, specific performance, damages, Rule 60(b) reopening or diversity for a new claim. Neither private consent nor postdismissal judicial rewriting supplies lost jurisdiction.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Ninth Circuit is reversed and remanded for dismissal of the settlement-enforcement motion for lack of the asserted ancillary jurisdiction. No breach adjudication or reopening follows. State contract enforcement and a new federal action with an independent jurisdictional basis remain distinct.

#### Hess v. Port Authority Trans-Hudson Corp., 513 U.S. 30

**Docket or dockets:** No. 93-1197.
**Decided:** November 14, 1994.

##### PATH’s actual compact and financial structure does not confer the States’ immunity

**Holding:** A bistate compact entity does not acquire the States’ Eleventh Amendment immunity merely by exercising governmental power: its actual legal, financial and institutional relationship must supply good reason to conclude that the States structured it as their arm and Congress concurred, with state-treasury exposure highly probative but not categorically indispensable. PATH is a distinct, financially self-supporting enterprise responsible for its obligations; these employee suits impose no judgment obligation on either State and do not treat either State itself as a defendant, so PATH lacks that immunity.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stevens), joined by Stone-Zsela, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer: six Justices.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *Lake Country Estates, Inc. v. Tahoe Regional Planning Agency*, 440 U.S. 391 (1979), applies its entity-specific compact inquiry: exercising state power does not itself make a bistate corporation the States, and the compact’s actual structure matters.
- *Mt. Healthy City School District Board of Education v. Doyle*, 429 U.S. 274 (1977), preserves the distinction between a separate public entity and an arm of a State; a governmental function alone supplies no immunity.
- *Port Authority Trans-Hudson Corp. v. Feeney*, 495 U.S. 299 (1990), is distinguished as deciding federal-forum consent while assuming, rather than deciding, PATH’s arm status. Its consent ruling is not an earlier holding that PATH is immune.
- *Hilton v. South Carolina Public Railways Commission*, 502 U.S. 197 (1991), preserves state-court FELA coverage without constructive waiver of federal-forum immunity; no abrogation or waiver rule is enlarged here.
- *Puerto Rico Aqueduct & Sewer Authority v. Metcalf & Eddy, Inc.*, 506 U.S. 139 (1993), preserves immediate review of a qualifying immunity denial without establishing that a particular entity is an immune arm. Its appealability holding does not decide PATH’s status.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court does not declare every compact entity nonimmune, make a debt disclaimer alone dispositive, decide a materially different entity’s practical dependence, abolish state sovereign immunity or hold that FELA abrogates federal-forum immunity. Negligence, causation, damages, the Boiler Inspection Act merits, every independently applicable nonimmunity limitations defense and hypothetical consent exceptions remain undecided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Both the Hess and Walsh immunity dismissals are reversed and remanded. The Eleventh Amendment bar is removed; ordinary nonimmunity defenses and the separate actions' merits remain undecided.

#### Doe v. Taylor Independent School District

**Docket or dockets:** Fifth Circuit No. 90-8431; no separate Supreme Court docket supplied.
**Decided:** October 16, 1995.

##### Immediate review of legal immunity questions and factual sufficiency

**Holding:** An immediate qualified-immunity appeal may decide a separable legal question on the plaintiff's properly supported assumed facts, but may not decide only whether the evidence is sufficient to establish those facts. A factual-sufficiency portion must be dismissed; an appellate court cannot resolve disputed knowledge or causation by choosing whose account is true and recasting that choice as law.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Part I, unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No particular additional factual-only appeal is found to exist. The Court resolves neither credibility nor the inconsistent January/February descriptions of Caplinger's first notice.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the separable legal immunity judgment and remand; dismiss any purely factual-sufficiency portion. The distinct supervisory-right and individual-immunity holdings appear under Civil Rights, Doe, October 16, 1995, Fifth Circuit No. 90-8431.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Johnson v. Jones supplies the legal-question/factual-sufficiency boundary. Elder v. Holloway requires examination of all relevant law, not reconstruction of a more favorable factual record for the officer. Neither rule expands collateral appellate jurisdiction.

#### Pinder v. Johnson

**Docket or dockets:** Fourth Circuit No. 93-2125; no Supreme Court docket stated in the Record
**Decided:** May 21, 1996

##### What may an appellate court decide on this separable legal qualified-immunity appeal?

**Holding:** Sections 1254(1) and 1291 permit review of the separable legal immunity issue on the plaintiff-favorable account governing that issue. This jurisdiction does not permit a determination of evidentiary sufficiency, disputed intent or causation, or claims excluded from the appeal.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (O'Connor), Part I, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; unanimous controlling rationale.

**Controlling explanation:** The reviewable question is whether the assumed conduct violated a right sufficiently established when Johnson acted. The district court's characterization of its disposition as one under Rule 12(b)(6), and the appellate court's summary-judgment terminology, do not authorize an unfavorable resolution of disputed facts or enlarge the legal issue before the Court. The Court does not rely on a second-appeal premise or convert a legal immunity appeal into fact review.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court determines neither disputed causation nor ultimate constitutional liability. The substantive notice holding and its exact five-Justice coalition appear under Civil Rights, Affirmative danger and conduct-time qualified immunity.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The reviewed individual-immunity judgment is affirmed, 5-4, on the conduct-time notice ground. No excluded claim or new factual question is decided.

#### Seminole Tribe of Florida v. Florida, 517 U.S. 44

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-12.
**Decided:** March 27, 1996.

##### IGRA's binding good-faith adjudication is judicial despite later statutory steps

**Holding:** Adjudication of IGRA's federal good-faith negotiation dispute is a judicial function when its judgment conclusively determines legal relations and activates constrained statutory consequences. Later mediation and executive action, and the absence of a guaranteed compact, do not make that judgment advisory.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's Part I, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer: seven Justices. Stevens's serious doubt and Stone-Zsela's nonjoin supply no additional support.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Aetna and Nashville permit conclusive adverse declarations without an immediately executable coercive decree. Brimson supports judicial assistance to administration within its adversarial, conclusive, enforceable-order setting, without creating IGRA contempt power. Ferreira's revisable commissioner process is distinguished. Gordon's jurisdictional disposition supplies no delivered reasoned majority opinion requiring execution in every judicial case; Hayburn produced no Supreme Court merits decision, and its reported circuit communications remain historical materials. Wisconsin's partial-redress rule concerns the judicial capacity to afford meaningful relief. Morgan Stanley and Plaut: Article III permits Congress to require reinstatement of qualifying completed private securities limitations dismissals under §27A(b)'s objectively defined procedure while courts independently determine eligibility and adjudicate the claims. The holdings do not establish an unrestricted power to reopen completed merits judgments and do not determine whether IGRA's conclusive adjudication ceases to be judicial because mediation or executive action follows.

##### A connected officer may be sued prospectively under IGRA within its complete statutory limits

**Holding:** Ex parte Young permits prospective official-capacity relief against Governor Chiles for an ongoing violation of IGRA's good-faith negotiation duty, subject to IGRA's full sequence and remedial limits. The State-directed wording and detailed procedure do not exclude that route in this case; they do not authorize greater remedies through the officer.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's Parts II–III, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Ginsburg and Breyer: five Justices. No additional general clear-indication theory commands that coalition.

**Material application:** The Governor's representatives negotiated, supplying connection to the alleged violation without establishing that he alone can bind Florida to every term. Mandatory good faith differs from discretion over compact terms, and nonagreement alone does not prove bad faith.

**Limits and questions not reached:** A qualifying request by the Tribe with jurisdiction over the Indian lands triggers §2710(d)(3)(A)'s good-faith duty; suit under (d)(7)(A)(i) may begin only after 180 days. The effective-compact enforcement action under (A)(ii) and Secretary action under (A)(iii) remain separate. Tribal evidence of no compact and failure to respond or respond in good faith precedes the State's good-faith burden under (B)(ii).

An actual adverse finding triggers an order to State and Tribe to conclude a compact within the first sixty days. The court may consider public interest, safety, criminality, financial integrity and adverse economic effects on existing gaming, and shall treat direct-tax demands on the Tribe or Indian lands as evidence of bad faith. Failure to agree during that period requires last-best proposals to a court-appointed mediator, who selects the proposal best comporting with IGRA, other applicable federal law and the findings and order. Submission to both sides begins a second sixty-day period for State consent; consent produces an agreed compact. Nonconsent triggers notice to the Secretary, who shall prescribe procedures in consultation with the Tribe consistently with the selected proposal, IGRA and relevant state law. The findings/order criterion expressly appears in selection under (iv), not verbatim in the Secretary clause (vii).

State nonparticipation does not automatically select the Tribe's proposal. Failure to agree or participate during the ordered period produces statutory progression, not added contempt or an order compelling agreement; every separate obstruction sanction is not decided. Section (d)(4) creates no new taxing authority but preserves agreed regulatory-cost assessments under (d)(3)(C)(iii), separately from direct-tax demands' evidentiary effect. Absence of other taxing authority does not justify refusing negotiation.

Class III gaming still requires approved tribal legislation, a State permitting such gaming for some person, organization or entity, and an effective compact subject to applicable remedial provisions; licensing and regulation remain distinct. Under (d)(8), disapproval may rest on IGRA, other federal law not relating to jurisdiction over gaming on Indian lands, or federal trust obligations. Forty-five-day inaction means approval only insofar as consistent with IGRA, and Federal Register publication remains necessary. Section 2721 severability supplies neither missing predicates nor an alternative program.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the categorical Governor dismissal, 5–4, without finding bad faith or granting a compact, mediator, default proposal, Secretary procedures, damages, game-scope declaration or license. The separate September 22, 1993 merits judgment and stayed appeal No. 93-5256 receive their own lawful treatment and are not vacated. The asserted Secretary substitute remedy, independent administrative authority, complete state-law compacting power, Tenth Amendment defense and mediator-appointments question remain unadjudicated.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Young reaches this connected officer and ongoing federal duty without a universal rule for detailed statutes. Edelman's retrospective-compensation boundary remains; prospective costs alone do not establish retrospective relief. Pennhurst is not triggered merely because the federal obligation incorporates state gaming law. Hafer's personal-capacity damages rule does not supply this action. Puerto Rico Aqueduct supplies appealability, not immunity. New York and Voting Rights Coalition do not decide the reserved IGRA constitutional defenses; X-Citement Video preserves appropriate respondent defenses without compelling their resolution here.

##### State amenability lacks a controlling rationale, despite agreement on direct-State statutory intent

**Authority of the judgment:** Florida's immunity-dismissal direction is set aside and remanded by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, 5–4. Stone-Zsela and Stevens would sustain direct-State amenability; Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer would vacate without deciding it. O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas would affirm. No controlling State-amenability rationale exists under Marks: avoidance is not a logical subset of the affirmative constitutional grounds, and three unused conditional alternatives are not actual abrogation joins.

**Independently majority-supported proposition:** IGRA unmistakably authorizes an action naming the State through its State-party action, evidentiary burden, State-and-Tribe order and successive State-directed steps. General federal-question jurisdiction, §1362 or scattered State references alone would not suffice.

**Proposition-level authority:** Six actual positions across the separate writings: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas. The other three's conditional acceptance is not counted. This agreement concerns statutory intent alone.

**Current force:** The clear-statement proposition controls; no majority sustains or rejects Florida's constitutional amenability or requires a particular avoidance sequence.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The State-removal judgment establishes neither jurisdiction nor its absence; lawful jurisdiction is necessary before any State merits adjudication. The State claim is not terminated by immunity, and no abandonment, mootness or indefinite stay is presumed. No express or constructive waiver, reciprocal tribal/state consent or delegated United States immunity exemption establishes this suit. The United States appears only as amicus.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Union Gas is not overruled: its damages judgment, statutory holding and five-vote constitutional conclusion retain their decided force without making every plurality sentence binding. Hans remains effective; separate common-law theories do not become law. Fitzpatrick's distinct Fourteenth Amendment authority is unchanged.

#### Citizens for a Better Environment v. Steel Co.

**Docket or dockets:** Seventh Circuit No. 96-1136; review of 90 F.3d 1237.

**Decided:** 1997-06-02.

##### Constitutional jurisdiction before a disputed EPCRA merits question

**Holding:** A federal court cannot assume disputed Article III jurisdiction to decide the contested merits of this EPCRA reporting claim. Section 11046(c) describes enforcement and relief without making every statutory liability element jurisdictional; the holding preserves legitimate exceptional threshold sequences and does not prescribe an exhaustive order for all nonmerits issues.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's Part I, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy and Thomas; five direct joins, including O'Connor and Kennedy's nonexhaustive sequencing reservation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Whether EPCRA authorizes suit over reporting violations cured before filing remains undecided. Norton and Avrech retain their distinct settings involving already-controlled merits.

##### Requested relief cannot redress this completed informational deprivation

**Holding:** Assuming past informational injury and traceability, Article III requires requested relief likely to repair that injury or prevent a supported continuing or imminent personal injury. When reports were current before suit and no continuing or imminent violation is alleged, a declaration of past lateness, Treasury penalties, monitoring, future report copies and litigation expenses supply no such redress.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's Part II, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas and Breyer; six direct joins. Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg support dismissal on statutory grounds without joining this constitutional rationale.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court assumes rather than rejects informational injury. Standing is absent at commencement, rather than lost by later cessation. Deterrent penalties may redress a supported threatened injury; annual reporting duties alone do not allege one. Section 11046(f) permits appropriate prevailing-or-substantially-prevailing-party costs on a final order, not independent compensation. A properly presented replacement-information loss claim, monitoring in a live action, recoverable prefiling expenses, associational requirements and statutory temporal reach remain open. Section 11022(f) gives the fire department no transferable automatic inspection entitlement for this plaintiff.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacate and remand with directions to dismiss, unanimously as to judgment. No merits relief, standing discovery, amendment entitlement or newly inferred recurrence inquiry is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **Bell v. Hood:** Applied to distinguish a substantial federal claim from its ultimate success; a contested statutory element is not automatically jurisdictional.
- **Young v. Northern Illinois Conference:** Applied at that same distinction; its constitutional-defense holding is not expanded into permission to assume Article III.
- **Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife:** Its controlling standing elements and stage-appropriate support remain governing. Its three-Justice absent-agency application is neither enlarged nor needed.
- **Church of Scientology v. United States:** Meaningful partial relief remains enough; no effectual relief of that kind is available on these allegations.
- **United Food & Commercial Workers v. Brown Group:** Congress's representative-enforcement choice does not eliminate personal injury and likely redress.
- **Bennett v. Spear:** Supported pleading-stage causal and remedial links remain sufficient; its ongoing constraint differs from the completed reporting deprivation here.
- **Arizonans for Official English v. Arizona:** Applied for the live-controversy requirement and limited corrective authority to vacate without reaching merits.
- **Norton v. Mathews; Secretary of the Navy v. Avrech:** Their already-controlled merits and distinct jurisdictional settings remain intact; neither decides this unresolved EPCRA dispute.
- **Gwaltney of Smithfield v. Chesapeake Bay Foundation:** Its different statutory wording and enforcement scheme do not settle the merits question the Court leaves open.
- **Meghrig v. KFC Western:** Its RCRA cause-and-remedy limits remain statute-specific; it supplies no automatic EPCRA current-violation rule.

#### Idaho v. Coeur d’Alene Tribe of Idaho

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-1474.
**Decided:** June 23, 1997.

##### A disputed federal tribal title question does not create a sovereign-lands exception to prospective officer relief

**Holding:** The Eleventh Amendment does not bar a claim for prospective declaratory or injunctive relief against officials actually connected to an alleged ongoing violation of federal tribal property rights merely because adjudicating that violation requires resolving a disputed federal title question or affects important sovereign interests. The decree must address proved unlawful future official conduct and may not bind the immune State's title, award damages or retrospective compensation, execute against state property or require the State itself to convey title.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone-Zsela's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer: five Justices.

**Material application:** The pleaded land-regulating officials have a concrete connection to the alleged interference; a general law-obedience duty would not suffice. The actual request for exclusive use and exclusion of regulation is acknowledged, but the Ninth Circuit withheld a decree binding Idaho’s title and the case remains at the pleading stage.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Neither generalized state-interest balancing nor a special sovereign-lands exception bars the route. The Tribe must establish the federal right, ongoing violation and lawful remedy. No ownership, navigability, prestatehood disposition or separate United States action is adjudicated; no general Article I abrogation rule is announced.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm permission for the prospective-officer claims, 5–4; leave the State, agency and direct quiet-title or retrospective-relief dismissals undisturbed, 9–0. Further merits and tailored-remedy proceedings are permitted, without automatic injunction or property transfer.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Young supplies the connected-officer route; Tindal and Treasure Salvors distinguish officer conduct relief from sovereign title. Oneida supplies federal-question character. Edelman, Green and Pennhurst retain retrospective and state-law limits. Seminole’s actual IGRA officer holding is consistent but supplies neither a universal statutory route nor an abrogation rule, and no IGRA sequence is imported.

#### Raines v. Byrd

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-1671.

**Decided:** June 26, 1997.

##### Whether individual Members may challenge abstract institutional dilution after fully effective votes

**Holding:** Individual Members of Congress whose votes were counted and given full effect lack Article III standing to challenge the Line Item Veto Act on the asserted abstract dilution of Congress's institutional power and alteration of future votes. Express statutory permission to sue removes the applicable prudential obstacle but cannot supply the missing concrete personal injury.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas and Ginsburg; six direct rationale votes. Souter supplies the seventh judgment vote on a separate ground.

**Material application:** The Members lost votes that were counted and identify no personal injury from a cancellation; neither House authorized them to represent Congress.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No categorical bar to legislator or official injury is established. Personal exclusion, lost salary, nullified decisive votes, an authorized institutional claim satisfying Article III and concrete cancellation injury remain distinct. Legislative remedies create no exhaustion requirement; no independent traceability holding is added.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Lujan supplies concrete personal injury, without enlargement of its noncontrolling redressability analysis. Akins's concrete shared informational injury is distinguished. Powell preserves personal exclusion claims; Coleman preserves complete nullification of decisive votes. Bender and Allen support the personal-stake and unauthorized-representation boundaries.

##### What disposition follows from the Members' lack of standing

**Holding:** Because these plaintiffs lack Article III standing, the District Court's constitutional declaration must be vacated and the complaint dismissed for lack of jurisdiction. The Court does not decide the Act's constitutionality, nondelegation, Presentment or the independent ripeness question.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas and Ginsburg; six direct rationale votes. Souter supplies the seventh judgment vote on a separate ground.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacate the District Court's constitutional declaration and remand for jurisdictional dismissal, 7–2. Vacatur neither sustains the Act nor orders a merits remand.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Steel Co. requires resolution of disputed Article III power before disputed merits in this setting; statutory expedition and direct review do not supply constitutional injury.

#### Collins v. City of Harker Heights, 503 U.S. 115 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-1279.
**Decided:** February 26, 1992.

##### Whether §1983 adds a freestanding governmental-power-abuse element for municipal employees

**Holding:** A municipal employee who establishes deprivation of a federal right under color of state law and municipal responsibility need not also prove an independent, freestanding abuse-of-governmental-power element. The existence of the constitutional wrong and its attribution to municipal policy or custom remain separate required inquiries.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part A of Stevens’s opinion, joined by the other eight Justices; unanimous statutory holding.

**Material application:** The Fifth Circuit’s additional statutory requirement cannot sustain dismissal merely because the city acted as an employer. Its rejection does not establish the underlying federal right or final municipal attribution; attribution is assumed only to resolve the constitutional question.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Employment alone does not establish municipal liability, and deficient training does not create a substantive constitutional right.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The extra statutory element is rejected, but dismissal of the federal claim is affirmed on the separate constitutional grounds below.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Monell v. Department of Social Services*, 436 U.S. 658, supplies municipal policy and causation requirements distinct from employment alone. *Canton v. Harris*, 489 U.S. 378, is clarified as an attribution doctrine, not a source of the underlying substantive right. *Oklahoma City v. Tuttle*, 471 U.S. 808, preserves the distinction between constitutional wrong and municipal responsibility.

##### Whether voluntary municipal employment or these pleaded safety omissions establish a substantive-due-process deprivation

**Holding:** The substantive Due Process Clause does not impose an independent general obligation on a municipality to provide its voluntary employees a workplace free from unreasonable risks of harm. Accepting public employment does not itself create the custody or deprivation of liberty that supports affirmative constitutional duties of care.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part B of Stevens’s opinion, joined by the other eight Justices; unanimous duty limitation.

**Material application:** The complaint alleges known hazards and deliberate failures to train, warn and equip sewer workers. Those allegations are accepted at dismissal; they do not make voluntary employment a custodial restraint.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The holding does not excuse violation of other applicable federal rights. Actual compulsion, deliberately inflicted bodily injury, a different properly pleaded affirmative-danger claim and procedural due process are not decided. State safety and tort remedies are not adjudicated, and knowing allegations are not reduced to negligence.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm dismissal of the federal substantive-due-process claim. No federal compensation or supervision order is entered; independently available state-law remedies retain their own merits and procedural requirements.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *DeShaney v. Winnebago County Department of Social Services*, 489 U.S. 189, supplies the absence of a general protective duty while leaving distinct custody and affirmative-deprivation questions. *Youngberg v. Romeo*, 457 U.S. 307, is distinguished because voluntary employment is not involuntary custody. *Daniels v. Williams*, 474 U.S. 327, preserves the distinction between constitutional due process and ordinary tort law without redescribing these allegations as negligence. *Bishop v. Wood*, 426 U.S. 341, rejects a general constitutional guarantee against employment mistakes.

**Independent alternative holding:** The pleaded municipal failures to train, warn and equip sewer workers against known hazards do not constitute arbitrary or conscience-shocking deprivation under substantive due process in this voluntary-employment setting. Even assuming the Texas safety statute creates the asserted duties and a protected liberty interest, breach alone does not establish that distinct substantive constitutional wrong.

**Proposition-level authority for alternative holding:** Part C of Stevens’s opinion, joined by White, Blackmun, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas; eight Justices control this alternative rejection. Stone’s agreement in the judgment does not join Part C.

**Limits, remedy, transition, or precedent treatment:** No deliberate bodily injury or direction knowingly sending Collins into a dangerous sewer is alleged. The public-program rationale recognizes competing social, political and economic choices as a legal presumption; it is not a finding that the city acted responsibly or that its budget justified these omissions. The alternative independently supports affirmance without deciding state remedies or a different affirmative-injury claim. *Rochin v. California*, 342 U.S. 165, is distinguished from these protective omissions. *Bishop*, 426 U.S. 341, and *Daniels*, 474 U.S. 327, preserve the distinction between state employment or tort duties and substantive constitutional arbitrariness. *Harrah Independent School District v. Martin*, 440 U.S. 194, distinguishes an assumed state-created interest from arbitrary deprivation.

#### Albright v. Oliver, 510 U.S. 266

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-833.

**Decided:** 1994-01-24.

##### Presented substantive-due-process claim

**Authority of the judgment:** Dismissal of Albright's presented substantive-due-process claim is affirmed, 7–2: Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg support the judgment; Blackmun and Stevens dissent. No controlling rationale exists under Marks. The four-Justice specific-source plurality, Kennedy and Thomas's distinct prosecution/due-process rationale and Souter's additional-injury rationale do not form a demonstrated logical subset.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The judgment binds the parties but establishes no general Fourth Amendment displacement rule, state-remedy bar or synthesized substantive-due-process test. It does not adopt the Seventh Circuit's incarceration, employment-loss or palpable-consequence requirement. The abandoned Fourth Amendment theory, accrual, immunity, municipal attribution and a general fabrication claim are undecided. Earlier authority retains its existing force; uses in the separate rationales supply no new majority construction.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Dismissal of the presented federal claim stands. The state malicious-prosecution count's dismissal without prejudice is undisturbed. No Fourth Amendment claim is reinstated, and no damages or merits remand is ordered.

#### Farmer v. Brennan, 511 U.S. 825

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-7247.

**Decided:** 1994-06-06.

##### Actual awareness and unreasonable disregard of substantial danger

**Holding:** A prison official violates the Eighth Amendment by disregarding a substantial risk of serious harm only if the official knows facts indicating that risk, actually appreciates the risk, and fails to respond reasonably.
