#### Procedure After Action

The Ninth Circuit's compulsory transfer of the whole preregistry collection is reversed. Further district-court proceedings must respect the preserved independent claim and the actual scope of existing rulings and agreements. No refund claim is revived for Westmoreland, no registry distribution is disturbed, and no additional amount, amendment or evidentiary proceeding is directed.

#### Source Notes

The reported record at 523 U.S. 696 and the underlying judgments supply the tax periods, registry chronology, June 1983 intervention and limited 1988 ruling. The current demand is restitution with interest; no proved compensatory measure or ruling allowing a proposed amendment is established. Legal tax coverage is distinct from the commercial effects of Interior's actual refusals.



<!-- frozen-source: OT_1997CHUNK9_NEUTRAL_NEW_JERSEY.md -->

# Neutral Modeling Packet — New Jersey v. New York

**Event:** No. 120, Original; exceptions to the Special Master's Final Report and Supplement; argued January 12, 1998; planned action May 26, 1998. **Research cutoff:** May 26, 1998. **Preparation baseline:** the frozen chunk-9 entering Neutral Projection through May 21, 1998, and the chunk-9 entering-law slice. Independent same-day decisions supply no additional law.

**Scope and readiness:** This packet completes the unfinished New Jersey preparation with the recovered antecedent record. It contains no proposed vote, same-matter Supreme Court merits result, comparator or private Stone position. The original report, supplemental report, New York exceptions and official compact have been read in full. New Jersey's exceptions are incorporated below from a separate clean full-source review, with the central pier challenge independently checked. The report and briefs provide sufficient supported evidence to model the live alternatives without inventing testimony or treating inaccessible original exhibits as litigant default. The Court must still decide the disputed inferences; the Master's conclusions are recommendations, not independent proof of their premises. Merits action on the exceptions and a later implementing decree remain separate stages.

## 1. Channel, questions, participation and requested relief

This is an Article III original controversy between States under 28 U.S.C. §1251(a). There is no lower appellate judgment to affirm or reverse. New Jersey invoked original jurisdiction in 1993. A twenty-three-day trial before the Master ran from July 10 through August 15, 1996, producing more than 4,000 transcript pages and almost 2,000 documents. Both States filed exceptions to the Master's March 31, 1997 report and May 30 supplement. The current Court consists of Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; no event-specific recusal or nonparticipation is supplied. With nine participants, five support the judgment; no ordinary appellate equal-division affirmance is available in this original action.

The separable questions are:

1. Whether Articles First–Third of the 1834 Compact place the artificial additions to Ellis Island within New Jersey's sovereignty, or Article Second's reservation of New York's island jurisdiction embraces its subsequent enlargement.
2. If the Compact otherwise favors New Jersey, whether New York acquired the fill by prescription and New Jersey's acquiescence.
3. Whether laches can independently bar this boundary claim and, if available, whether New York proved the required lack of diligence and prejudice.
4. Whether New York's retained original-island territory extends to the 1834 mean high-water or mean low-water line.
5. Whether the evidence justifies including the historic pier's footprint as land in 1834, and whether the Master's additional 0.2-acre allowance is supported.
6. Whether the Court may substitute the Master's practical 5.1-acre configuration for the established historical boundary, and what survey/decree work follows the resolution of these exceptions.

New Jersey seeks a declaration that the original island to its former mean high-water line belongs to New York and the artificial additions belong to New Jersey, and an injunction against New York enforcing laws or asserting sovereignty on the New Jersey portions. Its proposed 1857-survey contour, excluding the pier, gives New York approximately 2.74 acres. New York seeks a declaration of sovereignty over the entire island. New Jersey accepts New York's sovereignty over the original island; federal title and jurisdiction remain distinct. No party's acceptance of survey accuracy, compliance with the Master's survey directions, or alternative preference within his plan is consent to the opposing merits position.

## 2. Source map and provenance limits

| Source | Exact location and work performed |
|---|---|
| Congress's consent and complete Compact, June 28, 1834, 4 Stat. 708–711 | [Official four-page statute](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/STATUTE-4/pdf/STATUTE-4-Pg708-2.pdf); [complete local text](../research/chunk9_neutral_sources/1834_compact_statute_pages.txt). Read in full. The governing text is Chapter CXXVI; neighboring statutes and editorial annotations are not Compact provisions. |
| Paul R. Verkuil, Final Report, March 31, 1997 | [Official scan](https://www.supremecourt.gov/specmastrpt/Orig120_033197.pdf); [IA item](https://archive.org/details/micro_IA40385014_0101); [full recovered OCR](../research/chunk9_neutral_sources/new_jersey_master_ia_ocr.txt). Full report through printed pp. 169–170 proposed decree read; page citations below are printed report pages. This is the report, not the complete underlying trial transcript or all exhibits. Map-image detail not expressed in the OCR is not independently measured. |
| Supplement, May 30, 1997 | [Official OCR-rich copy](https://www.supremecourt.gov/pdfs/recordsandbriefs/8000001256/8000001256_001.pdf); [full local text](../research/chunk9_neutral_sources/new_jersey_master_supplement_digital.txt). Full text, printed pp. 1–15, read, including the proposed geographic description. It supplies a proposed Designated Survey, not an entered Supreme Court decree. |
| New York's Exceptions, July 30, 1997 | [Official brief](https://www.supremecourt.gov/pdfs/recordsandbriefs/1000259658/1000259658_001.pdf); [full local text](../research/chunk9_neutral_sources/new_york_exceptions.txt). Full brief and Compact appendix read. NY/NJ exhibit numbers and T transcript citations below are the brief's citations; cited originals are not represented as separately recovered. |
| New Jersey's Exceptions, July 31, 1997 | [Official brief](https://www.supremecourt.gov/pdfs/recordsandbriefs/1000259658/1000259658_002.pdf); [full local text](../research/chunk9_neutral_sources/new_jersey_exceptions.txt); [completed clean full-source digest](OT_1997CHUNK9_NJ_EXCEPTIONS_DIGEST.md). Separate clean review read printed pp. 1–49 and all appendices 1a–26a; key pier passages independently read. Map graphics are not independently measured. |
| Governing public law | [Entering-law slice](../entering-law/OT_1997CHUNK9.md); [frozen entering Neutral Projection](OT_1997CHUNK9_ENTERING_NEUTRAL_PROJECTION.md). The authorities, dates and limits below identify the relevant sections; the slice is a reading copy, not a new source of law. |
| Georgia v. South Carolina, 497 U.S. 376 (1990) | [Full official opinion](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-497/pdf/USREPORTS-497-376.pdf); [local full text](../research/chunk9_neutral_may26/georgia_south_carolina.txt). All writings and footnotes read, with component joins verified. |
| Illinois v. Kentucky, 500 U.S. 380 (May 28, 1991) | [Full official opinion](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-500/pdf/USREPORTS-500-380.pdf). All eleven pages read. Surviving pre-divergence authority, not a later historical comparator. |

The IA collection search and recovery were performed by control before this clean review. The earlier mechanically extracted compact/report/exception summary is used only as a location aid; substantive factual treatment here rests on the recovered reports, briefs and official statute. Historical Supreme Court procedural recitations embedded in briefs do not themselves create additional in-world Court actions. No reconstructed testimony, concession, notice event, missing filing, coordinate or merits premise is adopted. Published record recitations suffice under Engine §14; a precise implementing line still requires the Court's later decree.

## 3. Compact text and uncontested physical setting

The agreement was executed September 16, 1833 and received congressional consent June 28, 1834. Its first three articles provide:

> **Article First.** The boundary line between the two states of New York and New Jersey, from a point in the middle of Hudson river, opposite the point on the west shore thereof, in the forty-first degree of north latitude, as heretofore ascertained and marked, to the main sea, shall be the middle of the said river, of the Bay of New York, of the waters between Staten Island and New Jersey, and of Raritan Bay, to the main sea; except as hereinafter otherwise particularly mentioned.
>
> **Article Second.** The state of New York shall retain its present jurisdiction of and over Bedlow's and Ellis's islands; and shall also retain exclusive jurisdiction of and over the other islands lying in the waters above mentioned and now under the jurisdiction of that state.
>
> **Article Third.** The state of New York shall have and enjoy exclusive jurisdiction of and over all the waters of the bay of New York; and of and over all the waters of Hudson river lying west of Manhattan Island and to the south of the mouth of Spuytenduyvel creek; and of and over the lands covered by the said waters to the low water-mark on the westerly or New Jersey side thereof; subject to the following rights of property and of jurisdiction of the state of New Jersey, that is to say:
>
> 1. The state of New Jersey shall have the exclusive right of property in and to the land under water lying west of the middle of the bay of New York, and west of the middle of that part of the Hudson river which lies between Manhattan island and New Jersey.
> 2. The state of New Jersey shall have the exclusive jurisdiction of and over the wharves, docks, and improvements, made and to be made on the shore of the said state; and of and over all vessels aground on said shore, or fastened to any such wharf or dock; except that the said vessels shall be subject to the quarantine or health laws, and laws in relation to passengers, of the state of New York, which now exist or which may hereafter be passed.
> 3. The state of New Jersey shall have the exclusive right of regulating the fisheries on the westerly side of the middle of the said waters, Provided, That the navigation be not obstructed or hindered.

Article Fourth gives New York specified quarantine and passenger-law jurisdiction over the Kill Van Kull to Shooter's Island and, for vessels bound to New York ports, the sound to Woodbridge Creek. Article Fifth gives New Jersey jurisdiction over the specified southern sound and western Raritan Bay, subject to New York's underwater property, Staten Island wharves/improvements and aground/fastened-vessel jurisdiction, with New Jersey's quarantine/health/passenger-law exception, and New York fisheries rights conditioned on unobstructed navigation. Articles Sixth and Seventh separately permit the two States' identified criminal and civil process on the other's jurisdictional waters, except for persons or property on vessels aground or fastened to the other shore or wharf, or already under that State's arrest or seizure. These are particular jurisdictional allocations, not interchangeable exceptions. Article Eighth conditions binding effect on both legislatures' confirmation and Congress's approval. Congressional consent includes: "nothing therein contained shall be construed to impair or in any manner affect, any right of jurisdiction of the United States in and over the islands or waters which form the subject of the said agreement."

Ellis Island lies west of the Article First midwater line. Its original fast land was approximately three acres. The United States held the original island under New York's 1808 conveyance, following New York's 1800 jurisdictional cession with a reservation for service of process. Beginning about 1890, federal works enlarged the island by approximately 24.5 acres, principally for immigration facilities. The additions were artificial fill, a formation history the report treats as undisputed; New York's central answer is that the Compact displaced the ordinary boundary consequence, not that the substantial additions were natural gradual accretion. The present island was approximately 27.5 acres; the 1995 Marchuk survey measured 27.40 acres. High-water fast-land acreage is not the same quantity as acreage measured to mean low water. (Report 3–9, 90–99, 159–162.)

The 1904 New Jersey deed transferred underwater property to the United States, expressly preserving all United States rights and privileges as if no grant had been accepted (NJ Exceptions, Appendix D,9a–12a). The reported trial stipulation identified .5672 acres outside the grant along the northeast seawall, with additional narrowly described western seawall encroachment. The Master associated the approximately .57 acres with retained New Jersey ownership. This case need not transform that finding into a comprehensive property-title decree. Ownership, federal enclave authority, residual state jurisdiction and interstate sovereignty must be treated separately.

## 4. Law entering each issue

**Original forum and review.** The entering Holdings entry *Mississippi v. Louisiana*, December 14, 1992, and Standards and Tests **Exclusive interstate jurisdiction and private boundary-related claims**, **Present operation effective: 1992-12-14**, reserve an actual interstate controversy to this Court under §1251(a). An otherwise lawful private-title adjudication can locate a boundary incidentally without binding the States on sovereignty. Accordingly, *Collins v. Promark Products*, 956 F.2d 383 (2d Cir. 1992), supplies a lower-court interpretation and context for this litigation, not a binding sovereign boundary. *Louisiana v. Mississippi*, October 31 and December 4, 1995, requires independent original-action review, appropriate evidentiary weight for the Master's technical work, and an authenticated, internally consistent decree. Rule 52 appellate deference does not bind this Court to the Master. That case preserved an established island boundary despite a navigation-channel shift, but did not settle new islands, artificial enlargement of this enclave, prescription, or a universal island definition.

**Compact construction.** *Central Railroad Co. of New Jersey v. Jersey City*, 209 U.S. 473, 478–479 (1908), construes Article First as a sovereignty boundary and Article Third's New York authority as serving commerce and navigation rather than restoring all sovereignty to New York. The Court's construction remains precedent; the States' absence as parties to that tax case means it is not an earlier binding adjudication of this island boundary. The disputed reach of Article Second remains to be resolved. *Texas v. New Mexico*, 462 U.S. 554, 564 (1983), forbids relief inconsistent with the express terms of a constitutionally valid congressionally approved compact. *Cuyler v. Adams*, 449 U.S. 433 (1981), supplies its federal-law character; *Oklahoma v. New Mexico*, 501 U.S. 221, 234 n. 5 (1991), permits relevant negotiation history where ambiguity warrants it. All precede divergence.

The current *Kansas v. Colorado*, May 15, 1995, unanimously enforces the actual compact obligation and distinguishes its substance from remedial flexibility; neither state practice nor preferred policy creates a different bargain. Its liability determination does not choose a general proof quantum for every compact-enforcement factual dispute. Current *Hess*, November 14, 1994, and Standards and Tests **State immunity of bistate compact entities**, **Present operation effective: 1994-11-14**, examine an entity's actual compact, legal, financial and institutional relationship. They do not decide territorial boundaries or establish that a compact may be rewritten for convenience. *Hess*'s controlling author is Stevens, joined by Stone-Zsela, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; do not substitute the historical writing. Current *Alaska v. Native Village of Venetie*, February 25, 1998, unanimously distinguishes political status, title and statutory territorial predicates; its §1151(b) requirements do not become a test for Ellis Island.

**Formation and prescription.** *Georgia v. South Carolina*, 497 U.S. 376, Part IV, reads its own island reservation with the treaty's enduring-boundary terms and rejects a newly shifting boundary for later-emerging islands; it does not construe this different Compact. Part VII distinguishes gradual accretion/erosion from avulsion and treats the particular mixture of federal dredging, sedimentation and filling as predominantly avulsive on a close record. Federal activity alone is not a universal classification. Part III establishes prescription through sufficiently long possession and dominion with acquiescence, considering concrete sovereign acts and notice. A later protest does not undo sovereignty already acquired, but whether acquisition occurred first must be proved.

*Illinois v. Kentucky*, 500 U.S. 380, 384–389 (1991), places on the claiming State the preponderance burden for long, continuous possession and assertion of sovereignty over the disputed territory, followed by proof of the other's long acquiescence. Acts within an undisputed area do not establish control over a disputed adjacent sliver. Officers' statements as well as conduct matter. Its boundary discussion rejects an independent laches defense, explaining that prescription and acquiescence implement the relevant repose policy. It also separates entitlement from later precise geographic determination. Souter wrote for a unanimous Court; Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia and Kennedy participated and joined. Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer did not participate.

**Laches.** Current *Kansas v. Colorado*, May 15, 1995, unanimously assumes possible laches availability in compact enforcement and rejects it for failure to establish inexcusable lack of diligence; categorical availability remains open and no independent prejudice holding follows from that case. Thus *Illinois*'s boundary rule cannot be recast as an absolute exemption for every compact dispute, and *Kansas* cannot be recast as having authorized laches here. If the Court reaches the proposed independent defense, both lack of diligence and prejudice require their own support.

## 5. Compact-construction alternatives and their record support

**Original island plus New Jersey fill.** Article First, *Central Railroad*, New Jersey's underwater sovereignty and the retention of the existing island jurisdiction can be read to leave artificial additions on New Jersey's side. The ordinary avulsion principle then prevents enlargement of New York sovereignty by federal filling. The Master adopted this route, while treating "present" principally as recognizing preexisting federal jurisdiction rather than relying on that adjective alone as a precise geographic limitation. He found the Compact silent about subsequent landfill. Report 53–99.

New Jersey separately maintains that Article Second's present-tense retention itself limits the reservation geographically to the 1834 island. Its brief expressly distinguishes its interpretation from the Master's federal-enclave explanation (NJ Exceptions 9–11, especially10 n. 5; Report 60–63). A judgment for NJ fill could adopt either interpretation, but a common result does not establish a common compact rationale.

The strongest objection is New York's Article Second argument: a named island may be reserved as an enduring geographic entity without a fixed acreage; "present" can describe the quality of retained jurisdiction after the federal cession. The Compact expressly contemplates improvements elsewhere and contains no metes-and-bounds restriction here. The fact that the fill was artificial does not answer whether the Compact displaced the default rule.

**Continuing island reservation.** New York argues that retaining island jurisdiction without a size limitation encompasses foreseeable enlargement and serves the Compact's commerce, health and navigation settlement. It adduces the Dongan and Montgomerie charters' filling powers, historic Manhattan reclamation and New Jersey waterfront development; *City of Hoboken v. Pennsylvania Railroad*, 124 U.S. 656 (1888), and *Bell v. Gough*, 23 N.J.L. 624 (1852), document the latter context. New York disputes the Master's finding that evidence of accepted filling at the time was too ambiguous, and argues that contractual good faith and practical limits answer hypothetical unlimited expansion. NY Exceptions 11–21; Report 91–99.

The strongest objection is that foreseeable private or municipal filling does not establish an agreement to transfer the other State's sovereign territory. Article Third's jurisdictional allocation remains distinct from territory, and a court cannot infer that New York's conceded original enclave expands whenever federal land is attached. The reported 1827 proposals, 1889 interstate map and 1921 Port Authority materials support different inferences but contain no express award of later Ellis fill. The 1921 agreement preserves the earlier boundary/jurisdiction except as specifically modified; its silence about Ellis Island is not an express settlement in either State's favor.

**One island or several.** The Master found three physically distinct islands for a period, relying on the prelitigation NPS history, gangways on pilings and a 1901 photograph, including Unrau's references to a new hospital island and Squires's concession that a pile breakwater is not fill. Report 94–97. New York answers that submerged cribbing connected Island No. 2 to the original tract and the photograph was taken at high tide; Island No. 3 was always connected by a gangway, and contemporary descriptions treated both as additions to Ellis Island. NY Exceptions 18–20, T2963–2985, T3065, T3110, NY952. Physical connection by a bridge is distinct from continuous dry land. New York alternatively invokes *Michigan v. Wisconsin*, 270 U.S. 295, 313–314 (1926), on possession of part of a described tract under color of title. That analogy requires deciding whether this Compact supplies color of title to the enlarged tract; naming a later formation Ellis Island cannot itself decide that question. Neither the number-of-islands dispute nor ordinary wharfing rights necessarily decides the whole case.

## 6. Prescription, notice and acquiescence: evidence for both paths

The relevant claim concerns **the artificial additions**, not New York's conceded original island. The Master concentrated on 1890–1955; New York seeks an acquisition complete by 1954, before the later public controversy. Federal ownership reduces the opportunities for ordinary local government but, on the Master's approach, is not a categorical bar to prescription: *Arkansas v. Tennessee*, 310 U.S. 563, 568–571 (1940), and *New Jersey v. Delaware*, 291 U.S. 361, 377 (1934), supply counterexamples. New Jersey presses the further argument that New York's particular cession left it too little governmental authority to prescribe (NJ Exceptions 14 n. 8, citing *United States v. Unzeuta*, 281 U.S.138,142 (1930)); the Master's narrower treatment does not resolve that exception by assertion. Conversely, federal adoption of a standard is not automatically an act by the State or proof of exclusive sovereign control. The following are competing record accounts, not additional findings by this packet.

| Subject | New York's strongest supported evidence/argument | Master's treatment and competing evidence |
|---|---|---|
| Elections and residents | Statutes and district arrangements included Ellis Island; electoral records for 1917, 1918, 1919, 1925, 1926, 1930, 1936, 1939, 1945 and 1953 identify hospital, staff-house and Islands 2/3 residents. NY argues all staff residences were on fill, with none on the original island. NY Exceptions 23, NY52–58, 953–956, T2066, 2071. This is specifically fill-directed evidence, not merely an island label. | The report identifies voting records but notes district maps retaining the original almond-shaped outline and warns that whole-island administration may rest on undisputed original-island jurisdiction. Report 107,111–118, especially112 n. 43. NJ Exceptions 23–24 distinguishes registration from proof anyone voted, describes surviving registers as covering only eight years, and identifies no registered Ellis resident after 1953 (D957–965). Its fn.15 notes obsolete island outlines (D932 p. 9). These objections do not erase specific residence records; significance, continuity and notice remain contested. |
| Vital records and marriages | NY's brief identifies 23 birth certificates, including 1904/1908 hospital locations, and 22 deaths, nearly all at Marine Hospital No. 43 on fill. It cites NY41,44 and NY2–22, rather than relying only on unspecified Ellis labels. Six marriage certificates and immigration correspondence applying NY marriage law accompany Martocci's account of numerous island weddings. NY Exceptions 24–25. | Report 114–117 questions geographic attribution and sparse coverage; most death records are from 1924, some births precede the major fill, and marriage residences do not prove ceremony locations. Original-island marriage activity cannot alone establish sovereignty over fill. NY's hospital-specific challenge to the report remains live. |
| Census, ordinary federal usage and public understanding | Federal/state census entries, federal correspondence, congressional appropriations, President Taft's 1910 message and the 1923 INS statement associate Ellis Island with NY. Staff letters, addresses, naturalization papers and a former child resident's family account support perceived residence. NY Exceptions 25,27–29, NY59–69,912,971–972. | Report 115–122,129–130 finds whole-island labels and immigrant impressions equivocal. NJ Exceptions 24 cites INS historian Marian Smith that the New York immigration district included northern New Jersey in 1891–1956 and located administrative/post-office functions on the original island (T3942–3950). These are the reported testimony and NJ's inference; a postal address does not decide state lines. Immigrant movement also reached NJ railroad terminals. No numerical claim about what all immigrants believed is established. Federal descriptions conflict, rather than uniformly settling territory. |
| Police and fire | NY points to police sent after the 1897 fire, investigation of a 1934 fatal construction accident on fill, and firefighting at the 1916 seawall fire. NY Exceptions 25–26, NY293–297,933. | The Master notes limited incidents, some NJ policing, federal day-to-day control, and Mayor Wagner's 1962 acknowledgment that the City had supplied no routine services because the island was federal. Report 114,137–138. Harbor-wide fireboat services also operated across the Hudson under claimed water jurisdiction; assistance is not inevitably an adverse sovereignty claim to fill. |
| Labor, codes and compensation | Contracts and correspondence used NYC construction standards and prevailing wages; NY's brief specifically cites work on fill in 1905,1932,1934,1945 and NY workers' compensation treatment. NY Exceptions 26–27, NY608,638,794–795 and related records. | The Master distinguishes federal selection of a convenient standard from obligatory state jurisdiction. Report 113–117. The statutory setting of Davis–Bacon locality wages and federal authorization of state compensation laws may strengthen NY's inference in particular records; not every wage benchmark has the same legal significance. |
| NJ's 1904 deed | NY characterizes the Riparian Board as an owner/seller of underwater lands, not an administrator of island sovereignty. NY Exceptions 30–31, NJ3–7,332,351. Moody's letter seeks such title as NJ may have, invokes federal commerce authority/free use and explains obtaining a grant through comity and avoidance of dispute; it is more qualified than an absolute concession of every NJ claim. NJ Exceptions Appendix C,6a–8a. | Report 123–126 treats NJ's demand for the deed and its NJ recordation as assertion of retained sovereign rights once the Compact is construed to supply them. NJ Exceptions 15 emphasizes the federal decision to obtain its deed after NY's purported1880 transfer, and the Hudson County description/recordation (T886,953–954,4039–4040). Moody distinguished original-island NY authority from NJ underwater ownership; the letter is not itself a definitive sovereignty adjudication. Its force depends partly on Compact construction. |
| Harbor Line maps and 1921 silence | NY calls the 1890 "Ellis Island, New Jersey" title erroneous even under NJ's original-island concession, with later repetitions from an engraving. It disputes personal-signature inferences, especially a 1911 reproduction of Root's 1901 signature. It argues the 1921 documents' silence is consistent with accepted NY jurisdiction. NY Exceptions 31–34. | Report 118–129 treats repeated federal maps during changing fill configurations as evidence against uniform public recognition and regards 1921 silence mainly as recognition of federal control, declining to infer a definitive sovereign settlement. No assumption that Root personally signed each reproduction or consciously decided the boundary is necessary or established here. |
| NJ permits and tax rolls | NY notes the 1933 waterfront-permit application itself called the location NY; the 1937 water-main project crossed NJ submerged land. It argues county tax rolls designated both entire Ellis and Bedloe's, were exempt, and were unknown to NJ state officials until 1992. NY Exceptions 34–35, NJ10,470–471, NY471–492,751–752. | Report 123,130–135 treats the permits, county rolls and utility taxation as evidence of NJ activity; federal exemption explains why property tax was not collected. Whether a permit concerns submerged property, waterfront authority or sovereignty over the dry fill must be decided from its object, not assumed from the State issuing it. |
| 1934–35 jobs controversy | NY emphasizes Congresswoman Norton's and local unions' efforts to secure jobs, lack of direct NY-state involvement, and Procurement's later return to NY jurisdiction after considering Article Second. It disputes attributing union or federal conduct to NJ as a State. NY Exceptions 35–38, NJ12–57. | Report 132–135 treats Norton/Mayor Hague's geographic claim and the public intergovernmental dispute as nonacquiescence. Solicitor Wyzanski's comparison of Ellis to Hawaii/Philippines was legally overbroad, not a finding that Ellis was outside all States. The contractor resisted employment sharing; Procurement's April 8, 1935 NY conclusion must be considered, not omitted. |
| 1947–49 wages | NY identifies NJ scales applied by federal Labor officials for two years, then an express 1949 revision returning fill projects to NY rates; it cites a cost-saving motive and no participation/notice by either State. NY Exceptions 38–39, NJ61,68–91,441. | Report 135–136 treats the interim NJ rates, particularly the fill power-plant project, as contrary federal recognition and support for NJ nonacquiescence. The 1949 reversal prevents describing this as permanent NJ control. Its significance as notice or State protest requires an inference, not a fictional communication. |
| 1955 onward | NY argues sovereignty had already vested and later NJ protest cannot undo it. Later NY income/sales taxes, unemployment benefits, incinerator permits and compensation practice support continued activity. NY Exceptions 22,45 n. 12. | Report 136–145 describes a public dispute over disposal, legislative hearings, NJ officials' claims, the 1963 GSA Pike analysis, joint NPS planning and the 1986 Cuomo–Kean memorandum. The memorandum never became law in NY. These establish controversy, not a new compact; they bear differently on a claim already perfected and one still accruing. |

NJ Exceptions 21–24 largely adopts the Master's assessment of New York's vital records without individually answering every fill-located hospital certificate. It asserts that actual ARA sales/corporation tax receipts begin only in 1990; distribution of NY nonresident tax forms does not itself prove payment (p. 22 n. 14). Its police argument distinguishes surrounding-water patrol from island enforcement and cites contrary NJ policing (T2595–2614,3636–3637,3950–3961). These are specific objections to weight and geographic attribution, not concessions by NY that its identified events did not occur. NJ itself acknowledges the June 16, 1949 Labor reversal to NY, without a documented explanation (p. 20 n. 12); NY's cost-saving explanation is its competing inference.

**Path sustaining prescription:** Determine that the fill-specific electoral registration, vital records, compensation and other residual state activity constituted sufficiently long, continuous and adverse sovereign administration, known or sufficiently open to charge New Jersey with notice; find the early contrary episodes legally insufficient or too late to interrupt the requisite acquiescence. *Georgia*, *Michigan* and *Arkansas* support evaluating sovereign conduct in context without requiring ownership or every normal municipal service. The strongest objection is that the claimed acts were intermittent, federally chosen or referable to the original enclave, while conflicting official records and NJ conduct negate the necessary acquiescence. A prescriptive period cannot be assembled merely by counting unrelated acts.

**Path rejecting prescription:** Find that NY has not proved the requisite possession/dominion over the specific fill and NJ's long acquiescence, considering federal control, mixed descriptions, original-island ambiguity, the deed, permits and contrary assertions. *Illinois* supplies the need to connect activity to the disputed area; *Georgia* supplies the notice inquiry and makes silence meaningful in context. The strongest objection is that this approach may discount documented fill-resident elections and hospital acts, impose more governmental activity than a federal enclave permits, or treat unknown federal/union conduct as an NJ sovereign protest. It must answer those objections specifically. Neither direct formal notice from NJ nor filing an original lawsuit is automatically the sole means of nonacquiescence. No fixed universal number of years is supplied.

## 7. Laches as a distinct component

The Master initially declined to add the pleaded defense but admitted the prejudice evidence and preserved the issue for the Court. His final analysis cites the general interstate-boundary rule, assumes possible availability for this compact setting, and finds no established prejudice; he also reasons that prescription addresses the controversy. Report 27–28,103–105. Do not reduce that treatment to a finding that NY introduced no evidence or a categorical application of the current *Kansas* holding.

New York's distinct theory is unreasonable litigation delay causing loss of evidence even if NJ maintained verbal disagreement, whereas prescription requires acquiescence in actual dominion. It argues coequal States have competing public interests and invokes *Rhode Island v. Massachusetts*, 40 U.S. (15 Pet.) 233,272–274 (1841), *Guaranty Trust*, 304 U.S.126 (1938), and *Patterson v. Hewitt*, 195 U.S.309 (1904). NY Exceptions 40–45. These arguments require confronting *Illinois* and the limited current *Kansas* reservation; they do not automatically displace either.

For prejudice, NY cites records abandoned after 1954, loss of utilities/guards, vandalism, leaking roofs, historical papers still scattered in 1978, expired tax-retention periods, deaths of adult immigration-era witnesses, and inability to question officials whose acts NJ relies upon. It argues those circumstances explain sparse surviving records and impair both affirmative proof and rebuttal. NY Exceptions 46–50, T1957–1959,2478, NY74, NJ162. The Master finds the inference that lost material would establish additional sovereign conduct or defeat contrary acts speculative. Report 104–105. No identified missing document's contents or deceased witness's testimony may be invented; the loss circumstances are not proof of those contents. Available paths include applying *Illinois*'s boundary treatment, assuming possible laches but finding an element unproved, or recognizing an independently justified compact defense and deciding both elements on the actual evidence. A grant of laches relief must specify its consequence; it cannot silently become proof of each prescriptive element.

## 8. Historical shore, pier and lawful implementation

**High versus low water.** NJ relies on Article Third's underwater-land allocation, its sovereign tideland ownership to mean high water, Article Second's retention of the 1834 island, the approximately 2.97-acre 1808 deed and the 1857 Coast Survey. NY and the Master invoke the earlier 1827 proposal reserving islands to low water, *Handly's Lessee v. Anthony*, 18 U.S. (5 Wheat.)374 (1820), *Vermont v. New Hampshire*, 289 U.S.593 (1933), and access to navigable water. The Master regards low water as the more plausible original intent, while acknowledging uncertainty and that the express 1827 phrase did not appear in the final Article Second. Report 151–155. The high-water path must distinguish interstate territorial boundaries from ordinary ownership rules; the low-water path must explain why an omitted negotiation term and nonidentical river precedents govern this tidal island. Convenience can inform interpretation of an uncertain term without itself authorizing replacement of an established line.

NJ Exceptions 28–38 supplies the principal contrary grounds: the 1827 offer was rejected and further litigation/negotiations followed; Article Third expressly uses low-water language that Article Second omits; its 1829 complaint described NY possession as fast land, without necessarily conceding acquired sovereignty; the 1808 NY conveyance stopped at ordinary high water whereas the 1904 NJ deed included intervening tidelands; and *Handly's Lessee* concerned a State bounded by a river, unlike an island exception within the other State's midwater territory. NJ points to its demands beginning in 1892 (P383(a),P405,P1 p. 11). Its modern-access answer is that Ellis already was a federal enclave surrounded by NJ territory, and no evidence shows access had been obstructed. These support inference, not an express compact high-water clause or an independently adjudicated boundary. The conditional Moody letter and federal-rights reservation remain applicable.

**Historical mapping.** The Master credits the 1857 Coast Survey as the best available depiction, based on Castagna's account of survey quality and control points linking the Liberty Island seawall/Fort Gibson to the accurate 1980 map and 1995 Marchuk survey. He accepts negligible relevant change before major filling and rejects NY's preferred 1837 map as less precise. He adopts NY experts' estimate of 4.69 acres to mean low water and adds 0.2 for the pier, yielding 4.89. Report 156–162. The expert's broader 4.5–6-acre low-water range expresses measurement uncertainty, not permission to allocate any shape within that acreage.

NJ accepts the historical contour but disputes precision of area calculations by non-testifying Sander Prisloe: experts could not verify the equipment/methods, Swanson was absent for part, and some discarded calculations were unrecorded (NJ Exceptions 40–41 n. 18, T2940,3041–3042). That challenge does not necessarily defeat the contour's usefulness. The 1857 mean-high-water estimate of 2.74 acres, the 1808 deed's approximate 2.97, and NPS's later rough 3.5-acre description are different sources/measurements, not interchangeable judicial findings.

**Pier dispute.** The Master credits Squires's inference that part of the L-shaped pier was filled before 1834, based on the charted surrounding material and asserted need for a sturdy ammunition-rail facility, with inadequate early piling technology. Report 158–161. NJ Exceptions 46–48 responds that pile-supported structures trap sediment; Squires acknowledged dense pilings as a possible explanation; changing pier shapes suggest piles; no persuasive evidence establishes ammunition/carts' actual use or weight; and Squires disclaimed engineering expertise to calculate the required supports (T2831–2832,2928–2929,3023,3026–3027,3061–3062). The report's reference to an "1839 chart" is challenged at NJ Exceptions 47 n. 20: NJ says no such chart is in the record and the relevant document is the 1819 map. This remains a source-description dispute, not a new finding that a nonexistent exhibit proves anything.

NJ additionally claims the 4.69-acre estimate already included the purported filled pier and that adding .2 duplicates it (Exceptions 48 n. 21). The Master says the addition covers the half not included in Swanson's conservative calculation (Report 161). The brief's transcript citation, "T3061-6 to 3052-8," is internally inconsistent and is not silently repaired. Do not resolve that conflict or adopt a numerical allowance twice without addressing the evidence. The viable paths are a supported finding of actual land within the original boundary, rejection of the unsupported footprint/addition, or a bounded technical determination if necessary to implement a selected legal line. A structure projecting over water does not automatically make its entire footprint sovereign land. The available merits record permits evaluation of the proof; exact geographic implementation can remain for the decree.

**Convenience adjustment.** The Master proposes reconfiguring the historical area to include the entire Main Building, adjacent land, ferry access and Fort Gibson while avoiding boundaries through principal buildings. His supplement selects a modified Survey A of about **5.1 acres**, rather than Survey B's 4.89. He changes the Baggage/Dormitory corridor crossing to right angles, preserves margins around the Main Building and a fifty-foot Ticket Office margin, and treats the full stairway as NJ despite approximately five inches of railing across the line. Report 162–170; Supplement 1–15. These are proposed equitable adjustments with legal consequences, not merely location of the original tide line.

New York submitted four map alternatives rather than a separate new survey; it did not dispute Marchuk's execution of Surveys A/B and preferred A within the Master's choices. NJ furnished both without surrendering exceptions. Supplement 3–8. That procedural history establishes neither default nor consent to moving sovereignty. The strongest argument for the adjustment is that uncertain ancient mapping and the Court's equitable original jurisdiction allow a practical implementation faithful to the Compact's allocation, avoiding split-building administration and preserving approximate entitlement. Its strongest objection is that acreage equivalence and administrative usefulness do not authorize exchanging territorial parcels fixed by a congressional compact. *Texas v. New Mexico* and current *Kansas* constrain relief; *Georgia* Parts VI/IX concern locating unresolved lines, not rewriting a fixed land boundary. The alternative is to determine the lawful historical line from the supported evidence and require an authenticated implementing survey/decree, leaving the States to negotiate any lawful boundary change themselves.

NJ acknowledges that the historical template intersects buildings and leaves a strip between the Main Building and ferry landing, but disputes proven administrative harm: no evidence of obstructed access, no showing that a fixed irregular line is harder to identify, federal preservation consultation already includes both States, and NPS expressed no operational objection to divided jurisdiction (NJ Exceptions 42–44). It also challenges a proposed decree that identifies all fill as NJ but reallocates some in the practical survey (p. 46 n. 19). The Court must either justify the adjustment under the actual compact/remedial authority or carry the chosen historical line into a consistent decree; presumed convenience is not record proof that the historical line is unworkable.

## 9. Justice-specific date-eligible sources and analytical limits

| Justice | Relevant published source and exact scope |
|---|---|
| Stevens | Joined *Georgia* Parts III (prescription), IV (later islands), VI (geographic implementation) and VII (avulsion application). His Part IX dissent, joined by Scalia, proposes a different equitable maritime line where the treaty specified none. Joined Souter's unanimous *Illinois*; authored current *Hess*'s entity-specific inquiry; joined current *Kansas* and *Louisiana* holdings. Maritime equity does not by itself establish support for a compact land exchange. |
| O'Connor | Joined *Georgia*'s controlling prescription, island and avulsion propositions, and Souter's unanimous *Illinois*. Authored current unanimous *Kansas*, preserving the laches-availability question and enforcing actual compact obligations. These are different procedural and territorial settings, requiring a component-specific application. |
| Scalia | Joined *Georgia* Part III and IV; dissented from Part VII only on the factual classification of the particular dredging record, expressly accepting the governing accretion/avulsion law. Joined Stevens's Part IX maritime dissent and Souter's *Illinois*. Current *Louisiana*'s independent review and actual-boundary implementation apply. No inference that he favors all artificial additions is supplied. |
| Kennedy | Joined *Georgia* Part III; his Part IV/part-V dissent treats its "all islands" language as including later **naturally formed** islands and objects to rendering the reservation redundant. Joined Scalia's Part VII factual dissent. Joined *Illinois*, current *Hess*, *Kansas* and *Louisiana*. The different treaty text and artificial fill remain necessary distinctions; this is an analogy, not a vote in the present case. |
| Souter | Authored unanimous *Illinois*: proof linked to the disputed territory, actual acts/statements, acquiescence, boundary laches and separate geographic implementation. Authored current *Louisiana*'s supported continuity and precise-decree holdings; joined current *Kansas* and *Hess*. He did not participate in *Georgia* (1990). |
| Thomas | Joined current *Kansas* and *Louisiana* controlling propositions and current *Venetie*'s title/territory distinction. No *Georgia* or *Illinois* participation is attributed to him; current *Hess* was a dissent, not an invented join. |
| Ginsburg | Joined current *Hess*, *Kansas*, *Louisiana* and *Venetie*. These supply actual compact structure, bounded relief and territorial distinctions; no participation in *Georgia* or *Illinois* is attributed. |
| Breyer | Joined current *Hess*, *Kansas*, *Louisiana* and *Venetie*. The same published holdings constrain the inquiry without imposing a preferred result; no participation in *Georgia* or *Illinois* is attributed. |

Enhanced issue-specific modeling is warranted by the alternative compact constructions, disputed prescriptive inferences, separate laches ground and independent shore/pier/remedy questions. Each Justice must assess those components and the strongest contrary path. Acceptance of NJ sovereignty over most fill does not entail agreement on high water, the pier, laches rationale, or adjustment. Agreement on a judgment does not establish a common rationale. No historical authorship or same-matter joinder is supplied.

## 10. Readiness and remaining stage

Ready for independent non-Stone modeling on the six exceptions using the actual supported factual disputes above. There is no remaining research-only demand for possession of the entire trial archive, and no missing-record premise establishes a failure by a litigant. No underlying-record fact is newly reconstructed. Contested map interpretation, notice, adverse sovereign activity, weight of countervailing acts and pier proof are judicial issues for that stage, not facts for the operator to choose.

The May 26 event must specify the disposition of each exception and the legal boundary/remedial directions adopted. Any final decree, precise geographic schedule, injunction implementation or retained-jurisdiction action must be separately supported and dated. The Master's proposed decree and Designated Survey do not themselves close those proceedings. Preserve the United States' Compact reservation and distinguish sovereignty from private/federal ownership and unpresented municipal or regulatory questions.

# CLEAN antecedent-source digest — New Jersey exceptions brief

Source: `research/chunk9_neutral_sources/new_jersey_exceptions.txt`, 146,436 characters, the State's July 31, 1997 exceptions and supporting brief, printed merits pages 1–49 and appendices 1a–26a, with front matter and reproduced map sheets. The entire extracted text was read sequentially. Map OCR is largely illegible; no independent measurement or visual interpretation of those sheets is claimed. The text and deed/letter appendices are legible. This is a digest of **New Jersey's advocacy and its source citations**, not an adjudication, a commitment, or independent verification of every underlying trial exhibit. No historical Supreme Court resolution of this matter was read.

## What New Jersey actually excepts to

The State accepts the Master's general landfill-sovereignty and no-prescription recommendations but excepts to three things: low water instead of mean high water; practical redesign instead of following the historical boundary; and inclusion of the pier as filled land (exceptions sheet; brief pp. 3–4, 27–28, 49). It requests the mean-high-water contour on the 1857 Coast Survey, excluding the pier, approximately 2.74 acres for New York. The Master's calculation starts with 4.69 acres at mean low water, adds approximately 0.2 acres of purported filled pier to reach 4.89, and then implements a practical boundary allocating 5.1 acres. These are different quantities, not interchangeable estimates of one contour (pp. 25–27, 48 n.21, 49 continuation).

The brief reports a 1996 trial, roughly 4,000 transcript pages and nearly 2,000 exhibits; final report March 31, 1997; New Jersey's two alternative surveys April 22; Master conference May 14; supplemented report May 30, selecting modified Survey A (pp. 5–6). These are antecedent procedural source recitations; use the operator-authorized reconstructed procedure, not automatic import of an unrecorded historical Supreme Court action.

## Compact and avulsion: agreement versus disputed rationale

New Jersey says it is **undisputed between the parties** that the post-1890 additions were artificial landfill and avulsive rather than accretive (p. 11, citing Report 97). That admission does not decide the antecedent question of whether Article II itself assigns an enlarged island or the later prescription issue.

New Jersey and the Master do not give precisely the same construction of Article II. New Jersey reads the present-tense preservation of jurisdiction as itself limiting New York to the 1834 island; the Master reads that wording in light of the federal military enclave and treats the Compact as not expressly deciding subsequent landfill, requiring the common-law avulsion rule (pp. 9–11, especially p. 10 n.5, Report 60–63). Preserve this as a real interpretive fork.

New Jersey invokes Article I's middle-water sovereign boundary and Article III(1)'s reserved submerged property, relying on Central Railroad, 209 U.S. 473, 478–79; it treats Article III jurisdiction as limited extraterritorial police power rather than transfer of sovereignty (pp. 6–10). Its assertions about the controlling force of any postdivergence cases cited in the brief are only research leads; the fresh validator must use actual simulated law.

## Prescription, notice, and contrary acts

The brief splits the history into 1834–1890 (no disputed fill), 1890–1934 (predominantly federal immigration control), 1934–1955 (immigration and some military use), and post-1955 (initial abandonment, eventual museum and overt interstate dispute), citing Report 103–106 (pp. 12–13). It contends neither continuous prescription nor New Jersey acquiescence is proved. Federal occupation reduced both States' opportunities for ordinary administration.

**Federal enclave issue:** New Jersey goes further than the Master. It argues New York ceded too much governmental authority to acquire prescription at all, citing Unzeuta and Murray; the Master treated cession as a complication affecting the quality of acts, not an absolute legal bar (p. 14 n.8, Report 110–11). The fresh packet should not merge those two positions.

**New Jersey's claimed sovereign acts and their precise sources:**

- Early objections to filling, followed by the 1904 deed. New Jersey says its demands began in 1892 (p. 35: P383(a), P405, P1 p.11). It treats the deed as a sovereign act and a rejection of New York's purported 1880 submerged-land conveyance (pp. 14–15; Report 112–13, 124–26; T886, T953–54, T4039–40). The deed was recorded in Hudson County December 23, 1904. **See the primary-appendix qualifications below; the brief's characterization is stronger than the letter's actual conditional wording.**
- Jersey City tax rolls listing the Island as federally exempt realty since at least 1940, plus water/gas utility taxes metered and paid in New Jersey (p. 16, Report 123, 131). An exempt listing differs from actual real-property tax collection.
- A New Jersey waterfront-development permit in 1933, sought by Immigration Commissioner Edward Corsi; Treasury and Army Corps references placed the work in New Jersey (pp. 16, 19; Report 134–35; P487 ¶28, P10–11; T1288–90, T2549–53, T1367–68; P374–380).
- New Jersey employment demands during 1930s projects, including Representative Mary Norton and unions; federal division of work between the States. New Jersey says these demands were expressly grounded in sovereignty, citing Report 132–33 (pp. 16, 19–20), rather than merely requests for economic favors.
- Federal Davis–Bacon locality decisions in 1947–1949 identifying Hudson County and rejecting New York construction wage rates (p. 20, P61–63; Report 135; P76–85; P490 ¶79). **Adverse qualification:** June 16, 1949 changed the locality determination to New York without a documented explanation (p. 20 n.12; P487 ¶¶45–47; P60–92, P428–434, P436–445; T2641-22 to T2645-17). Do not omit that reversal.
- 1963 Senate sale hearings with overt New Jersey sovereignty claims; New York Senator Keating recognized both States' interests, Mayor Wagner said jurisdiction could be resolved by agreement, and Representative Lindsay in 1965 described the fill as New Jersey jurisdiction (pp. 16–17, n.10; Report 137–39; P143, P154; P487 ¶¶69–78). These are relevant to New York's awareness by then, not proof of notice a century earlier.
- A 1986 Governors' Memorandum of Understanding to seek legislation sharing Ellis/Liberty tax revenues for programs for homeless persons; New Jersey enacted implementing law in 1987, New York did not (p. 17; Report 143–44 and 17a–23a; N.J. Stat. Ann. 32:32-1 et seq.). New Jersey characterizes this as recognition of taxing power. Do not convert the contemplated arrangement into a completed bilateral statutory settlement or agreed boundary.

**Federal recognition asserted as evidence, not adjudication:** Pierhead/bulkhead maps from 1890 through 1915 carry an Ellis Island, New Jersey designation (pp. 18–19; Report 118–22; appendices F–G). New Jersey says these enabled filling, not merely navigation, and notes Secretary Elihu Root's approval. It also cites the 1963 GSA legal opinion (Report 140–42; P144 p.70), 1968/1980 Park Service planning documents (P166; P484 p.9), 1984 historic-register nomination (D74 vol. III pp.1343–50; D952), and the federal New Jersey workers' compensation position in Collins, 956 F.2d 383 (pp. 20–21). These are attributed federal positions; the Park Service planning appendix estimates an original 3.5-acre area, so it is not a precise survey or a judicial determination of shoreline acreage.

## New Jersey's response to New York's acts

The response here largely adopts the Master's insufficiency conclusions. **It does not individually refute every fill-located hospital birth/death or residence item that New York may detail in its own brief.** That difference matters: the other source should not be displaced by a generic statement from this one.

- **Vital records:** New Jersey says many birth, death and marriage records are inconclusive as to fill rather than the original island (p. 22; Report 114). It gives no new certificate-by-certificate analysis in this brief.
- **Taxes:** It asserts no more than a scintilla before 1955, later evidence not tied to fill, and actual ARA Leisure sales/corporation tax payments only since 1990 (p. 22; Report 130). A Park Service employee's distribution of New York nonresident forms does not prove actual tax payments (p. 22 n.14; Report 115). The State emphasizes that the dispute had become fixed by 1984 and the tax sharing MOU followed in 1986, preceding those recent receipts (p. 23; Report 131–32).
- **Police:** New Jersey disputes credible proof of New York criminal enforcement or ordinary patrol on the island, distinguishing Harbor Police work in surrounding waters (p. 23; T2595-4 to T2614-1; T3950-23 to T3961-10). It cites countervailing New Jersey police activity (Report 114; T3636–37). This is advocacy about evidentiary weight; not a conceded absence of every New York police act.
- **Voting:** New Jersey distinguishes voter registration from proof someone voted. It says registers cover only eight years, no person claiming Ellis residence was registered after 1953 (pp. 23–24; Report 112 n.43; D957–965). It argues nine years of voting maps show the original almond-shaped island and Oyster Island, dredged away before 1900, rather than filled Ellis (p. 24 n.15; D932 p.9). This is a map-specific rebuttal, not proof that no registered resident ever lived on fill. The fresh validator should compare New York's person/building evidence.
- **Letterhead/address:** Forms using New York or New York Harbor do not necessarily state sovereign jurisdiction; immigration/postal districts did not follow State lines (p. 24; Report 116–17; T3944, T1494, P490 ¶¶129–134). INS historian Marian Smith says northern New Jersey was in the New York immigration district from 1891 to 1956, and the administrative office/post office were on the original island (p. 24 n.16; T3942-21 to T3950-22).

**Laches:** The brief reports New York's eve-of-trial amendment request, the Master's denial and allowance of relevant evidence nevertheless (pp. 5, 12 n.6; Interim Opinion 51; Report 27–29). New Jersey says laches cannot substitute for prescription/acquiescence. This brief does not furnish a separate developed record of prejudice or a complete independent laches analysis. Do not infer that the issue vanished from review or that late pleading establishes litigant default.

## Mean high water versus low water

New Jersey's substantive grounds, pp. 28–38:

1. The Master's low-water negotiation evidence was one **rejected 1827 offer**, preceding further litigation and later negotiations. Article II omits low-water language while Article III expressly uses it (pp. 28–30; Report 72; P280–292). New Jersey argues deletion/omission and overall structure outweigh that offer; the inference is disputed, not a textual express high-water designation.
2. The State uses the ordinary meaning of island and submerged/tidally flowed lands to favor high water; it invokes Article III(1) and the mid-water sovereign boundary. Handly's Lessee concerned a State bounded by a river, whereas this island is an exception within the other State's middle-water boundary (pp. 30–32, 38).
3. Its 1829 complaint described New York's adverse possession as limited to fast land (pp. 32–34; quoted in Devoe, 108 U.S. 401, 407). New Jersey denies the Master correctly called this an admission that sovereignty had already passed (p. 33 n.17). A party's older pleading is evidence of its position, not necessarily mutual agreement.
4. The 1808 New York conveyance expressly reaches ordinary high water; the 1904 New Jersey deed includes land between high and low water (pp. 34–36; T326-1 to T327-3; T2943-19 to -23; T695-15 to T706-41; P4–5, P7, P339–341, P351). New Jersey argues silent New York acquiescence in the later deed as practical construction; the primary letter/deed preserve important qualifications below.
5. An island surrounded by New Jersey waters was already an enclave, federal jurisdiction prevailed, and Congress reserved federal rights when consenting to the Compact; a modern access concern does not prove the States intended low water in 1834 (pp. 36–38). This is a direct reply to the Master's historical-convenience inference.

## Historical template versus practical redesign

The brief accepts the 1857 Coast Survey as the best evidence of the 1834 island and explains why (pp. 39–41): agency tidal measurement and forty-step method (T812-16 to T814-21), general agreement on best available methods (T3430-24 to T3431-20), scale focused on three islands, Liberty Island seawall control point and two Fort Gibson wall points matching 1980 photomap/1995 survey (Report 157). It says no proven intervening fill between 1834 and 1857, and the 1837 map lacked comparable detail/control confirmation (Report 158).

New Jersey challenges acreage estimates prepared by non-testifying Sander Prisloe: experts did not know equipment accuracy, methods or verification; Swanson was absent for part, and some calculations discarded unrecorded (pp. 40–41 n.18; T2940, T3041–42). This attacks numerical precision, not necessarily the contour's usefulness.

New Jersey acknowledges the template line would intersect a few buildings and put a strip between the Main Building and ferry landing (p. 42). It disputes **evidence of resulting administrative harm**: no instance of obstruction crossing New Jersey waters since 1834, no witness/document suggesting strip access would be blocked, no proof a fixed irregular line would be harder to identify, federal preservation consultation already involves both States, and the Park Service has supported split jurisdiction without expressing operational concern (pp. 42–44). These are responses to practical risk, not denials of the geometric facts.

The legal objection is that convenience cannot rewrite a congressionally approved compact; any new bargain belongs to States/Congress (pp. 44–46, citing Ohio v. Kentucky, Illinois v. Kentucky, Vermont v. New York and Wyoming v. Colorado). Georgia v. South Carolina's actual parts and joins must be independently assessed; this brief does not erase that distinction. Paragraphs 1–2 of the proposed decree identify fill as New Jersey and the Compact boundary, while paragraph 3's practical survey grants New York some fill; New Jersey calls that internally inconsistent (p. 46 n.19; Report 169–70; Supplement 16–18).

## Pier: concrete evidentiary objections and numerical caution

At pp. 46–48 New Jersey identifies two asserted supports for the Master: apparent accretion around the pier on an old map, and Squires's inference about an ammunition rail pier requiring fill. It supplies these specific objections:

- **Map-date problem:** The report's reference to an 1839 chart apparently means the 1819 map; no 1839 chart is in the record, and the 1819 map is the only pre-1834 map with possible accretion near the pier (p. 47 n.20; Report 158–59; T282-11 to -25). Do not silently invent another chart.
- **Pilings also trap sediment:** T282-11 to -25; T299-4 to -7; T3020-20 to T3021-4; T3061-12 to T3062-5; P382(e), P382(g). Squires conceded at least part could be dense pilings rather than fill, accounting for apparent accretion (T2928-19 to T2929-20; T3023-20 to -24; T3061-12 to T3062-5; P382(e)). This supplies a competing inference, not affirmative certainty that every part was piles.
- **Changing pier geometry, 1819–1857:** New Jersey says changes support a piling structure (T297-3 to T298-19; T313-3 to -8; T338-23 to T339-16; T3046-7 to -20; T3053-24 to T3054-3; T3059-24 to T3060-2; P382 series). The OCR of the exhibit letters is imperfect; consult the actual source before naming an exact subexhibit.
- **Engineering/use premise:** New Jersey says no convincing evidence of 1834 ammunition/rail use, cargo weight or necessary support method. Squires admitted he was not a licensed engineer and would not calculate the necessary support (p. 48; T2831-18 to T2832-25). This is a targeted foundation objection to the inference, not exclusion of all his historical testimony.
- **Alternative double count:** Even if fill existed, New Jersey says the 4.69-acre mean-low-water estimate already included the claimed filled pier, so the additional 0.2 acres was duplicative (p. 48 n.21). The printed/OCR transcript cite reads `T3061-6 to 3052-8`, internally inconsistent; do not repair it by guessing. The validator can assess the estimate against the Master and source evidence without importing false precision. The ultimate practical 5.1 acres is yet another alteration.

## Appendix qualifications that the advocacy summary can obscure

- **Appendix A, p.1a:** The 1800 New York federal cession expressly reserves execution of State civil and criminal process. Any claim that absolutely no State authority survived needs to confront that proviso.
- **Appendix B, pp.2a–5a:** The June 30, 1808 conveyance describes the Island to ordinary high water and an estimated two acres, three rood, thirty-five perches. It conveys the State's title for the specified federal purposes. This does not by itself establish an interstate sovereign boundary at high water.
- **Appendix C, pp.6a–8a, Moody July 15, 1904:** The Attorney General asks for **such title as New Jersey may have**, recognizes unquestioned New York power over the island proper, and says it would seem submerged ownership is in New Jersey under the Compact. He also asserts Congress's commerce power permits use without State consent or compensation and seeks a grant to avoid dispute and promote comity. Thus the brief's assertion of absolute federal rejection of New York's 1880 transfer overstates the letter's level of certainty. The request is strong evidence of recognition but qualified in the document itself.
- **Appendix D, pp.9a–12a:** The November 30, 1904 deed recites $1,000 consideration, conveys all New Jersey right/title/claim/interest in formerly or presently tidally flowed land, and records the grant in Hudson County December 23. It **expressly preserves all United States rights and privileges as though no grant had been accepted**. Do not omit that saving clause or infer a federal waiver; private title and sovereign jurisdiction remain distinct questions. The metes-and-bounds OCR has possible directional anomalies; this digest performs no boundary survey.
- **Appendix E, pp.13a–14a:** The 1980 Park Service environmental assessment identifies fill/submerged lands as New Jersey and the original portion as New York, but uses a 3.5-acre original-area estimate. It supports an attributed jurisdictional understanding, not precise acreages for decree drafting.
- **Appendices F–G, pp.15a–24a:** Reproduced harbor-line maps, badly OCR'd. The text of the brief identifies their dates/titles. No independent graphical verification or measurement here.
- **Appendix H, pp.25a–26a:** The 1880 New York act purports to cede its right/title and jurisdiction to specified submerged parcels for federal docks, wharves, sea walls, batteries and related structures. Its actual existence supplies the contrary claimed governmental act; its validity is contested, not erased by New Jersey's later deed.

## Completion and remaining source limits

All text of this State's exceptions brief and appendices has now been read. No commitment or merits determination is made. This completes the requested supporting source work; the separate fresh validator has the Master, supplement and New York exceptions and must reconcile their specific contrary evidence. The unresolved transcript citation and unmeasured map graphics are disclosed rather than silently supplied. This digest supplies enough to identify the material evidentiary forks; it does not demand a full archive to decide any point already supported by sufficient reported evidence.

# OT1997 chunk 9 — clean later-matter neutral preparation

This file covers **Lacy, DeCarlo, City of Florence/Stemler, and Duffy only**. Waste Management, Cabrales and Beggerly require separately validated handoffs and are not framed here. This is neutral preparation, not a commitment or Court action. Read with [the entering-law slice](../entering-law/OT_1997CHUNK9.md) and [the frozen May 21 neutral projection](OT_1997CHUNK9_ENTERING_NEUTRAL_PROJECTION.md). The latter two control over summaries and navigation copies. Research cutoffs are the individual scheduled dates below; retrieval occurred October 24, 2026.

## Common institutional, source, and chronology rules

The roster is Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. All are seated before submission and decision; no case-specific nonparticipation or argument date is supplied for these four added matters. Do not invent either. Review of a federal court of appeals decision is available under §1254(1), including a case remanded below; the state-finality restrictions of §1257 do not govern these federal appellate cases. The user's authorized inclusion and date fix the proposed Court event; they do not create historical grants, assigned Supreme Court dockets, intervening proceedings, preservation, or facts. No new procedural reconstruction is adopted.

Each lower appellate opinion was read completely, including footnotes and separate writings. CAP's reported judicial text was compared with the Public.Resource.Org transcription of the same opinion. Allegations, testimony, assumed facts, lower legal conclusions, and adjudicated findings retain their different status. Historical post-divergence Supreme Court citations inside lower opinions are **not** independent entering authority: use the effective simulated entry and its limits. Published accounts supply the necessary propositions for the stated review, without claiming possession of original pleadings, transcripts, warrants, depositions or briefs. No archive search is needed merely to acquire every original. A path requiring an unreported material fact or exact instrument term must return to focused research under Engine §14, beginning with the Archive if original Supreme Court record materials are actually needed. Source absence establishes no litigant default.

The baseline ends May 21. These dates remain prospective until intervening earlier decisions are completed and law is refreshed. **Florence cannot be finally modeled before the May 26 County of Sacramento v. Lewis decision is committed and its public law enters a fresh neutral handoff.** Duffy must receive any material May 28 DeCarlo rule before its June 3 event. Lacy and DeCarlo, both May 28, use the same pre-group law unless expressly coordinated; neither automatically governs the other. A pending proposal has no precedential effect. No return, enforcement, deadline, stay or later lower proceeding is inferred.

Exact selected Holdings entries are copied without substantive editing in [LATER_EXTRACT](../entering-law/OT_1997CHUNK9_LATER_EXTRACT.md), and selected reusable rules in [LATER_STANDARDS](../entering-law/OT_1997CHUNK9_LATER_STANDARDS.md). [LATER_CURRENT](../entering-law/OT_1997CHUNK9_LATER_CURRENT.md) copies relevant public-law passages from the frozen projection. These are reading aids only; consult the full authorized source for any entry or qualification not reproduced.

## United States v. Lacy — May 28, 1998

### Identity, vehicle, and scope

Ninth Circuit No. 95-30370, **119 F.3d 742 (July 10, 1997)**; no Supreme Court docket or argument date supplied. The Ninth Circuit affirmed a conviction after a jury trial in the Western District of Washington. This is ordinary review of that completed appellate judgment, not an interlocutory proceeding. Lacy seeks reversal of the adverse suppression ruling and resulting conviction judgment; the United States defends the warrant and judgment.

The two authorized questions are staleness of the ten-month-old download information and particularity of the authorization to seize the computer system, storage media and related records. Instructional scienter, the statutory meaning of “matter,” the interstate-commerce element and plain error were separately decided below but are **outside this grant**. Receipt was acquitted and the importation charge dismissed before trial; do not reinstate either or transfer them into the possession review.

### Supported record and actual lower reasoning

BAMSE, a Danish electronic bulletin board, recorded sixteen calls by a user identifying himself as “Jim Bakker” and six downloaded picture files. Customs traced the number to Lacy's apartment; telephone records matched the recorded dates. The warrant affidavit specifically described at least two downloaded GIF files depicting minors engaged in sexual activity. This is evidence of receipt, not merely an order or attempt to purchase. The agent described, from training and experience, possessors' high valuation and lengthy retention of such material in secure locations, often their homes. Lacy contested the foundation for applying those retention observations to him. The lower court considered the confirmed downloads sufficient foundation; it expressly declined an indefinite-retention assumption.

The warrant authorized an apartment search and seizure of computer equipment, records and BAMSE-related documents. The reported authorization described hardware generically. The agent stated that the investigators could not determine whether the images were on the drive or disks, or specify in advance the hardware and software needed for accurate retrieval. The Ninth Circuit found probable cause regarding the system and objective document limits, including the BAMSE connection. It distinguished *Kow*, whose authorization and affidavit lacked comparable limitations. Its particularity analysis considered the basis for each described category, objective selection standards, and whether greater specificity was possible with information available at issuance. Those are the circuit's formulation, not a newly adopted Supreme Court checklist.

Agents seized the computer, more than 100 disks and documents. **Some disks came from a separate storage room searched with Lacy's consent**; do not describe every disk as seized from the apartment under the challenged warrant. The district court suppressed several documents both sides called inconsequential. Images subsequently discovered and trial admissions cannot retrospectively establish probable cause or particularity. The record's March 6, 1993 possession date is reproduced in the reported jury instruction; no separate exact warrant date is supplied by this preparation.

### Law entering, lawful alternatives, and remedies

*Sgro*, 287 U.S. 206 (1932), requires an adequately current basis; *Gates*, 462 U.S. 213 (1983), requires the practical totality and substantial-basis review of a magistrate's determination. *Andresen*, 427 U.S. 463 (1976), and *Coolidge*, 403 U.S. 443 (1971), supply protection against general exploratory searches. The lower opinion's *Weber*, *Dozier*, *Gann*, *Cardwell*, *Kow* and *Spilotro* are date-eligible circuit authorities, with their actual facts and limits, not national law merely because cited below.

Simulated *Pinson* (May 8, 1995) and *Myers* (June 11, 1996) protect concealed domestic information but do not invalidate every technologically related warrant or prescribe digital-search protocols. Their Scalia opinions and exact joins are copied in the reading aid. Their remedial rules keep the violation, preservation and exclusion questions separate. *Ornelas*'s nonwarrant appellate-review rule, reproduced in **Independent appellate review of suspicion and nonwarrant probable cause** (effective May 28, 1996), expressly preserves Gates's distinct warrant review. The earlier source's O'Melveny reference has no demonstrated role in these search questions and supplies no controlling digital-search rule.

The warrant-supporting path connects actual receipt, durable electronic storage and the supported retention account to continued possession after ten months; unknowable storage location and retrieval requirements can support inclusive equipment categories while offense-linked document limits constrain execution. Its strongest objection is the risk of turning generalized habits into perpetual probable cause and investigative convenience into permission to seize everything. A lawful version must address this person's proven acquisition, this interval and the particular categories.

The challenge path treats the interval and generalized retention inference as insufficient on this affidavit, or distinguishes the justification for looking for two files from seizure of an entire system and unrelated records. Its strongest objection is that the affidavit does not merely guess at acquisition, the circuit reports specific limits, and investigators could not know where the files were stored. A narrower treatment may decide one issue without issuing a categorical rule for all electronic evidence. No modern cloud, smartphone, remote-server, forensic-protocol or later digital-search doctrine enters.

If a defect is found, suppression and conviction relief still require preserved remedial analysis. *Leon* is available only on a properly presented, supported ground, excluding knowing/reckless magistrate deception, abandonment of neutrality, an affidavit so devoid of probable-cause indicia that reliance is wholly unreasonable, and facial deficiency preventing reasonable reliance. It includes relevant information-supplying officers and presupposes execution within scope. *Evans*'s court-clerical-error rule creates no broader police technology immunity. The reported opinion did not decide a Leon fallback; do not assume it was waived, established, or automatically dispositive. No automatic acquittal, release, retrial, new hearing, or suppression of consent-obtained evidence follows merely from correcting the warrant ruling.

### Source limit and readiness
