[CAP complete opinion](https://static.case.law/f3d/119/html/0742-01.html), [local text](../research/lower/united_states_v_lacy_cap.txt); [Public.Resource.Org](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/119/119.F3d.742.95-30370.html), [local transcription](../research/lower/united_states_v_lacy_source.txt). The complete warrant and affidavit are not separately reproduced. The reported categories and quoted affidavit propositions support the two authorized legal issues; a ruling on an unquoted clause, warrant incorporation, item-level execution, a disputed investigator representation or unsupported good-faith facts requires the actual source rather than inference. Ready for independent modeling after the ordinary earlier-event refresh, with these limits preserved.

## DeCarlo v. Fry — May 28, 1998

### Corrected judgment, channel, and components

Second Circuit Nos. 97-7702, 97-7704, 97-7754, **141 F.3d 56 (April 6, 1998)**. The supplied description of what a source “proposes” is replaced by the actual appellate disposition. The Second Circuit **affirmed qualified-immunity summary judgments for Astle and Blank; vacated the official-capacity judgment against DuRose and remanded for a correct municipal-liability determination; required clarification of the disposition of the distinct post-expungement personal-capacity claims against him; allowed amendment of pre-expungement claims; and affirmed Turner's personal-capacity jury verdict.** No personal damages judgment against DuRose was entered by that disposition.

The authorized question concerns Astle/Blank's immunity and the distinct proceedings remaining concerning DuRose. The DeCarlos seek relief from the adverse immunity judgments; DuRose's successful challenge to the official-capacity judgment and the unresolved personal claims require separate review rather than describing both parties as seeking the same disposition. A party wishing to disturb an additional lower component requires authorized scope; the unappealed Perales declaration and Turner's affirmed personal verdict are not automatically revived. This is §1254(1) review of the appellate decision following trial, cross-appeals and summary judgments, **not an interlocutory appeal from a denial of qualified immunity**.

### Record and lower grounds

The 1985 “indicated” abuse finding led to revocation of Juanita DeCarlo's day-care certification. The plaintiffs alleged a deficient bad-faith investigation; that allegation is not a new finding. A state administrative judge affirmed revocation in April 1986. The State expunged the indication against Juanita in May 1987 but retained the household link while the indication against Vito remained. The Appellate Division affirmed revocation in October 1987. After a later hearing, the administrative judge expunged Vito's indication in September 1988 for lack of credible evidence. Expungement and the earlier appellate judgment are distinct events; the packet does not convert either into an automatic reinstatement adjudication.

Juanita requested reinstatement in October 1988; her application was complete by February 1989. The lower opinion identifies the operative state regulations as requiring investigation within 75 days of a valid application and notice within 30 days after completion, with a fair hearing available after denial. The county failed to decide. These requirements do not themselves establish the federal right, entitlement to certification, constitutional damages or an individual defendant's culpability.

Astle testified that she did not recall instructions on the deadlines, was uncertain how to reconcile the prior revocation decision with expungement, and asked Blank. Blank consulted Turner; Turner and Blank consulted state regional director Klump rather than DuRose. Klump told **Turner** that the county had to decide. No decision followed. Do not infer that Astle or Blank received every later instruction, or that DuRose knew what the consultation chain conveyed. Conversely, consultation is not a new universal immunity defense.

The original pro se complaint concerned pre-expungement acts. The later amended complaint added post-expungement claims, including personal and official claims against DuRose and claims against Turner, Astle and Blank. At trial the judge directed official-capacity liability against DuRose under Rule 50(a), treating an earlier denial of his summary-judgment motion as though it established liability as law of the case. The jury awarded $300,000; fees and costs were $70,324.47. The Second Circuit found the premise mistaken: denial of summary judgment was no judgment for the plaintiffs. It left the familiar trial court to decide whether the trial evidence warrants judgment for plaintiffs, judgment for the county, or a jury trial under actual Monell standards. It did not decide that liability exists or fails, and did not reach damages/fee amounts.

The post-expungement personal claim's disposition was unclear: an earlier order could not logically dispose of claims added later, and an informal letter did not substitute for a ruling. The circuit required clarification and the proper stage-specific decision. DuRose's Article 78 adequate-process argument remained expressly undecided. The lower court affirmed Astle/Blank because reasonable officials in their actual circumstances sought supervisory advice; it supplied no wider categorical rule excusing all known deadlines.

### Entering law, competing paths, and limits

Harlow/Anderson governs objective, particularized **conduct-time** notice. *Elder* (February 23, 1994, unanimous Ginsburg opinion) requires all relevant legal authority on a preserved immunity question and permits no retroactive notice. *Lanier* (March 31, 1997, unanimous) rejects a Supreme-Court-only or factual-twin requirement while preserving uncertain applications; a state-law violation or bad intent alone supplies no federal right. Apply the law available during the actual 1988–1989 delay, not later decisions as new notice. The state scheme must establish the asserted entitlement and required process; missed timing alone does not decide the federal claim.

*Monell*, *Pembaur*, and *Kentucky v. Graham* distinguish county policy and causation from employee liability. The operative Standards module **Municipal responsibility and the underlying federal deprivation**, effective as supplemented by *McMillian* June 2, 1997, requires the correct entity and function-specific policymaker inquiry; operational discretion or a job title alone does not establish final policy. Its Alabama sheriff holding does not identify Oneida's policymaker. The damages judgment's official capacity forecloses using **personal** qualified immunity to defeat that county claim. The federal deprivation and attribution remain distinct.

*Crawford-El* (June 16, 1997) and **Circumstantial proof of unconstitutional motive despite qualified immunity** preserve competent evidence and ordinary burdens, necessary bounded discovery and actual causation without a special heightened quantum. They establish neither motive as an element of every process claim nor a new immunity defense. Current *Clanton*, *Valot*, and *Smith* preserve personal responsibility, conduct-date notice, proper assumed facts, and the separation of notice from present merits. Johnson/Behrens's interlocutory limits are real law but do not turn this postjudgment appeal into a barred factual-sufficiency appeal.

On Astle/Blank, one supported path affirms reasonable reliance on the actual administrative uncertainty and supervisory consultation without declaring every deadline breach constitutional. The competing path finds that an unreasoned consultation label does not resolve the specific prolonged deprivation or the actual extent of authority, knowledge and notice, and remands for a properly particularized inquiry. The counterarguments are reciprocal: genuine uncertainty cannot be erased by hindsight, while simply consulting someone cannot immunize otherwise plainly unlawful conduct. The model must not resolve absent knowledge facts or treat a discretionary license decision as exemption from all procedural duties.

On DuRose, a supported path preserves the circuit's correction of the mistaken Rule 50 premise and its separate clarification remand; another may seek a narrower disposition if the correctly reviewed trial record compels a legally available outcome. The latter cannot be selected merely because liability seems implausible: the full trial evidence is not reproduced and the circuit expressly reserved the factual application. No new county policy finding, personal liability, damages award, trial entitlement, recertification order, or universal Article 78 answer is supplied. The actual stages of pleading, summary judgment and trial remain distinct.

### Sources and readiness

[CAP full opinion](https://static.case.law/f3d/141/html/0056-01.html), [local](../research/lower/decarlo_v_fry_cap.txt); [Public.Resource.Org](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/141/141.F3d.56.97-7754.97-7704.97-7702.html), [local](../research/lower/decarlo_v_fry_source.txt). These complete opinions support the bounded remand and immunity issues. The original trial evidence, amended pleadings and administrative file were not recovered. Those limits prevent a new dispositive finding about county policy, DuRose's knowledge, or the merits of an unclarified claim. Ready for independent modeling after ordinary chronological refresh, preserving the actual split disposition and source limits.

## City of Florence v. Chipman / Stemler v. City of Florence — June 1, 1998

### Actual channel, parties, and review scope

Sixth Circuit Nos. 96-5993 and 96-5996, **126 F.3d 856 (October 8, 1997), rehearing denied November 13, 1997**. The consolidated appeals were brought by **the plaintiffs** after individual-officer dismissals under Rule 12(b)(6) and municipal summary judgments. This is **not** an officers' interlocutory appeal from denial of immunity. The source warning about Johnson factual-sufficiency jurisdiction must not misdescribe the channel. Ordinary pleading and summary-judgment standards still prohibit resolving disputed facts against the nonmovant.

The City and official-capacity defendants **won below**: their summary judgments were affirmed. Black's individual-officer substantive-due-process claim and Stemler's individual-officer orientation-based equal-protection claim were reinstated. All other challenged dismissals remained affirmed. “City of Florence” is the inventory caption, not proof that the city lost or may claim personal qualified immunity. The review opposing reinstatement belongs to the adversely affected individual-officer components; no new petition number or unnoticed party substitution is fabricated. The authorized questions concern the compelled dangerous placement, discriminatory selection despite probable cause, and conduct-time immunity. They do not independently reopen municipal liability, false arrest, malicious prosecution, abandoned excessive force or an unpleaded fabrication claim.

### Supported facts and distinction between claims

**Conni Black died; Susan Stemler did not.** The opinion recites evidence in the plaintiffs' favor unless otherwise indicated. Black and Steve Kritis had been drinking; witnesses described his assaults, threats and dangerous pursuit after Black asked Stemler to drive her away. These are reported allegations and evidence, not this Court's findings. Barker warned Lieutenant Dusing that Kritis threatened the women's safety. Stemler described intoxication, assaults and threats. Other Florence and Boone officers arrived. Kritis called Stemler a lesbian and accused her of taking Black; the label spread among the officers. Stemler denies being a lesbian, which does not eliminate a claim based on their perception.

The account supports knowledge of Kritis's intoxication and violence, but the officers' roles and knowledge differ. Reuthe told Dusing that Kritis smelled of alcohol; Dusing's later claim conflicts with other evidence. Dusing directed Dolan to tell Black she would be arrested for public intoxication if she did not leave with Kritis. Dolan and Alsip lifted her from Stemler's car; Alsip placed her in Kritis's truck. The estate alleges this was against her will; Dolan's contrary account remains disputed. Alsip said he never heard a request to leave and would have acted differently if he had known the preceding events. No collective inference supplies every officer's personal knowledge, culpability or causal role.

The crash occurred about five minutes after departure. Kritis reported a renewed struggle; reconstruction could not determine what caused the steering movement. He was later convicted of state offenses. Causation is not automatically established by proximity, nor erased merely because an officer did not drive the truck. Avoid gratuitous injury detail; the legally material facts are fatal injury, timing and causal dispute.

Stemler was arrested for DUI, prosecuted twice and acquitted at the second trial. A separate state civil judgment found probable cause; Kentucky treated it as final for issue preclusion despite a pending appeal. The circuit used that predicate to affirm false-arrest and ordinary malicious-prosecution dismissal, while preserving reconsideration if the state judgment later reversed. No such later reversal is established here. A fabrication theory raised after the officers had been dismissed was not pleaded against them and did not support reversal. A separate excessive-force claim was voluntarily dismissed. Equal protection remains distinct: the opinion described a supported comparison between Stemler's arrest and the nonarrest of visibly intoxicated Kritis, plus alleged purposeful group-based animus.

### Lower reasoning and material concurrence

The circuit rejected a general inference that uncertainty at the edges of substantive due process defeats every claim. It characterized Black as actually restrained by threats and physical placement, distinguished a general failure to protect, and found the alleged knowing dangerous transfer sufficient under the custody/deliberate-indifference authorities. It declined to decide noncustodial state-created danger. Its formulation sometimes says “knew or reasonably should have known”; the actual entering law and any new Lewis holding must determine the proper culpability, not that shorthand.

On Stemler, the court held probable cause did not foreclose selective enforcement, used effect and purpose plus an actually comparable unarrested person, and rejected a blanket claim that orientation-based animus could never violate equal protection. It found no need for heightened scrutiny. Its references to historical Armstrong, Whren, Romer and Bryan County must be replaced by their actual simulated scope; in particular Armstrong's federal-prosecutor discovery rule does not automatically supply a merits or police-stop discovery rule here. The supplied entering module identifies Bryan County's hiring/fault issue as reserved, not a historical holding to import.

Judge Wellford agreed with the disposition but would avoid declaring facts, broadening culpability or equating classifications. He found sufficient allegations of intentional arbitrary conduct or conscious disregard toward a person in custody and would leave actual constitutional tort proof below. At rehearing he stressed actual awareness and actual drawing of the serious-risk inference under Farmer. He also preserved questions concerning later Kentucky appellate rulings and claim splitting rather than adjudicating them. This separate lower opinion is a legal alternative, not a Supreme Court vote predictor.

### Entering law and open paths

*DeShaney*, 489 U.S. 189 (1989), distinguishes nonprotection from affirmative restraint; *Youngberg*, 457 U.S. 307 (1982), supplies custody-related protection; *Daniels*, 474 U.S. 327 (1986), excludes mere negligence. Simulated *Collins* (February 26, 1992) denies a general safe-workplace duty while expressly reserving compulsion, direct bodily injury and different affirmative-danger claims. Its separate attribution and substantive-right inquiries remain intact. Simulated *Pinder* (May 21, 1996) rejects custody from knowledge, promises or detention of an assailant, but leaves distinct affirmative deprivation analytically open. Its 5–4 immunity holding concerns a noncoercive 1989 undertaking, not this alleged forced 1994 transfer. O'Connor's conduct-time application has Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas with Stone; Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer dissented. The precise public positions matter without compelling a vote here.

*Harlow*, *Anderson*, *Elder* and simulated *Lanier* require particularized apparent unlawfulness at the conduct date without a factual twin. **The event occurred overnight February 18–19, 1994.** Elder's February 23 opinion, Farmer later in 1994, and all later decisions may clarify present analysis but do not themselves give earlier notice. The conduct-date inquiry must rely on DeShaney, Youngberg, Daniels, relevant pre-event circuit authority and other law then effective. The lower opinion's *Nishiyama*, *Walton*, *Meador*, *Heflin* and similar authorities remain evidence of their actual holdings; later *Foy*, *Kneipp*, *Lewellen* or *Gazette* cannot be treated as earlier notice. No mandatory merits-first sequence exists.

One Black path treats the coercive transfer as the officers' own custodial deprivation, applies the constitutionally sufficient culpability on properly assumed facts and rejects immunity where earlier law obviously covered it. Its strongest limits are individual knowledge, the correct mental state, the particular deprivation, causation and the possibility that later cases created rather than merely explained an extension. The competing path grants conduct-time immunity or a narrower correction if the required duty/culpability application was not sufficiently clear; it must explain why actual coercive custody differs insufficiently from received protection rules, rather than rely on abstract uncertainty about all substantive due process. A specific constitutional provision may require analysis if actually applicable and presented, but no new Fourth Amendment claim is silently substituted for the authorized due-process theory.

On equal protection, *Yick Wo*, *Oyler*, *Wayte*, and *Moreno* provide received nonarbitrary-enforcement and purpose/effect principles. Simulated *Whren* (June 10, 1996, unanimous Thomas opinion) preserves independent equal-protection objections despite ordinary traffic probable cause; *Romer* (May 20, 1996, seven-Justice Souter opinion) preserves ordinary review's limits on bare hostility without an orientation tier. Walmer/Thomasson's military conduct and rebuttable-inference holdings do not authorize civilian animus. Neither postdates conduct to create notice retroactively. The supported path allowing the claim rests on alleged arbitrary group-based purpose, differential treatment and the actual comparator under already existing principles; the competing immunity path must test their particular conduct-time clarity, not assert that no protection exists absent a suspect class. No intentional discrimination is found merely because the officers discussed orientation. A ground rejecting the comparison must respect the pleading posture and plaintiff-favorable account.

*Crawford-El*, *Clanton*, *Valot*, *Smith* and *Ricci* preserve separate elements, personal responsibility, ordinary proof, manner/scope limits, and conduct-date immunity. Ricci's additional civil-custody rule does not itself decide this compelled transfer or supply notice in 1994. The municipal defendants' favorable judgment is not reopened by finding an individual claim viable. A remand would authorize ordinary claim-specific proceedings, not liability, damages, a mandatory trial, specified discovery or automatic action on separate state cases.

### Sources and required dependency refresh

[CAP full opinion and concurrence](https://static.case.law/f3d/126/html/0856-01.html), [local](../research/lower/city_of_florence_v_chipman_cap.txt); [Public.Resource.Org including rehearing and all footnotes](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/126/126.F3d.856.96-5996.96-5993.html), [local](../research/lower/city_of_florence_v_chipman_source.txt). The complete lower published account supports these claims and posture. No new Kentucky appellate result, party participation, officer knowledge or immunity finding is admitted. **Record preparation is ready; final neutral law and independent commitments are not ready until Lewis's May 26 public law has been checked and the affected analysis refrozen.** A newly effective DeCarlo rule must also be checked, though its different facts and capacities cannot be assumed dispositive.

## Duffy v. Wolle — June 3, 1998

### Corrected relief and actual channel

Eighth Circuit No. 96-3210, **123 F.3d 1026 (August 19, 1997)**; no Supreme Court docket or argument date supplied. The circuit **affirmed final summary judgment for Wolle, Vietor and Longstaff** after the district court converted their supported motion to dismiss. Thus **Duffy seeks reversal and further proceedings; the judges defend the favorable judgment**, including alternative immunity and remedial arguments. The supplied request had those positions reversed. This is not an interlocutory immunity appeal; ordinary review of the entire final summary-judgment record and the Rule 56(f) ruling is available within the granted questions.

The three questions concern the availability of an individual constitutional damages action despite internal judicial EEO remedies, absolute immunity for administrative hiring, and summary judgment/discovery on alleged sex discrimination. Duffy sought a declaration, appointment injunction, damages, fees and costs below. The existence of those prayers does not establish entitlement, an automatic appointment remedy, an action against the court as an agency, or identical immunity consequences for all relief.

### Record and lower reasoning

The three district judges selected a chief probation officer under 18 U.S.C. §3602. A screening committee considered sixteen applications and forwarded McPhillips, Duffy and Stites; local applicants were forwarded as a courtesy. Duffy and McPhillips were experienced supervising probation officers. McPhillips had two more years of probation experience, two larger-district assignments, Administrative Office/Sentencing Commission work, a bachelor's and master's degree and a law degree. Duffy had a bachelor's and master's degree, incomplete doctoral study, extensive local work and strengths in management, pretrial and supervision. The outgoing chief recommended Duffy; Minnesota judges and McPhillips's supervisor recommended her. Relative strengths, stated reasons and the inferences they support must not become an unreported finding of actual motive.

Wolle described an Administrative Office recommendation for national advertising and a diverse qualified pool. Ailts recalled a passing comment about interest in appointing a female, but could not identify the source and inferred the position involved. Duffy reported Ailts's statement about recruiting minorities and women. The vacancy announcement itself did not specify gender. The circuit assumed the reported Administrative Office interest could influence recruitment; it did not find an actual sex-specific recruitment measure. The panel unanimously selected McPhillips and each judge swore that sex played no role, citing experience, education, interview and references. Duffy disputed the explanations and invoked the female-majority law-clerk hiring history of two judges. No motive finding follows simply from either sworn denial or accusation.

The district court rejected absolute immunity and the EEO-remedy bar but granted summary judgment. The circuit likewise treated an individual Davis-type employment action as cognizable; it rejected absolute immunity under Forrester because selecting an employee is administrative despite importance. It found no congressional direction making the Judicial Conference's internal EEO scheme exclusive. It also said the equal-protection right was clearly established, then analyzed whether the evidence supported a violation rather than granting immunity for an unclear right.

Applying the McDonnell Douglas framework, it found a prima facie case, legitimate nondiscriminatory explanations, and insufficient evidence of pretext/intentional discrimination. It distinguished inclusive recruitment from discriminatory selection, evaluated actual qualifications, and upheld disregarding the law-clerk comparison. It held Rule 56(f) did not compel discovery merely in hopes of finding support. Duffy had filed a counsel affidavit specifically asking to depose defendants to test their explanations; the district court reasoned that affidavits from defendants and screening members made additional discovery unhelpful. Whether that was permissible is genuinely presented, not an omitted request. Rule 56(f)'s text, reproduced in the lower opinion, permits postponement, affidavits, depositions, discovery or another just order on a supported inability to present essential opposing facts; it does not mandate every requested deposition.

### Entering law, supported paths, and limits

*Davis v. Passman*, 442 U.S. 228 (1979), supplies the received individual federal employment-discrimination action and Fifth Amendment protection. *Bush*, 462 U.S. 367 (1983), addresses congressional remedial design; the actual source, coverage and completeness of an alternative scheme matter. Simulated *Meyer* (February 23, 1994, unanimous Thomas opinion) rejects a **direct agency** Bivens extension, expressly preserving received individual Davis/Bivens/Carlson actions at their own scope; it does not decide this claim by agency analogy. A model proposing that statutory employee coverage itself forecloses relief must establish that exact date-effective predicate rather than import later remedial doctrine.

*Forrester*, 484 U.S. 219 (1988), treats personnel administration as a different function from adjudication. Simulated *Antoine* (June 7, 1993, unanimous Stevens opinion) places historical/functional justification on the immunity claimant; importance alone is insufficient. Current *Bogan* (March 3, 1998, unanimous Thomas opinion) distinguishes prospective departmental legislation from individualized administrative personnel decisions. It creates no general employment immunity and does not make an appointment judicial merely because formalized in an order. Absolute immunity, qualified immunity, constitutional merits, personal participation and remedy remain separate.

*McDonnell Douglas*, *Burdine*, *Aikens* and simulated *Hicks* (June 25, 1993) supply the actual discrimination-proof background. Hicks's controlling Scalia proposition, joined by Stone, O'Connor, Kennedy and Thomas, rejects both automatic liability from rejected explanations and a mandatory extra category of direct or independent evidence. Burdine's ultimate persuasion allocation remains. No universal equivalence of constitutional and Title VII claims follows; Title VII's exclusion is not silently undone. Simulated *Virginia* (June 26, 1996) requires a genuine important objective and substantial fit for a proven government sex classification, but a disputed discriminatory hiring decision first requires proving the challenged classification or motive. Inclusive recruitment is not automatically a discriminatory preference or a compelled constitutional program.

Most directly, *Crawford-El* (June 16, 1997, unanimous) rejects a heightened motive quantum and a categorical demand for the unavailable proof before necessary discovery. Its rule preserves specific nonconclusory support, early dispositive legal defenses, protective orders, tailoring and ordinary summary judgment. It orders neither unrestricted discovery nor a particular deposition; a general credibility attack is insufficient. The operative modules **Circumstantial proof of unconstitutional motive despite qualified immunity**, **Rejected employment explanations and circumstantial discrimination proof**, **No direct federal-agency Bivens damages action**, and **Ministerial court reporting and absolute judicial immunity** have the authorities and dates stated in the copied Standards. Current Clanton/Valot/Smith preserve actual proof and conduct-date notice; current Bogan supplies the directly relevant functional distinction. Any new DeCarlo holding must be refreshed before June 3 rather than importing its proposed treatment.

One supported path affirms final judgment because the supported nondiscriminatory qualifications/references and the actual limited contrary showing leave no genuine issue, and the Rule 56(f) request fails to identify how the requested inquiry could materially rebut it. This path must address the prima facie showing and distinct discovery request rather than accept defendants' denials as conclusive merely because judges made them. It cannot invent an additional direct-evidence burden or special judicial defendant exemption.

The competing path preserves the individual action and rejection of absolute immunity but vacates premature judgment for bounded discovery or ordinary reconsideration, because the reported statement about appointment, hiring pattern and comparative reasons require fair opportunity to test facts principally controlled by defendants. Its strongest objection is that no automatic discovery follows from pleading discrimination, the qualifications are substantially documented, recruiting a diverse pool is lawful, and the affidavit must satisfy the real Rule 56(f) requirements. A narrower remand may correct a legal evidence barrier without deciding that discrimination occurred or guaranteeing any requested deposition. An independent cause-of-action or immunity path must be supported under date-eligible law, not contemporary Bivens restrictions or a personal substitute framework.

No path supplies an appointment injunction, liability, backpay, damages, trial, open-ended inquiry into judges' work, or equal treatment of every defendant without actual grounds. The appointment took effect May 2, 1994; the alleged recruitment and selection conduct spans 1993–1994. Later decisions cannot retroactively furnish conduct-time notice, though Davis and Forrester predate it. A distinct merits failure and a finding that existing law was unclear must not be mislabeled as the same holding.

### Sources and readiness

[CAP complete opinion](https://static.case.law/f3d/123/html/1026-01.html), [local](../research/lower/duffy_v_wolle_cap.txt); [Public.Resource.Org](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/123/123.F3d.1026.96-3210.html), [local](../research/lower/duffy_v_wolle_source.txt). These sources quote the relevant affidavits, procedural request, vacancy terms and Rule 56(f), and reproduce the entire lower reasoning. Original depositions, complete joint appendix and EEO Plan are not claimed recovered. The reported record supports the presented legal and discovery issues. A contrary claim based on an unquoted EEO term, additional employment history or original filing omission requires targeted source review. Ready for independent modeling after the later-date law refresh, particularly any material DeCarlo rule.



<!-- frozen-source: OT_1997CHUNK9_NEUTRAL_WASTE_BEGGERLY.md -->

# CLEAN FROZEN neutral supplemental packet — Waste Management, Cabrales, Beggerly

Status: **READY for fresh neutral modeling of these three matters only, subject to the chronological refresh below.** No non-Stone commitment, proposed opinion coalition, historical same-matter Supreme Court rationale, or merits outcome is supplied. Despite this file's short name, its coverage includes Cabrales. Prepared from the authorized sanitized input, entering-law slice, neutral projection and antecedent primary sources. Exact copied source text and inherited/public-law snapshots are in [the companion](OT_1997CHUNK9_NEUTRAL_SUPPLEMENT_LAW.md); the companion is part of this frozen handoff.

## Common scope, participation, and chronology

All nine seated Justices are available: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. The neutral input and operator confirm no case-specific new-run nonparticipation. No historical recusal is inferred. This packet models nobody's position; its Justice tables identify evidence and questions a fresh modeling context must consider.

The inherited-law slice includes dated law through September 1997; its stale descriptive headers do not negate dated entries. The frozen current-term neutral projection supplies effective law through May 21, 1998. No proposed chronological neighbor is law. Before each event, the operator must give the fresh model any **actually completed** intervening public neutral law effective before May 27 (Waste Management), June 1 (Cabrales), or June 8 (Beggerly). Within the presently authorized projection, no waste-disposal or money-laundering-venue holding resolves these questions. Beach, April 21, directly informs Beggerly's statute-specific timing inquiry but does not establish a universal rule against equitable tolling. The public prior-term Brockamp decision, omitted as a standalone entry from the selected slice but cited by Beach, has been read and copied here; its preserved limits matter.

Questions below are analytical formulations of the authorized scope, not invented petition quotations. No merits briefs or joint appendix have been represented as read. The complete lower opinions were read, including their footnotes, the Beggerly dissent, and the Cabrales amendment. Reported facts remain at the lower court's procedural status. Supported published recitations suffice under Engine §14 for these material paths; no missing document establishes default, fraud, title, a stipulated contract term, or guilt. No new substantive reconstruction premise is adopted.

## 1. Waste Management, Inc. of Tennessee v. Metropolitan Government of Nashville and Davidson County

### Identity, vehicle, grant, and record

**Scheduled event:** May 27, 1998, an operator scheduling assignment under the user's authorization to review this lower-court matter. There is no assigned Supreme Court docket or historical merits citation. **Court below:** Sixth Circuit, Nos. 95-5863 and 95-5900, 130 F.3d 731, decided November 5, 1997; argued there August 6, 1996. Source: [complete lower opinion](https://static.case.law/f3d/130/html/0731-01.html), mechanically copied in the companion.

The district court permanently enjoined the disposal fee, declined to enjoin the routing provisions, and denied relief on the passenger-car/pickup ordinance. WMIT appealed the latter two rulings; Metro cross-appealed the fee injunction. The Sixth Circuit expressly exercised **28 U.S.C. §1292(a)(1)** jurisdiction over the injunction dispositions. It reversed denial of the routing injunction, affirmed the fee injunction, and vacated/remanded the passenger-vehicle/pickup ruling for takings and ripeness consideration. Do not describe this as an appeal from a single final judgment under §1291 or as a lower-court finding that a physical taking occurred.

The authorized Supreme Court scope consists of two preserved, ripe Commerce Clause issues: (1) whether compulsory routing to this municipally owned facility discriminates against interstate commerce; (2) whether the separate fee on disposal outside Metro's system is discriminatory or a valid compensatory charge. Supreme Court review of a case in the court of appeals is available under §1254(1); the remaining takings proceedings are not a §1257 state-finality obstacle. The grant does **not** include the passenger-car/pickup taking or its ripeness. No additional argument date, filing deadline, grant order, or resolution of the separate property component is invented.

The parties stipulated the facts relevant to this appeal. Preserve the following distinctions:

- Metro owns the waste-to-energy facility; Nashville Thermal Transfer Corporation operates it. NTTC supplies power to downtown buildings. Public ownership is an established fact; the record supplied does not establish the operator's ownership, profit distribution, nationality, procurement terms or a contract transferring commercial risk. A private corporate operator does not itself make the plant privately owned.
- The rules require licensing and approved disposal sites. Unless the Director specifically authorizes otherwise in writing, all residential solid waste collected within Metro must go to NTTC. Nonresidential waste need not go there unless allocated to meet the plant's operating need, reported as 6,300 tons per week. The quoted regulation requires monthly reporting, at-least-quarterly pro rata calculations using best available data, advance NTTC notice and advance notice of each collector's estimated share. The exact quoted qualifications are copied in the companion; do not collapse this into an unconditional requirement for all waste.
- The appellate narrative describes a $36-per-ton NTTC tipping fee and a separate $8-per-ton outside-system disposal fee. **Footnote 2 also reproduces an enacted increase to $44 per ton effective January 1, 1995**, with cubic-yard equivalents. Do not freeze $36 as the only enacted rate or use an unverified rate to calculate competitive losses. The outside-system fee is $2 per cubic yard or $8 per ton, imposed on waste generated/collected within Metro and disposed at a non-Metro facility within Metro or collected within Metro and disposed outside its boundaries. The lower court reports that NTTC and other Metro-owned sites are exempt.
- WMIT has a Davidson County transfer station and landfills in Benton County, Tennessee, and Logan County, Kentucky. BFI has no transfer station and disposes at its Rutherford County, Tennessee, landfill. Sanifill uses NTTC, WMIT's transfer station or BFI's landfill. Thus intrastate competitors also suffer exclusion or charges; actual interstate alternatives exist. That does not by itself answer discrimination classification.
- Metro asserts reliable fuel supply, environmental service and compliance with Tennessee's requirement to reduce by 25 percent the volume disposed in landfills/incinerators. The Sixth Circuit identified competitive tipping charges as an alternative and recorded Metro's concession that increased direct or contracted collection could secure a stream, though legislation and preparation would be needed. The court recorded both parties' acknowledgment of a nondiscriminatory generator/container tax alternative. These are the reported alternatives, not a new finding that every substitute is free, immediate or equally profitable.
- Metro supplied no evidence of a specific intrastate tax burden that the $8 charge compensated. That lower-record deficiency is distinct from a statutory declaration of cost recovery, and from a finding that Metro incurs no waste-management costs at all.

The lower court treated the routing and fee as discriminatory under its understanding of Carbone and Oregon Waste. It reasoned that reserving only part of the demand changes discrimination's extent, not its existence. That is the judgment under review; the fresh model must apply the **actual simulated** versions, whose public/private boundaries differ in legally important ways from an undifferentiated citation.

### Entering law and material forks

**Actual Carbone, May 16, 1994:** Kennedy, with Stevens, Scalia, Thomas and Ginsburg among the six controlling Justices, invalidated coerced private transactions reserved to a selected local **private** processor. Competing local exclusions do not cure that private-service discrimination. A tonnage guarantee and option to purchase did not make the service a present municipal purchase. The Court expressly left a materially different genuinely public system undecided. O'Connor supported the judgment only under Pike; Souter dissented on the municipal-service and insufficient-burden grounds, without categorical proprietary immunity.

**Actual SSC Corp., March 11, 1996:** Kennedy's unanimous opinion permitted a destination term for collection **and disposal actually purchased by the Town**; private ownership and contractor performance did not negate that purchased service. The Court separately applied Carbone to a coercive ordinance reserving other private business, unanimously, with O'Connor and Souter retaining their original classification objections. Investment, financial guarantees and contractual labels alone did not establish public ownership or transform independent purchases into government purchases. Its materially different public-system reservation survives.

**Actual Oregon Waste, April 4, 1994:** Thomas's opinion, joined by all currently seated Associates then serving, except Breyer who was not yet seated, requires an identified intrastate burden, rough approximation without excess, and substantially equivalent taxable events for compensation. General income taxation and revenue comparisons alone fail. It rejects a geographic surcharge at private facilities while leaving government-owned landfill charges and materially different proven costs undecided. Once discrimination is established, the legitimate-purpose/adequate-nondiscriminatory-alternatives inquiry governs; Pike cannot replace it. None of these decisions requires perfect certainty or disproving every imaginable substitute.

The alternatives are meaningful and component-specific:

1. **Apply the discrimination rule to coercive public-facility routing.** Treat protection of this local processing demand, despite public title, as the relevant geographic service preference. Confront the express Carbone/SSC reservation: this would resolve an open extension, not mechanically apply a prior public-system holding. Evaluate the actually recorded environmental purposes and feasible neutral alternatives. If the rule fails, preserve lawful licensing and independently valid public purchases; no substitute program is judicially chosen.
2. **Distinguish a genuine public service and apply Pike.** Explain why public ownership and the program's actual incidence matter even with a separate operator and burdens on interstate providers. The strongest objection is that a sovereign routing command still compels independent private purchases; calling it a service or invoking environmental goals does not establish market-participant immunity. Pike requires a record-based account of interstate burden and local benefit, not mere opposition to a higher price. This path can support upholding the route or a bounded remand if the applicable inquiry was not adequately resolved below. It cannot invent operator economics or an unproved procurement arrangement.
3. **Separate the fee from the routing classification.** The charge may be a discriminatory penalty on use of competing services even if the public route survives; alternatively a materially evenhanded public-system charge may need its own burden analysis. A compensatory-tax defense must meet Oregon Waste's three requirements; the reported absence of an identified equivalent burden is a serious barrier. Neither a public label nor revenue need establishes equivalence. An alternative fee rationale must fit the enacted incidence and existing record; no new matched-cost finding or new assessment is supplied.

### Justice-specific source bearings, without commitments

| Justice | Date-eligible own writing or actual join | Two-path question and join barrier |
|---|---|---|
| Stevens | Joins Kennedy's actual Carbone; Thomas's actual Oregon Waste; unanimous SSC | Is public ownership a legally material difference from the private reservation he invalidated, or does compulsory market exclusion carry the same defect? Either answer must preserve genuine purchases and treat the separate fee's missing equivalent burden. |
| O'Connor | Actual Carbone concurrence under Pike; joins Oregon Waste; SSC application with express reservation | Her rejection of Carbone's geographic classification supports a public-service/Pike path, but her conclusion of excessive private-route burden prevents treating municipal purpose as sufficient. SSC requires respecting the existing private-service rule while resolving the reserved public case independently. |
| Scalia | Joins actual Carbone and Oregon Waste; unanimous SSC | The source-supported fork is the reserved public category versus extension of the private-service rule. These actual joins cannot be replaced by an assumed wholesale rejection of dormant-commerce doctrine. Any narrower result must distinguish routing from compensation. |
| Kennedy | Authors actual Carbone and unanimous SSC | He must identify the transaction the Town buys versus the private transaction it compels. Public ownership may support a new distinction, but ownership alone does not establish that Metro purchases all covered disposal. His express public-system reservation must receive substantive treatment. |
| Souter | Actual Carbone dissent; joins Oregon Waste; unanimous SSC with classification reservation | Municipal service and financing support nondiscriminatory/Pike treatment; coercion, competitive exclusion and adequate neutral alternatives support the contrary extension. His Carbone dissent rejected categorical proprietary immunity, and his SSC join requires applying actual precedent at its settled scope. |
| Thomas | Authors actual Oregon Waste; joins actual Carbone and SSC | Public-facility reservation permits a distinct classification inquiry, while his compensation rule demands an actual equivalent burden, not total revenue need. A routing distinction does not silently dispense with the separate fee analysis. |
| Ginsburg | Joins actual Carbone, Oregon Waste and SSC | Public ownership may distinguish a public program from protected private processing, but the source-backed alternative is scrutiny of the same compulsory demand reservation. Her three joins require keeping public capacity, purchased performance and cost compensation separate. |
| Breyer | Joins actual unanimous SSC, both components | Genuine public purchasing supports Metro within the service actually bought; the separate compulsory private route was invalid despite financing objectives. SSC reserved a materially different public system. This source does not determine his view of that open category or supply a missing cost finding. |

**Remedy limits:** Decide or remand only the two reviewed injunction components. A narrower outcome can distinguish them. The separate takings/ripeness remand remains outside the grant. No damages, refund, breach, debt, guaranteed competitor profits, mandatory tax, new operating contract or title transfer follows from either Commerce Clause answer.

## 2. United States v. Cabrales

### Identity, posture, questions, and decisive amendment

**No. 97-643; argued April 29, 1998; scheduled event June 1, 1998.** Eighth Circuit No. 96-3080WM, 109 F.3d 471 (March 24, 1997), as amended at 115 F.3d 621 (June 12, 1997). [Original lower opinion](https://static.case.law/f3d/109/html/0471-01.html) and [amending order](https://static.case.law/f3d/115/html/0621-01.html) are copied in full. The Government appealed pretrial dismissal of Counts II and III for improper Missouri venue; the court of appeals affirmed. The district court had denied dismissal of Count I, conspiracy. The question here is venue for the two substantive laundering charges, not sufficiency, guilt, suppression or sentencing.

**Material source correction:** The original opinion's footnote 2 said Missouri overt acts supported conspiracy venue. On Cabrales's motion, the panel expressly removed that explanation so that no incorrect inference would affect the other prosecution. The operative amended footnote says only that the district court did not dismiss Count I and that Count I is not part of this appeal. The full original is reproduced only with the amendment alongside it. **Do not treat Missouri overt acts, valid conspiracy venue, or any conspiracy merits finding as established.**

Count I alleges §371 conspiracy to violate §1956(a)(1)(B)(ii), the reporting-avoidance branch. Count II charges conducting a financial transaction under that branch; Count III charges a monetary transaction in criminally derived property worth more than $10,000 under §1957. The sanitized objective account supplies a January 1991 Florida bank deposit of $40,000 and four $9,500 withdrawals within a week. The lower opinion supplies the material geographic facts: all charged banking transactions occurred in Florida; the proceeds are traceable to Missouri drug sales. The Government does not dispute that the acts Cabrales performed constituting those laundering transactions occurred outside Missouri. It relies on the Missouri predicate crime as making laundering a continuing offense. **It does not allege she transported the funds from Missouri to Florida.** Counts II–III identify no act of hers in Missouri. No aiding-and-abetting charge or acts are added to cure the indictment.

The authorized analytical question is whether the place of the proceeds-producing crime independently supplies venue for these substantive laundering counts although the charged financial dealings began, continued and ended in Florida. The Government's argument treats the illicit source, an indispensable statutory ingredient, as part of a multistate offense; Cabrales distinguishes the character of the property from where the conduct prohibited by these statutes occurred.

### Entering law and alternatives

Article III, §2, cl.3, the Sixth Amendment and Rule 18 locate criminal trial where the charged crime was committed. The lower opinion quotes the operative statutory language and Anderson, 328 U.S. 699, 703 (1946), tying the locus to the nature of the crime and the acts constituting it. The full 1994 official §3237 source has been read and copied; its amendments establish that the pertinent language predates January 1991. Subsection (a) covers an offense begun in one district and completed in another, or committed in more than one; its separate paragraph addresses offenses involving mails, interstate/foreign transportation or importation. An ordinary interstate-commerce connection in a financial-transaction definition is not automatically proof that this defendant transported these funds or committed conduct in each place they previously passed. The tax-election subsection is distinct and irrelevant to the charged offenses.

**Actual United States v. Thomas, May 19, 1997, Ginsburg Part III, unanimous:** Venue lay where the charged, authorized interstate transportation of obscene images reached the recipient. The Court expressly reserved remote effects alone, every district crossed by signals, unknown viewers and passive pages. Destination knowledge supported that application without becoming a universal extra element. This is directly relevant entered venue law absent from the source input's short list. It requires examining the charged conduct, while leaving room for genuine multistate offenses.

**Actual Gaudin, June 19, 1995, Scalia, unanimous:** A statutory materiality element belongs to the jury under the specified criminal statute. It does not hold that every circumstance element identifies a place of criminal conduct, nor decide this pretrial statutory venue question. Respect for all elements is necessary on either path; it is not an automatic geographic answer.

1. **Conduct-location path:** Treat the Missouri proceeds as a necessary attribute of the property and Florida transactions as the prohibited conduct for these counts. The strongest objection is the Government's argument that removing the proceeds-producing crime would remove the laundering offense altogether. Explain why element status does not invariably relocate the offense. Affirm dismissal of Counts II–III for Missouri venue, without an acquittal or decision about lawful prosecution elsewhere, Count I, transportation, or a different indictment.
2. **Integrated-offense path:** Treat the particular statutory offense as beginning with the Missouri criminal production of proceeds and ending with the Florida transactions. The strongest objection is that this charges Cabrales's financial transactions, not the predicate drug conduct, and alleges no transport by her; a general laundering scheme cannot substitute for statutory language. Explain the limiting principle that prevents venue wherever any historical circumstance arose. If this construction is accepted, reverse the venue dismissal and permit further proceedings on the two counts, making no guilt finding.
3. **Narrow distinction without a universal rule:** Resolve this indictment while reserving a defendant who committed the predicate crime, transported the proceeds, or is properly charged with a broader scheme. Beddow, 957 F.2d 1330, 1336 (6th Cir. 1992), as discussed and quoted below, involved predicate involvement and transport; Sax, 39 F.3d 1380, 1390 (7th Cir. 1994), supported a broader origin theory which the Eighth Circuit rejected to that extent. Their conflict supplies the real alternative; it does not establish missing Cabrales acts. The panel's rehearing correction forecloses relying on its former conspiracy footnote to manufacture a shortcut.

### Justice-specific source bearings, without commitments

| Justice | Own date-eligible authority | Two-path comparison required |
|---|---|---|
| Stevens | Joins actual Thomas Part III and Gaudin | Whether the illicit-origin ingredient gives the offense a Missouri beginning or merely describes the Florida transaction's object; his actual Thomas join ties venue to charged transportation, while preserving genuine continuing crimes. |
| O'Connor | Same actual joins | Must compare an actual interstate transportation offense with a transaction involving proceeds from another district; neither effects alone nor a categorical ban on multistate venue follows from her sources. |
| Scalia | Authors actual Gaudin; joins Thomas Part III | Determine the enacted offense's conduct and circumstances, then its place; element fidelity can support either statutory construction but does not make every element a geographic act or permit an uncharged scheme. |
| Kennedy | Joins actual Thomas Part III and Gaudin | The source-backed fork is actual completion in the recipient district versus prior crime as part of laundering itself; a broader route needs a statutory and record-based limiting principle. |
| Souter | Same actual joins | Test whether the Government's integrated account describes this charged offense or adds predicate/transport participation not alleged; preserve lawful continuing-offense venue without assuming conspiracy venue. |
| Thomas | Same actual joins | Every actual element must retain its force; nevertheless Gaudin allocates factfinding rather than defining venue. His Thomas join supports actual delivery as sufficient, not all antecedent or downstream connections. |
| Ginsburg | Authors actual Thomas Part III; joins Gaudin | Her writing permits venue where authorized transportation ends and expressly limits remote-effect reasoning. She must determine whether these different statutes make the prior Missouri conduct part of this offense or leave all charged conduct in Florida. |
| Breyer | Joins actual Thomas Part III and Gaudin | Compare the functional unity of the offense with statutory and geographical boundaries in Thomas; administrative convenience and linked criminal activity alone are not a source-backed substitute for those boundaries. |

These common prior joins are actual issue-specific evidence, not eight inferred identical commitments. No historical Cabrales author, join or outcome is provided.

## 3. United States v. Beggerly

### Identity, record, and separate routes

**No. 97-731; argued April 27, 1998; scheduled event June 8, 1998.** Fifth Circuit No. 95-60625, 114 F.3d 484, decided May 29, 1997, corrected on rehearing July 28, 1997. [Full lower opinion and Garza dissent](https://static.case.law/f3d/114/html/0484-01.html), copied in the companion. The district court dismissed under Rules 12(b)(1)/(6), denied the claimants' summary-judgment cross-motion and leave to amend to assert Quiet Title/Tucker Act jurisdiction. The court of appeals reversed, vacated the 1982 consent judgment as to these claimants, treated the Quiet Title Act as an alternative basis, and directed title to be quieted for them. Review must distinguish jurisdiction, entitlement to extraordinary reopening, the time bar, title merits, and remedy.

The lower opinion reports a 1950 tax-sale purchase of part of Horn Island; park legislation in 1971; negotiations begun in 1972; a 1975 contract for $156,500 canceled when the Government asserted it had issued no patent. The Government brought its quiet-title action in 1979. During discovery its officials reportedly searched public land records and formally represented that no private disposition existed. In 1982 the parties settled: $208,175.87 to the claimants and title quieted to the United States. These payment amounts and chronology are distinct.

The claimants continued searching, including records in several States, correspondence and information requests; a genealogical researcher they hired located the Boudreau document in the National Archives in 1991. It was an English translation of a 1781 Spanish grant. The lower majority accepted it as the available copy after destruction of the Spanish archives and relied on Professor Hans Baade's affidavit about Spanish law. It rejected the Government's contention that the paper was only a patent application and treated an early land commissioner's refusal to accept it as nonconclusive. **Those are appellate conclusions contested by the Government and by the dissent's objection to deciding title on an incomplete record; they are not newly stipulated historical facts or an independently adjudicated good chain of title.**

The new suit was filed June 1, 1994. The lower majority described Government misrepresentation, negligence or mistake and found the traditional independent-action conditions satisfied. It did not establish intentional concealment simply by identifying a document later found in government archives. The distinction between an unsuccessful search, an inaccurate discovery representation, intentional concealment, and obstruction of a claimant's own reasonable search remains material. The packet supplies no affirmative bad-faith finding beyond the lower court's stated conclusions.

The lower majority found knowledge of the Government's adverse claim by 1976, when negotiations ceased; the sanitized account identifies knowledge by 1979 at the latest. Both precede the new suit by more than twelve years. Do not confuse first knowledge of the adverse claim with later discovery of evidence to defeat it. The majority tolled from the original search during the quiet-title litigation until the 1991 discovery. The Government's earlier suit and the settlement are notice of its claim; whether misrepresentation warrants equitable suspension is a different question.

### Law entering the case

The 1998 version of Rule 60(b) preserves an independent action, apart from its ordinary motion remedies; the complete lower opinion quotes that savings language and its five traditional requirements: a judgment inequitable to enforce; a good defense; fraud, accident or mistake preventing use of it; absence of the claimant's fault/negligence; and absence of an adequate legal remedy. The lower court derives these from Bankers Mortgage, 423 F.2d 73, and National Surety, 120 F. 593, 599. This is an exceptional equitable route, not a means of converting every later-discovered item into an unrestricted second case. The neutral input identifies extraordinary relief to prevent a grave miscarriage of justice as the proposed governing threshold; the model must explain its application through established equitable principles rather than assume a finding satisfying it.

The lower opinion reports **QTA §2409a(g)'s twelve-year period**, accruing when the claimant knows or should know of the United States' adverse claim. It reports §2409a(b)'s choice, after an adverse title judgment, to deliver possession or retain it and pay just compensation. Full historical statutory pages were not recovered despite direct official-source attempts. The handoff relies on these authoritative reported operative provisions under §14, rather than pretending to quote the complete statute or its unrelated exceptions. If a model needs another unquoted qualification to decide the case, it must obtain it before adopting that ground. No such missing clause is used by the paths below.

**Actual Morgan Stanley (May 23, 1994) and Plaut (April 18, 1995)** uphold the expressly bounded §27A(b) congressional reinstatement procedure while retaining independent judicial eligibility, merits and remedy decisions. Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg joined both controlling opinions; Breyer joined Plaut. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas dissented in both; O'Connor did not participate in Morgan Stanley and joined Scalia's Plaut dissent. These actual cases foreclose importing historical Plaut's absolute Article III rule as controlling law. They equally do **not** turn all completed settlements into discretionary fairness reviews. The difference between legislatively prescribed reopening and longstanding judicial correction remains essential.

**Actual Kokkonen (May 16, 1994), Scalia, unanimous among those then seated:** Mere federal dismissal does not supply jurisdiction to enforce a separate settlement contract. Incorporation/retention can support enforcement of the court's own order within lawful authority. Rule 60 reopening was expressly reserved. Thus the Government's limited-jurisdiction argument deserves consideration, but Kokkonen is not already a holding that a court cannot entertain an independent attack on its own allegedly corrupted judgment.

**Actual Peacock (February 21, 1996), Thomas:** Eight Justices, including all Associates other than Stevens, rejected ancillary jurisdiction for a new action imposing an existing judgment debtor's debt on another person. Authority to effectuate a decree remains distinct from creating new substantive liability. This supports careful differentiation between undoing the original decree and obtaining new title or compensation relief, not automatic denial of every equitable attack.

**Actual Nordic Village (February 25, 1992), Scalia, and Department of Energy v. Ohio (April 21, 1992), Souter:** Federal consent must cover the particular remedy; general jurisdiction, an equitable label, or a narrower waiver does not establish an affirmative monetary obligation. Their actual joins and limits are copied in the companion. They do not decide whether the Government's own original title suit carries continuing jurisdiction to correct its judgment, nor whether a timely QTA claim meets its express waiver.

**Irwin, 498 U.S. 89 (1990), official predivergence opinion, fully read:** O'Connor, Scalia and Kennedy joined the Court's rebuttable presumption that limitations in suits against the Government can be equitably tolled after an express waiver; that presumption does not create a waiver or automatically extend every deadline. Ordinary attorney neglect did not suffice; misleading conduct inducing expiration was a recognized example, with diligence required and no more favorable rule against the Government than private defendants. Stevens expressly agreed with tolling availability and its failure on that record, dissenting on the notice trigger. Souter did **not** participate. Do not attribute an Irwin join to him, Thomas, Ginsburg or Breyer.

**Actual Brockamp, February 18, 1997, Breyer, unanimous on the timing rules:** Assuming Irwin applies, the integrated §6511 filing/allowance/payment scheme rebutted nonstatutory incapacity tolling. Specific exceptions alone and administrative scale alone were not independent grounds. The Court reserved fraud, affirmative governmental inducement and separate recoupment. **Actual Beach, April 21, 1998, unanimous:** The particular §1635(f) text expires the rescission right, subject to its stated agency extension, and cannot be revived by a defensive label. Neither decision classifies every deadline as jurisdictional or decides the QTA. Beggerly requires examination of this twelve-year notice-triggered scheme and alleged interference, not transplantation of a tax or lending outcome.

### Material alternatives and remedies

1. **Same-court corrective jurisdiction but extraordinary relief fails.** Distinguish reopening the Government's own decree from a new independent demand for money, while finding that the alleged search/disclosure failure and subsequent discovery do not satisfy the narrow equitable threshold. The strongest counterargument is official misinformation inducing settlement despite sustained claimant diligence. A rejection must address that causal account without inventing bad faith or blaming claimants for a search they reasonably could not complete. This narrower route need not decide every sovereign-consent issue if its posture permits that treatment.
2. **Corrective jurisdiction and sufficient extraordinary circumstances.** Treat the reported misleading representation, unavailable defense and persistent search as meeting the traditional conditions. The strongest barriers are the final settlement, other remedies/timeliness, and the distinction between material evidence later found and conduct preventing fair presentation. Even if reopening is justified, it does not compel an appellate title judgment. A remand for contested grant validity, title chain and remaining defenses is a separate, narrower remedial choice.
3. **Independent jurisdiction/consent required for the new relief.** Distinguish court power to correct its decree from a renewed QTA title action or affirmative compensation claim; apply the actual waiver precedents to the remedy sought. The opposing argument is that allowing the Government to invoke jurisdiction, obtain judgment through a material error, and then foreclose correction undermines the court's authority over its own process. Do not equate continuing subject-matter jurisdiction with consent to every monetary award, or read Kokkonen's reserved reopening question as settled.
4. **QTA timing either defeats or permits the alternative title route.** One path treats twelve years from actual/constructive knowledge as the enacted balance already allowing time to investigate, and rejects searching until favorable evidence appears as an extra accrual rule. The opposing path applies Irwin's presumption to proven adversary inducement or obstruction and diligence, distinguishing Brockamp's expressly reserved governmental-misconduct question. A decision must state whether it finds statutory rebuttal, inadequate equitable facts, or tolling on established facts; these are not interchangeable. Neither general equity nor a still-valid settlement can simply disappear from this analysis.
5. **Even with jurisdiction and reopening, title may require remand.** Garza's dissent separately objects that the majority decided the cross-motion, grant validity and title on an incomplete record. The Court can reject that objection on an adequately established record or remand for the necessary adjudication. It cannot treat the claimant's affidavit and a found translation as automatic proof of an unbroken title chain or turn a preliminary legal victory into an unspecified damages award.

### Justice-specific source bearings, without commitments

| Justice | Own date-eligible writing or actual published position | Two-path comparison and barrier |
|---|---|---|
| Stevens | Actual Morgan Stanley/Plaut majority joins; Kokkonen join; Irwin separate; Brockamp and Beach joins | Lawful bounded reopening and equitable tolling remain available in principle, but their conditions and statutory rebuttal matter. His Irwin opinion expressly rejected tolling on inadequate facts. Distinguish a misleading representation causing lost rights from evidence discovered after a voluntary settlement; do not equate reopening with title. |
| O'Connor | Irwin majority join; actual Kokkonen, Nordic Village, Peacock, Brockamp and Beach joins; Plaut dissent | Sovereign consent and finality support caution, but she joined Irwin's rebuttable presumption and actual cases reserve judicial correction. A rejection of legislative reopening is not a rejection of traditional independent actions. Test the particular remedy and whether misconduct meets a preserved exception. |
| Scalia | Authors actual Kokkonen/Nordic Village and Plaut dissent; Irwin majority join; Brockamp/Beach joins | Textual waiver limits and conclusive judgments constrain the route, while his own sources preserve proper decree control and conditional tolling. He must confront whether this is correction of the court's judgment or a new unconsented obligation; historical Plaut cannot replace the actual controlling decision. |
| Kennedy | Actual Plaut dissent and Kokkonen/Nordic Village/Peacock joins; Irwin majority; Brockamp/Beach | Distinguish impermissibly broad reopening from an established judicial equitable remedy; compare the long notice-based period to induced inability to assert a claim. The lower majority's equity characterization does not itself establish waiver, while Irwin prevents a blanket no-equity assumption. |
| Souter | Actual Morgan Stanley/Plaut and Kokkonen/Peacock joins; authors DOE v. Ohio; Brockamp/Beach joins | His own remedy-specific immunity analysis is a barrier to new monetary liability, but his bounded-reopening joins permit independent judicial adjudication where law authorizes it. He did not sit in Irwin. Determine whether fraud/mistake, diligence and a lawful statutory route actually exist rather than importing either broad finality or broad fairness. |
| Thomas | Actual Plaut dissent; Kokkonen/Nordic Village joins; authors Peacock; Brockamp/Beach joins | Peacock distinguishes enforcement from a new liability action but does not decide correction of the same decree. Ask whether the requested title/compensation is ancillary correction or new substantive relief; preserve the exact QTA trigger and do not make the word equitable a waiver. |
| Ginsburg | Actual Morgan Stanley/Plaut, Kokkonen and Peacock joins; Brockamp/Beach joins | Bounded independent adjudication and strict jurisdiction can coexist. A genuine defect in obtaining the original decree supports a corrective route; new title relief and a late lawsuit still require their own authority. Brockamp reserved government inducement and supplies no automatic bar here. |
| Breyer | Actual Plaut and Peacock joins; authors actual Brockamp; Beach join | His statute-specific treatment of timing supports examining the full operation of the QTA rather than using a universal sovereign deadline rule. Extraordinary inability alone did not overcome §6511, but the opinion reserved government inducement. Separate that issue from whether the existing record permits an appellate title judgment. |

**Remedy boundaries:** If the title direction survives, the lower court's QTA §2409a(b) route permits the Government to retain possession upon just compensation rather than necessarily ejecting it; the $208,175.87 already paid must be accounted for. The lower opinion states that the district court lacked Tucker Act jurisdiction over an inverse-condemnation claim exceeding $10,000; no new compensation jurisdiction is inferred. If the appellate judgment is reversed or vacated, specify whether the 1982 consent judgment remains operative and which, if any, further proceedings remain available. No general governmental archive-search immunity, automatic fraud finding, guaranteed hearing, final valuation or universal rule about all settled judgments follows.

## Source and completion receipt

The companion mechanically copies the selected inherited holding sections, the effective Beach public projection, the actual public Carbone/Plaut/SSC/Brockamp entries, every lower opinion used, the complete official Irwin opinion and the official §3237 pages. Copied source bytes are identified by SHA256. The Cabrales June 12 amendment is authoritative over its original explanatory footnote. Source text was copied by script; no long statutory or opinion passage was retyped to create supposed primary text.

All three lower decisions and the amendment were read completely. Prior public entries were read only for specific relevant positions. No same-matter Supreme Court decision was opened for these three. The supplied sanitized objective recitations establish dates, scope and additional procedural facts, without supplying historical Supreme Court outcomes. Lower-court reasoning is evidence of the dispute, not controlling simulated Supreme Court law. Failed official historical code retrieval is disclosed above and no HTML error page is cited as authority.

This packet is frozen for handoff. No commitments or merits choices have been made. The only live prerequisite is the operator's ordinary update for actually effective intervening public law before each later event; completion of this preflight creates none.

## Inherited law: C & A Carbone

Source: `terms/OT1997/entering-law/OT_1997CHUNK9.md`

Source SHA256: `0ccf978132798bdec6ec2299d0b2287843c0e61cc31f525d54c154b1909fe088`

BEGIN EXACT COPIED TEXT

#### C & A Carbone, Inc. v. Town of Clarkstown, 511 U.S. 383

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-1402.

**Decided:** 1994-05-16.

##### Compulsory use of this local private processor discriminates in the service market

**Holding:** A municipality discriminates against interstate commerce when it compels private waste transactions to use a selected local private processing service and excludes competing interstate services, even though competing local processors are excluded too and the material may later leave the State. This build-and-operate financing arrangement, including the Town's tonnage guarantee and option to purchase later, does not turn the present regulatory command into a purchase of disposal for the Town's own waste; the Court decides no materially different genuinely public system.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Thomas and Ginsburg; six Justices adopt the discrimination classification and its stated private-service boundary. O'Connor agrees only with the judgment on a distinct Pike ground.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Dean Milk Co. v. Madison, 340 U.S. 349 (1951): applied to compulsory local processing despite burdens on other local competitors.
- Fort Gratiot Sanitary Landfill v. Michigan Department of Natural Resources, 504 U.S. 353 (1992): applied to geographic private-service reservation and the distinction between planning and proprietary conduct.
- New Energy Co. of Indiana v. Limbach, 486 U.S. 269 (1988): applied; discrimination does not require a minimum number of favored businesses.
- Exxon Corp. v. Governor of Maryland, 437 U.S. 117 (1978): distinguished; this ordinance reserves a processing market rather than merely changing its business structure without a local service preference.
- Building & Construction Trades Council v. Associated Builders & Contractors (Boston Harbor), 507 U.S. 218 (1993): preserved for genuine proprietary project purchases; its labor-preemption holding does not immunize coercive regulation of unrelated private transactions.

##### Sanitary service and financing do not justify this discriminatory route

**Holding:** A discriminatory local processing requirement can survive only if the municipality establishes a legitimate local purpose that reasonable nondiscriminatory alternatives cannot adequately serve. Clarkstown's interests in safe, reliable disposal and financing the station do not justify the challenged compulsory private-service reservation on this record; neutral safety regulation and lawful nondiscriminatory financing remain available, without a new proof quantum, perfect-certainty demand, or requirement to disprove every imaginable alternative.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Thomas and Ginsburg; six Justices adopt this application of the discrimination rule. No independent Pike holding is adopted by that coalition.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Chemical Waste Management v. Hunt, 504 U.S. 334 (1992): applied for the legitimate-purpose and adequate-nondiscriminatory-alternatives inquiry, with its proof and remedial limits preserved.
- Oregon Waste Systems v. Department of Environmental Quality, April 4, 1994: applied to keep discrimination, justification and public/private capacity distinct; no compensatory-tax issue is decided here.
- Philadelphia v. New Jersey, 437 U.S. 617 (1978): applied to the Commerce Clause's protection of waste commerce despite valid environmental objectives.
- Pike v. Bruce Church, Inc., 397 U.S. 137 (1970): reserved for genuinely evenhanded incidental burdens; it supplies O'Connor's separate ground, not an independent holding of the Court.

**Limits and questions not reached:** A genuinely public system, ordinary government purchases and independent valid zoning, health, permitting, recycling, dumping and financing rules are not decided. O'Connor's Pike ground has one vote and is no independent Court holding. The ordinance's severability remains applicable; no contract rescission, refund, damages or substitute financing method is ordered.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The state enforcement judgment is reversed and remanded, 7–2. Set aside the challenged compulsory route against Carbone and adjust the injunction consistently, preserving independent valid rules and severability. No damages, refund, unconditional operating injunction, contract rescission or substitute financing method is ordered.



END EXACT COPIED TEXT


## Inherited law: Oregon Waste Systems

Source: `terms/OT1997/entering-law/OT_1997CHUNK9.md`

Source SHA256: `0ccf978132798bdec6ec2299d0b2287843c0e61cc31f525d54c154b1909fe088`

BEGIN EXACT COPIED TEXT

#### Oregon Waste Systems, Inc. v. Department of Environmental Quality and Columbia Resource Co. v. Environmental Quality Commission, 511 U.S. 93

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 93-70 and 93-108.

**Decided:** 1994-04-04.

##### An express waste-origin surcharge triggers the discrimination rule

**Holding:** A State discriminates against interstate commerce when it imposes a higher governmental disposal charge on otherwise comparable waste because it was generated outside the State; a small amount or environmental objective does not remove that express geographic preference. Such a differential can survive only if the State establishes a legitimate local purpose that reasonable nondiscriminatory alternatives cannot adequately serve; ordinary Pike balancing of evenhanded incidental burdens does not govern this classification.
