**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg; eight Justices directly adopt this proposition and its application.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Philadelphia v. New Jersey, 437 U.S. 617 (1978): applied to geographic discrimination in waste commerce.
- Chemical Waste Management, Inc. v. Hunt, 504 U.S. 334 (1992): applied to an origin-based disposal surcharge and its purpose-and-alternatives burden; its unpressed compensation theory remains distinct.
- Fort Gratiot Sanitary Landfill, Inc. v. Michigan Department of Natural Resources, 504 U.S. 353 (1992): applied to regulation of private landfills.
- Wyoming v. Oklahoma, 502 U.S. 437 (1992): its express-geographic-discrimination rule is preserved and applied.
- Pike v. Bruce Church, Inc., 397 U.S. 137 (1970): distinguished as governing evenhanded regulation with incidental burdens.

##### General income taxes do not establish a compensatory disposal tax

**Holding:** A compensatory-tax defense requires an identified intrastate burden, an interstate charge that roughly approximates and does not exceed that burden, and substantially equivalent taxable events capable of operating as mutually exclusive proxies. Oregon's reliance on residents' general income-tax contributions fails because income generation and disposal of waste are not substantially equivalent events; even a quantified revenue comparison would not cure that independently sufficient defect, and the statutory cost command is not proof of a matching burden.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg; eight Justices directly adopt this proposition and its application.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Maryland v. Louisiana, 451 U.S. 725 (1981): applied to require identification of the actual domestic burden being compensated.
- Henneford v. Silas Mason Co., 300 U.S. 577 (1937): preserved for genuinely complementary taxes on substantially equivalent events; general taxation is not such a proxy.
- Armco Inc. v. Hardesty, 467 U.S. 638 (1984): applied to the independently fatal absence of substantially equivalent taxable events.

##### Conservation and cost-recovery labels do not justify this private-facility differential

**Holding:** Oregon's conservation purpose, comprehensive regulatory program and statutory direction to recover otherwise-unpaid costs do not establish that this origin differential satisfies the discrimination rule: the record supplies no demonstrated origin-specific added cost that defeats reasonable nondiscriminatory alternatives. Because the facilities are private, regulation does not make this a proprietary charge by a government owner, and a user-fee analogy cannot eliminate nondiscrimination as a separate requirement; the Court leaves government-owned landfill charges and materially different proven cost justifications undecided.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg; eight Justices directly adopt this proposition and its application.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Chemical Waste Management v. Hunt: applied to preserve legitimate environmental aims while requiring an adequate nondiscriminatory-alternatives showing.
- Sporhase v. Nebraska ex rel. Douglas, 458 U.S. 941 (1982): distinguished; conservation concerns do not validate this origin preference at private landfills.
- Evansville-Vanderburgh Airport Authority District v. Delta Airlines, Inc., 405 U.S. 707 (1972): reasonable approximation does not displace its independent nondiscrimination condition.
- Northwest Airlines, Inc. v. County of Kent, January 24, 1994: its distinct nondiscrimination requirement is applied; unlike that record, this fee expressly classifies by interstate origin.
- Fort Gratiot Sanitary Landfill v. Michigan Department of Natural Resources: applied to distinguish private-facility regulation from state market participation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The statutory direction to recover costs is not a lower-court finding of numerical equivalence. The Court supplies no contrary numerical finding, no universal bar to cost recovery and no rule for government-owned landfills. Materially different proven origin-specific costs remain subject to the governing justification requirement.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Both matters are reversed and remanded for relief from the origin differential through the existing state review proceeding. No replacement rate, refund entitlement or amount, alteration of the separately unchallenged charge, required waste shipment or reinstatement of severed state-law provisions is ordered.



END EXACT COPIED TEXT


## Inherited law: SSC Corp.

Source: `terms/OT1997/entering-law/OT_1997CHUNK9.md`

Source SHA256: `0ccf978132798bdec6ec2299d0b2287843c0e61cc31f525d54c154b1909fe088`

BEGIN EXACT COPIED TEXT

#### SSC Corp. v. Town of Smithtown, judgment below 66 F.3d 502

**Docket or dockets:** Petition No. 95-782 and cross-petition No. 95-1015 identify the underlying filings; no separate Supreme Court docket assigned.

**Decided:** March 11, 1996.

##### The Town may specify disposal as part of the service it purchases

**Holding:** A municipality purchasing residential waste collection and disposal may specify the disposal facility as part of that purchased performance. Payment through a hauler, separate price items and use of a privately owned facility do not make the disposal component an unrelated downstream transaction. The rule reaches the service actually bought; contractual form or participation in another market alone supplies no authority over unrelated business.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Kennedy), unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The rule does not reach unpaid unrelated later transactions or materially different public waste systems. The government need not own the facility or employ every performer; investment, contractual form or capacity commitments alone do not turn independent customers' purchases into government purchases.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** White's purchased-performance and privity principles are applied; Reeves's participant distinction remains within its scope. South-Central Timber's downstream plurality is distinguished without enlarging its authority. Carbone's reservation of genuine purchasing receives this specific application; Boston Harbor retains its separate labor-preemption scope.

##### The separate compulsory route remains invalid under Carbone

**Holding:** The Town may not use the challenged ordinance to reserve independently arranged private waste-processing transactions to the designated local private operator. Under Carbone, that discriminatory reservation requires a legitimate local purpose that reasonable nondiscriminatory alternatives cannot adequately serve. Smithtown's financing commitments and sanitation interests do not establish that justification on this record.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Kennedy), unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** O'Connor and Souter join the application without withdrawing their prior objections to Carbone's classification. Criminal penalties identify compulsion here, but are not the full test. The exclusions for recyclables and unacceptable waste do not cure the covered private-demand restriction. State encouragement and financial commitments do not constitute congressional authorization; no perfect-certainty requirement or obligation to disprove every imaginable alternative is imposed.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm both components: the purchased destination term survives the constitutional challenge and the ordinance remains invalid. Only properly pending and legally available contract/payment proceedings remain. No breach, diversion, debt, withheld-payment award, damages, rescission, automatic trial or replacement program is established. Antitrust immunity, CERCLA liability and a general subcontract rule remain undecided.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Carbone's private-processing rule and justification requirement are applied without change, with Oregon Waste's distinction between discrimination and justification intact. West Lynn's scrutiny of linked arrangements does not make the Town's genuine purchases identical to the ordinance's compulsion. Pike remains available for genuinely evenhanded incidental burdens; no separate Pike holding is made here.



END EXACT COPIED TEXT


## Inherited law: Morgan Stanley

Source: `terms/OT1997/entering-law/OT_1997CHUNK9.md`

Source SHA256: `0ccf978132798bdec6ec2299d0b2287843c0e61cc31f525d54c154b1909fe088`

BEGIN EXACT COPIED TEXT

#### Morgan Stanley & Co. v. Pacific Mutual Life Insurance Co., 511 U.S. 658

**Docket or dockets:** No. 93-609.

**Decided:** 1994-05-23.

##### Bounded statutory reinstatement of completed securities limitations dismissals

**Holding:** Article III permits §27A(b)'s expressly retrospective requirement to reinstate a private §10(b) action commenced on or before June 19, 1991, dismissed as time barred after June 19, 1991, and timely under the jurisdiction's law, including retroactivity principles, as that law existed on June 19, 1991, upon a motion filed within sixty days after the provision's December 19, 1991 enactment. The federal court must independently determine whether those cumulative conditions are satisfied and, if they are, must reinstate the action without a discretionary balancing test, retaining independent responsibility for the claims, defenses and remedies; this bounded entitlement to adjudication neither directs a merits judgment nor establishes an unrestricted power to reopen completed federal judgments.

**Proposition-level authority:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela's Opinion of the Court is joined in full by Blackmun, Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg. These five direct joins control the rule, explanation, qualifications and remedy; no combination of separate rationales is necessary.

**Material application:** Pacific Mutual's January 31, 1992 motion met the sixty-day deadline; that fact is not a finding of fraud or entitlement to a merits judgment.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Robertson v. Seattle Audubon Society, March 25, 1992: extends its distinction between changing governing law and prescribing adjudication to this bounded reinstatement procedure; resolves the final-judgment question left open there only within the present limits.
- United States v. Klein, 80 U.S. (13 Wall.) 128 (1872): applies its protection of independent adjudication; a new eligibility rule requiring renewed adjudication does not supply a legislative finding or dictate securities liability.
- McCullough v. Virginia, 172 U.S. 102 (1898): preserves protection of completed judicial determinations while distinguishing this new statutory entitlement to litigate claims dismissed on limitations grounds from legislative contradiction of adjudicated facts or liability.
- Pennsylvania v. Wheeling & Belmont Bridge Co., 59 U.S. (18 How.) 421 (1856): distinguishes a change in the continuing operation of prospective relief; its setting does not itself resolve reinstatement of this completed dismissal.
- United States v. Sioux Nation of Indians, 448 U.S. 371 (1980): distinguishes waiver of the Government's own defense; retained judicial adjudication is relevant, but no governmental waiver of a private defendant's defense is assumed here.
- Lampf, Pleva, Lipkind, Prupis & Petigrow v. Gilbertson, 501 U.S. 350 (1991): identifies the intervening limitations rule to which Congress responded; §27A(b) supplies the bounded statutory remedy without deciding the underlying fraud claims.
- James B. Beam Distilling Co. v. Georgia, 501 U.S. 529 (1991), and Harper v. Virginia Department of Taxation, June 18, 1993: retain the distinction between judicial retroactivity in still-open litigation and final judgments; the present entitlement arises from an express statute, not judicial retroactivity alone.
- Landgraf v. USI Film Products and Rivers v. Roadway Express, April 26, 1994: preserve the separation between express legislative temporal direction and constitutional limits; no ordinary presumption against retroactivity defeats §27A(b)'s explicit reach.
- Chase Securities Corp. v. Donaldson, 325 U.S. 304 (1945): preserves its due-process treatment of revived limitations claims without treating that decision as dispositive of Article III.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Congress may not declare a completed judicial factual finding erroneous and compel the contrary finding, dictate a merits judgment, or disguise an individualized award or assessment of liability as a procedural reopening statute. This holding does not decide compelled reopening of merits judgments or individualized legislative commands to adjudicate liability. The Court announces no independent due-process holding and does not decide §27A(a), general severability, fraud, liability, damages or entitlement to judgment on the securities merits. Pending litigation, prospective relief and independently authorized judicial reopening remain distinct.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Fifth Circuit's judgment sustaining the reinstatement procedure is affirmed. Proceedings may continue under §27A(b). The federal court must determine the statutory prerequisites and reinstate the action when they are proved; it has no discretion to deny reinstatement through a balancing test. The securities claims, defenses and remedies remain for ordinary judicial adjudication. No fraud finding, liability judgment or damages award is entered.

**Material judgment:** Affirmed, 5–3. Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg support affirmance; Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas dissent. O'Connor does not participate.



END EXACT COPIED TEXT


## Inherited law: Plaut v.

Source: `terms/OT1997/entering-law/OT_1997CHUNK9.md`

Source SHA256: `0ccf978132798bdec6ec2299d0b2287843c0e61cc31f525d54c154b1909fe088`

BEGIN EXACT COPIED TEXT

#### Plaut v. Spendthrift Farm, Inc.

**Docket or dockets:** No. 93-1121.
**Decided:** April 18, 1995.

##### Section 27A(b) permits mandatory reinstatement of this completed limitations dismissal

**Holding:** Under Morgan Stanley, Article III permits Congress to require reinstatement under §27A(b) of a qualifying private §10(b) action even though its limitations dismissal became final without appeal before the provision's December 19, 1991 enactment: the action must have commenced on or before June 19, 1991, been dismissed as time barred after that date, and been timely under the jurisdiction's law, including retroactivity principles, as it existed June 19, 1991, with a reinstatement motion filed within sixty days after enactment. The court independently determines those cumulative conditions and, when they are satisfied, must reinstate without discretionary balancing, retaining independent responsibility for all claims, defenses, and remedies; this expressly retrospective entitlement does not authorize legislative findings, dictated liability, or unrestricted reopening of completed merits judgments.

**Proposition-level authority:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela's Opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Those five direct rationale joins control every proposition, qualification, and remedy stated here. O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas dissent; no combination of separate rationales is necessary.

**Material application:** A narrowly defined, exclusively retrospective class does not by itself turn this procedure into a legislative judgment: the statute covers every action meeting its legal criteria and selects no parties for legislatively determined liability. The statutory conditions are satisfied, including the timely February 11, 1992 motion.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Morgan Stanley & Co. v. Pacific Mutual Life Insurance Co., May 23, 1994: applies its five-Justice Article III holding sustaining §27A(b)'s bounded mandatory reinstatement procedure to this qualifying completed private limitations dismissal; preserves independent eligibility and merits adjudication and all constitutional limits.
- Robertson v. Seattle Audubon Society, March 25, 1992: applies its distinction between genuine legislative change and prescribed judicial adjudication; Morgan Stanley supplies the completed-dismissal rule that Robertson did not decide.
- United States v. Klein, 80 U.S. (13 Wall.) 128: applies its protection of the independent judicial function; a class-defined entitlement to renewed adjudication does not dictate findings or securities liability.
- McCullough v. Virginia, 172 U.S. 102: preserves protection of completed judicial determinations while distinguishing the statutory entitlement to litigate a limitations-dismissed claim from a legislative declaration of facts or liability contrary to an adjudication.
- James B. Beam Distilling Co. v. Georgia, 501 U.S. 529: preserves finality against reopening by later judicial doctrine alone; the entitlement here comes from an express statute whose Article III validity Morgan Stanley establishes.
- Pennsylvania v. Wheeling & Belmont Bridge Co., 59 U.S. (18 How.) 421: distinguishes the continuing operation of prospective equitable relief; that setting does not itself decide this completed limitations dismissal.
- United States v. Sioux Nation of Indians, 448 U.S. 371: distinguishes the Government's waiver of its own defense; no governmental waiver of a private defendant's defense supplies the holding here.
- Chase Securities Corp. v. Donaldson, 325 U.S. 304: leaves its due-process revival rule intact without treating it as a controlling answer to Article III.
- Lampf, Pleva, Lipkind, Prupis & Petigrow v. Gilbertson, 501 U.S. 350: identifies the limitations rule that produced the dismissal and the statutory response; §27A(b) supplies reinstatement without establishing the securities merits.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Congress may not command findings contrary to the record, impose liability on identified defendants by legislative declaration, or disguise an individualized award as a procedural reopening law. The Court decides no general power to set aside completed judgments adjudicating the underlying merits, no constitutionality of every retrospective statute, and no independent due-process question; applicable due-process protections and other constitutional restrictions remain. Section 27A(a), other applications of subsection (b), general severability, federal waiver, continuing prospective relief, and independently authorized judicial reopening are not decided. The decision neither adjudicates other parties' completed cases nor creates criminal or innocence remedies. Fraud, reliance, causation, liability, damages, and ordinary merits defenses remain for the courts; reinstatement establishes none of them.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The judgment of the Sixth Circuit is reversed. The case is remanded for reinstatement under the satisfied conditions of §27A(b) and further judicial proceedings on the underlying securities claims. Reinstatement is required, not a discretionary remedy. The courts retain responsibility for the merits, defenses, and any relief; no merits outcome is prescribed.

**Material judgment:** Reversed and remanded, 5–4. Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer support reversal; O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas dissent.



END EXACT COPIED TEXT


## Inherited law: Kokkonen v.

Source: `terms/OT1997/entering-law/OT_1997CHUNK9.md`

Source SHA256: `0ccf978132798bdec6ec2299d0b2287843c0e61cc31f525d54c154b1909fe088`

BEGIN EXACT COPIED TEXT

#### Kokkonen v. Guardian Life Insurance Co. of America, 511 U.S. 375

**Docket or dockets:** No. 93-263.

**Decided:** 1994-05-16.

##### Postdismissal contract enforcement requires a jurisdictional basis

**Holding:** After a Rule 41(a)(1)(ii) stipulated dismissal that neither incorporates settlement obligations nor retains jurisdiction, a court has no ancillary or inherent jurisdiction to enforce the separate settlement merely because it ended a federal action; an independent jurisdictional basis is required. Neither factual interdependence nor authority to manage proceedings supports jurisdiction when the settlement-breach facts are separate from the former claim and breach violates no obligation in the dismissal order; judicial awareness or approval of the agreement is insufficient.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's Opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg; all nine adopt the rule, application, and reversal with remand.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Willy v. Coastal Corp., 503 U.S. 131 (1992): distinguished; collateral Rule 11 authority does not create jurisdiction over a new settlement contract.
- Julian v. Central Trust Co., 193 U.S. 93 (1904): distinguished by its express reservation concerning judicially conveyed property; broad language is not general settlement-enforcement authority.
- Moore v. New York Cotton Exchange, 270 U.S. 593 (1926): its factual-interdependence basis is inapplicable to these distinct controversies.
- Chambers v. NASCO, Inc., 501 U.S. 32 (1991): its authority over litigation misconduct does not convert every settlement breach into interference with judicial proceedings.
- McNutt v. General Motors Acceptance Corp., 298 U.S. 178 (1936): applied; the party asserting federal jurisdiction bears the burden of establishing it.

##### A lawful dismissal order may make settlement compliance enforceable

**Holding:** A court may enforce settlement obligations through ancillary jurisdiction when, within its lawful dismissal authority, its order incorporates those obligations or expressly retains jurisdiction over the settlement, making breach a violation of the judicial disposition. Under Rule 41(a)(2) the court may impose such terms in its discretion; under Rule 41(a)(1)(ii) it may incorporate or retain jurisdiction if the parties agree, but private consent alone, awareness of the bargain, or an attempted later addition after jurisdiction has ended does not create jurisdiction.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg; all nine adopt this qualification defining the boundary of the jurisdictional holding.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Julian's actual reservation-based holding remains effective; Rule 41's distinct mechanisms are applied without creating a mandatory retention rule. Rule 60(b) reopening is reserved and is not treated as contract enforcement.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court decides jurisdiction, not breach, specific performance, damages, Rule 60(b) reopening or diversity for a new claim. Neither private consent nor postdismissal judicial rewriting supplies lost jurisdiction.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Ninth Circuit is reversed and remanded for dismissal of the settlement-enforcement motion for lack of the asserted ancillary jurisdiction. No breach adjudication or reopening follows. State contract enforcement and a new federal action with an independent jurisdictional basis remain distinct.



END EXACT COPIED TEXT


## Inherited law: Peacock v. Thomas

Source: `terms/OT1997/entering-law/OT_1997CHUNK9.md`

Source SHA256: `0ccf978132798bdec6ec2299d0b2287843c0e61cc31f525d54c154b1909fe088`

BEGIN EXACT COPIED TEXT

#### Peacock v. Thomas, 516 U.S. 349

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-1453.

**Decided:** February 21, 1996.

##### Whether the later collection action has original ERISA jurisdiction

**Holding:** A later action imposing the corporate judgment on Peacock alleges no underlying ERISA or plan violation supporting the asserted federal jurisdiction. Veil piercing is a means of imposing liability, not an independent statutory violation; §502(a)(3) supplies relief for the obligations it identifies, not equitable relief at large.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Thomas), joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; seven Justices. Stone-Zsela does not join this analysis.

**Material application:** Later control and collection obstruction do not erase the original nonfiduciary adjudication or establish fiduciary responsibility during the earlier breaches. An unrestricted compensation demand does not become an authorized remedy merely by being called equitable.

**Limits and questions not reached:** ERISA's possible permission for veil piercing in an appropriately founded action is assumed without decision. No general ban on equitable monetary remedies or rule treating every unsuccessful federal merits theory as jurisdictionally defective follows.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Mertens's bounded equitable-category holding and antecedent-action reservation remain. O'Melveny and Travelers supply no general federal collection rule, and the lower court's categorical federal-preemption rationale is not approved.

##### Whether ancillary enforcement jurisdiction permits a new full-debt action

**Holding:** Authority to identify, preserve and execute against a judgment debtor's assets does not authorize a later independent action imposing its entire debt on another person without an independent jurisdictional basis. Prior party status does not itself make that person a judgment debtor.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Thomas), joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; eight Justices.

**Material application:** The later conduct differs materially from the original pension violations and requires new facts, duties and defenses. Rule 69 supplies procedure, not original jurisdiction; the original 1987 action predates §1367's December 1, 1990 boundary, and the later case supplies no original claim to which supplemental jurisdiction can attach.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Contempt, asset discovery, ordinary attachment and garnishment, fraudulent-transfer process directed to debtor property and independently founded actions remain available. No particular asset restoration, general alter-ego prohibition, personal-jurisdiction or service holding, governing South Carolina enforcement rule, federal displacement or later-conduct preclusion determination is made. The preserved service objection is neither rejected nor waived; subject-matter jurisdiction alone supplies no nationwide service.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse and remand for dismissal of the later action and removal of dependent personal liability, interest, fees and expenses, including the appellate recalculation instruction. The vacated $205,182.01 combined assessment is not reinstated; the original $187,628.93 Tru-Tech judgment remains intact. Otherwise available state-law rights and independently founded actions are not extinguished.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Kokkonen's independent-authority requirement and distinct decree-effectuation branch apply; Beecher limits creation of new obligations. Riggs and Labette County are distinguished because they enforced existing official duties rather than shifting the judgment debt.



END EXACT COPIED TEXT


## Inherited law: United States v. Nordic Village

Source: `terms/OT1997/entering-law/OT_1997CHUNK9.md`

Source SHA256: `0ccf978132798bdec6ec2299d0b2287843c0e61cc31f525d54c154b1909fe088`

BEGIN EXACT COPIED TEXT

#### United States v. Nordic Village, Inc., 503 U.S. 30 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-1629.
**Decided:** February 25, 1992.

##### Whether original Bankruptcy Code §106(c) waives immunity from this affirmative personal money judgment

**Holding:** Original Bankruptcy Code §106(c) does not unequivocally waive the United States’ immunity from an affirmative personal money judgment recovering an unauthorized transfer under §550. A plausible construction permitting binding determinations of governmental rights and estate liabilities without an affirmative payment order defeats the required clear consent to this monetary remedy.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia’s opinion, joined by Stone, White, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas; seven Justices control the statutory threshold.

**Current force:** Remains controlling as stated for original §106(c) and the personal monetary remedy adjudicated.

**Material application:** The trustee avoided an unauthorized transfer under §549 and sought $20,000 from the United States under §550 after corporate money paid an officer’s individual taxes. Ordinary recovery authority under §550 does not answer the separate sovereign-consent question; no claim-linked waiver under §106(a) or (b) supplies this judgment.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The holding does not validate the diversion, strip §106(c) of effect, decide state constitutional immunity, or construe a different or later waiver. It does not alter §106(a) or (b); neither subsection supplies consent to the actual judgment.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Sixth Circuit’s judgment is reversed and the $20,000 personal money judgment set aside for want of sovereign consent. No substitute award is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Irwin v. Department of Veterans Affairs*, 498 U.S. 89; *United States v. Mitchell*, 445 U.S. 535; and *United States v. King*, 395 U.S. 1, supply the requirement of unequivocal consent to the particular monetary remedy. *Hoffman v. Connecticut Department of Income Maintenance*, 492 U.S. 96, supplies plurality statutory reasoning adopted for this federal question; its fractured state judgment is not a controlling federal-waiver holding. *Ruckelshaus v. Sierra Club*, 463 U.S. 680, and *McMahon v. United States*, 342 U.S. 25, prevent enlarging consent beyond Congress’s language. *United States v. Menasche*, 348 U.S. 528, supports giving both paragraphs of §106(c) operative work. *Dellmuth v. Muth*, 491 U.S. 223, supplies textual clear-statement reasoning without a new ruling about States.

##### Whether bankruptcy jurisdiction, property and trust characterizations, or equitable recoupment sustain this money judgment

**Holding:** A general grant of jurisdiction over bankruptcy-estate property in §1334(d), an asserted trust interest, or the origin of transferred money in estate assets does not supply federal consent to this personal money judgment. Because the trustee seeks a sum rather than identified property under the court’s control, this order cannot be sustained as a genuine property adjudication or as equitable recoupment.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia’s opinion, joined by Stone, White, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas; the same seven Justices reject each alternative offered to sustain the monetary judgment.

**Material application:** The order imposes a general obligation to pay $20,000. No identified property or fund supports it, and the relief is an affirmative recovery rather than defensive recoupment.

**Limits and questions not reached:** A genuine specific-property proceeding requires its own facts and authority and is not adjudicated here. No traceable fund is assumed.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The alternative grounds do not preserve the personal money judgment. Genuinely distinct, properly supported remedies remain for ordinary proceedings; none is awarded.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Blatchford v. Native Village of Noatak*, 501 U.S. 775, distinguishes subject-matter jurisdiction from immunity. *United States v. Whiting Pools, Inc.*, 462 U.S. 198, is distinguished because tangible debtor-property turnover does not authorize this Treasury payment. *Begier v. IRS*, 496 U.S. 53, does not supply an identified fund here. *United States v. Shaw*, 309 U.S. 495, is preserved against inferring monetary consent from a property characterization. *Bull v. United States*, 295 U.S. 247, and *United States v. Dalm*, 494 U.S. 596, preserve recoupment’s limited defensive operation, which does not sustain this affirmative award.



END EXACT COPIED TEXT


## Inherited law: United States Department of Energy v. Ohio

Source: `terms/OT1997/entering-law/OT_1997CHUNK9.md`

Source SHA256: `0ccf978132798bdec6ec2299d0b2287843c0e61cc31f525d54c154b1909fe088`

BEGIN EXACT COPIED TEXT

#### United States Department of Energy v. Ohio, 503 U.S. 607 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 90-1341 and 90-1517.
**Decided:** April 21, 1992.

##### Whether the Clean Water Act and Resource Conservation and Recovery Act citizen-suit provisions waive immunity from the requested punitive fines

**Holding:** The CWA and RCRA citizen-suit provisions authorize proceedings against the United States but do not unequivocally expose it to the punitive civil penalties incorporated from their separate enforcement provisions. Those incorporations include the limits of the penalty provisions, whose generally defined liable persons exclude the United States.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter, Part II-A, joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas; six votes control. White, Blackmun and Stevens dissent on this issue.

**Material application:** The express inclusion of the United States among defendants does not alter the separate incorporated penalty provisions, whose general definitions of liable persons omit the United States.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The ruling concerns the provisions effective April 21, 1992 and the requested punitive fines. It decides no later amendment, other express waiver, particular coercive contempt sanction, settled pollution allegation, independent cause of action or fine amount. The stayed technical-study claim is unaffected.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The consolidated judgment is reversed and remanded. In No. 90-1341, the CWA facilities and RCRA citizen-suit punitive-waiver rulings are reversed, 6–3. In No. 90-1517, rejection of RCRA facilities waiver is sustained, 9–0, and the CWA citizen-suit alternative is rejected, 6–3. The contingent amount stipulation supplies no independent liability and operates only if a lawful remaining basis warrants it. Clear compliance duties and coercive relief remain distinct.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- United States v. Mitchell, 445 U.S. 535 — applied: federal consent to the asserted liability must be unequivocal.
- United States v. Nordic Village, Inc., decision of February 25, 1992 — applied: a plausible narrower construction defeats asserted consent to the particular affirmative monetary remedy.
- Engel v. Davenport, 271 U.S. 33 — applied: statutory incorporation includes the referenced provision’s limitations.
- Ruckelshaus v. Sierra Club, 463 U.S. 680, and McMahon v. United States, 342 U.S. 25 — applied: waiver is not enlarged beyond what the enacted language requires.

##### Whether the Clean Water Act federal-facilities process-and-sanctions language waives immunity from past-violation punishment

**Holding:** The CWA federal-facilities provision’s submission to requirements, process and sanctions does not unequivocally waive immunity from the punitive fines sought for completed violations. In its statutory context, sanctions directed to enforcing process support coercive compliance relief without clearly adding retrospective punishment.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter, Part II-B.1, joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas; six votes control. White, Blackmun and Stevens dissent.

**Material application:** The statute twice links sanctions to process. That context permits the narrower reading of coercive measures securing compliance rather than punishment for completed substantive violations.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The ruling concerns the provisions effective April 21, 1992 and the requested punitive fines. It decides no later amendment, other express waiver, particular coercive contempt sanction, settled pollution allegation, independent cause of action or fine amount. The stayed technical-study claim is unaffected.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The consolidated judgment is reversed and remanded. In No. 90-1341, the CWA facilities and RCRA citizen-suit punitive-waiver rulings are reversed, 6–3. In No. 90-1517, rejection of RCRA facilities waiver is sustained, 9–0, and the CWA citizen-suit alternative is rejected, 6–3. The contingent amount stipulation supplies no independent liability and operates only if a lawful remaining basis warrants it. Clear compliance duties and coercive relief remain distinct.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Penfield Co. of California v. SEC, 330 U.S. 585 — applied to distinguish coercive compliance sanctions from punishment.
- Hicks v. Feiock, 485 U.S. 624 — applied to the conditional character of coercive sanctions, without deciding a contempt case here.
- Shell Oil Co. v. Iowa Department of Revenue, 488 U.S. 19 — applied: statutory context determines the relevant meaning.
- United States v. Mitchell, 445 U.S. 535, and United States v. Nordic Village, Inc., February 25, 1992 — applied: ambiguity about the particular punitive remedy defeats the claimed waiver.

##### Whether EPA approval makes state penalties arise under federal law for the Clean Water Act waiver

**Holding:** State-law punitive penalties do not arise under federal law for the CWA federal-facilities waiver merely because EPA approved the state pollution program. The provision’s qualified reference to civil penalties, read with its coercive-enforcement language and the separate penalty definitions, does not unequivocally authorize the requested punitive fines under either asserted source.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter, Part II-B.2, joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas; six votes control. White, Blackmun and Stevens dissent.

**Material application:** Federal approval does not transform Ohio’s penalty statute into a federal source of liability. The reference to civil penalties does not independently enlarge the incorporated federal penalty definition.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The ruling concerns the provisions effective April 21, 1992 and the requested punitive fines. It decides no later amendment, other express waiver, particular coercive contempt sanction, settled pollution allegation, independent cause of action or fine amount. The stayed technical-study claim is unaffected.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The consolidated judgment is reversed and remanded. In No. 90-1341, the CWA facilities and RCRA citizen-suit punitive-waiver rulings are reversed, 6–3. In No. 90-1517, rejection of RCRA facilities waiver is sustained, 9–0, and the CWA citizen-suit alternative is rejected, 6–3. The contingent amount stipulation supplies no independent liability and operates only if a lawful remaining basis warrants it. Clear compliance duties and coercive relief remain distinct.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Gully v. First National Bank in Meridian, 299 U.S. 109 — applied by statutory analogy: federal permission does not make a state-law claim arise under federal law.
- International Bridge Co. v. New York, 254 U.S. 126 — applied: congressional approval does not itself create the state-law right.
- ICC v. Brotherhood of Locomotive Engineers, 482 U.S. 270 — applied: familiar statutory language may carry its established construction into another statute.
- Arkansas v. Oklahoma, decision of February 26, 1992 — distinguished: federal character of permit administration does not determine punitive-waiver scope or convert every state penalty into federal law.
- United States v. Mitchell, 445 U.S. 535, and United States v. Nordic Village, Inc., February 25, 1992 — applied: uncertain implications do not expand a clearer, narrower consent.

##### Whether the Resource Conservation and Recovery Act federal-facilities provision waives immunity from punitive fines

**Holding:** RCRA’s federal-facilities provision does not unequivocally waive federal immunity from punitive fines for past violations. Its submission to substantive and procedural requirements, including permits, reporting, injunctive relief and sanctions enforcing that relief, reaches compliance mechanisms without clearly authorizing retrospective penalties.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter, Part II-C, joined by the other eight Justices; all nine Justices join this holding.

**Material application:** Permitting, reporting, injunctions and sanctions enforcing injunctions address compliance; the text identifies no unequivocal mechanism for punishing completed violations.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The ruling concerns the provisions effective April 21, 1992 and the requested punitive fines. It decides no later amendment, other express waiver, particular coercive contempt sanction, settled pollution allegation, independent cause of action or fine amount. The stayed technical-study claim is unaffected.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The consolidated judgment is reversed and remanded. In No. 90-1341, the CWA facilities and RCRA citizen-suit punitive-waiver rulings are reversed, 6–3. In No. 90-1517, rejection of RCRA facilities waiver is sustained, 9–0, and the CWA citizen-suit alternative is rejected, 6–3. The contingent amount stipulation supplies no independent liability and operates only if a lawful remaining basis warrants it. Clear compliance duties and coercive relief remain distinct.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Mitzelfelt v. Department of Air Force, 903 F.2d 1293 — adopted on this point: requirements can encompass standards and their implementation without punitive measures.
- Hancock v. Train, 426 U.S. 167 — distinguished: subjection to permitting procedures does not determine the scope of punitive remedies; amended statutory language receives its enacted effect.
- EPA v. California, 426 U.S. 200 — retained as pre-amendment statutory context, not authority to disregard the later express compliance duties.
- United States v. Mitchell, 445 U.S. 535 — applied: no unequivocal consent to punitive liability appears in the specified injunctive-enforcement language.



END EXACT COPIED TEXT


## Inherited law: United States v. Gaudin

Source: `terms/OT1997/entering-law/OT_1997CHUNK9.md`

Source SHA256: `0ccf978132798bdec6ec2299d0b2287843c0e61cc31f525d54c154b1909fe088`

BEGIN EXACT COPIED TEXT

#### United States v. Gaudin

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-514.
**Decided:** June 19, 1995.

##### The jury decides application of the materiality element

**Holding:** When materiality is an element of the charged criminal false-statement offense, the Fifth and Sixth Amendments require the jury to determine it beyond a reasonable doubt. The judge states statutory meaning and the governing legal standard; labeling its application a mixed question does not remove an element from the jury.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia’s Opinion of the Court, joined by all eight other Justices; nine Justices adopt the element-allocation rule.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Materiality concerns a statement's natural tendency or capacity to influence the relevant governmental decision. The Government conceded that it was an element and did not contest the lower court's error classifications. Clause-specific materiality requirements, universal structural-error treatment and exact unverified preservation history remain undecided. Statutory interpretation and threshold evidentiary sufficiency remain judicial functions.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- In re Winship: applies proof beyond a reasonable doubt to facts establishing guilt.
- Sinclair v. United States: overruled to the extent it assigns a criminal pertinency element to the judge; unrelated propositions are not displaced.
- Kungys v. United States: distinguishes civil denaturalization and its appellate allocation.
- Sullivan v. Louisiana: preserves the actual existing constitutional jury/burden holding; this case creates no universal omitted-element error classification.
- Hubbard v. United States: preserves §1001 entity coverage at its actual scope; supplies no authority to remove HUD offense elements from a jury.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the Ninth Circuit's reversal of the reviewed false-statement convictions. Lawful retrial submitting every element, including materiality, to the jury remains available; no acquittal, disposition of the separate equity-skimming conviction or sentence calculation follows.



END EXACT COPIED TEXT


## Inherited law: United States v. Thomas

Source: `terms/OT1997/entering-law/OT_1997CHUNK9.md`

Source SHA256: `0ccf978132798bdec6ec2299d0b2287843c0e61cc31f525d54c154b1909fe088`

BEGIN EXACT COPIED TEXT

#### United States v. Thomas

**Docket or dockets:** Sixth Circuit Nos. 94-6648 and 94-6649; no separate Supreme Court docket assigned.
**Decided:** 1997-05-19.

**Companion or consolidated matters:** Robert and Carleen Thomas's appeals are consolidated. For the separate community-standards holding, see First Amendment — Electronic obscenity distribution, United States v. Thomas, May 19, 1997, these dockets.

##### The offense-date statute covers these transmitted images

**Holding:** Offense-date §1465 reaches obscene visual image files knowingly transported through interstate computer-and-telephone facilities for sale or distribution. Applying its picture, image and electrical-transcription terms to this means of conveying the charged images does not depend on a later computer-specific amendment; the statutory distribution purpose and applicable culpability requirements remain necessary.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Part I, unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The distribution purpose and applicable culpability remain necessary; no rule brings every new technology within every old crime.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Alpers applies comprehensive media terms to different means of actual conveyance; purpose alone creates no offense. Lanier requires conduct-time fair warning, not a retroactive computer-specific amendment or decision.

##### Venue lies where the authorized interstate transportation ends

**Holding:** Article III, the Sixth Amendment and 18 U.S.C. §3237(a) permit trial in the district where the charged continuing interstate transportation reaches the recipient whom the distributor authorized to receive it. The approved Memphis downloads completed that transportation there; known destination supports this application without becoming a universal additional statutory element.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Part III, unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The ruling concerns actual authorized delivery, not remote effects alone, every district crossed by signals, unknown viewers or passive pages. Knowledge of destination supports this application without becoming a universal added statutory element.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the reviewed convictions on statutory coverage, receiving-community standards and venue, 8–1. Robert Thomas's Count 11 acquittal is untouched; entrapment, selective prosecution, evidence, counsel, sentencing and forfeiture remain outside decision.



END EXACT COPIED TEXT


## Effective current-term law: Beach

Source: `terms/OT1997/freeze/OT_1997CHUNK9_ENTERING_NEUTRAL_PROJECTION.md`

Source SHA256: `92026c6446a07a02a0c9698b01d33aa98ebd7310da651b25b88eb14c6375f8fa`

BEGIN EXACT COPIED TEXT

#### Beach v. Ocwen Federal Bank, No. 97-5310. — 1998-04-21

[Record](../records/beach_v_ocwen_federal_bank_merits_1998-04-21.md#public-projection)

##### Event

Beach v. Ocwen Federal Bank, No. 97-5310. Merits decision, April 21, 1998; October Term 1997, chunk 7. Argued March 2, 1998. On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of Florida. The Beaches refinanced their home in 1986, stopped payments in 1991 and faced foreclosure in 1992. The trial court allowed damages offsets but rejected rescission on transaction-classification and expiration grounds. Florida's highest court addressed only expiration. The question is whether the Truth in Lending Act's federal rescission right survives its statutory expiration when asserted as a foreclosure defense.

##### Participation

All nine Justices participate at argument and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

##### Public Action

The Court affirms the Supreme Court of Florida.

##### Judgment & Remedy

**Affirmed, 9–0.** Supporting: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. The expired federal rescission defense fails. The existing $396 actual-damages and $1,000 statutory-damages offsets remain untouched. No new award, hearing or injunction is ordered.

##### Opinion Topology

Stone-Zsela delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. No separate writing is filed.

##### Holdings

###### Expiration of the right defeats a later federal rescission defense

**Holding and operative rule:** Under 15 U.S.C. §1635(f), the federal rescission right ordinarily expires three years after consummation or upon earlier sale of the property, even when required disclosures have not been made, subject to the agency-proceeding extension stated below. After that expiration a borrower cannot revive the right by asserting rescission defensively in foreclosure.

**Authority:** Stone-Zsela's opinion, joined in full by the other eight Justices, supplies the unanimous rule and complete explanation.

**Controlling explanation:** Congress limited the life of the right itself. Reiter and Bull preserve same-transaction recoupment against a limitation on an affirmative action, only up to the plaintiff's asserted liability; neither recreates a right Congress has ended. Section 1640(e) separately preserves damages by recoupment or setoff in a collection action after its one-year affirmative-action period, except as otherwise provided by State law. That express distinction reinforces the different consequence of §1635(f). The lender's control over collection timing cannot alter it. Consumer protection does not require perpetual availability of every remedy; limiting the time for unwinding a secured transaction can protect title while preserving defensive damages on their own terms.

The agency extension requires an agency empowered to enforce the subchapter to institute a §1635 enforcement proceeding within three years of consummation, find a violation, and involve a matter on which the obligor's rescission right rests in whole or part. The right then expires at the later of the ordinary endpoint—three years after consummation or earlier sale—and one year following conclusion of the proceeding, any judicial review, or the period for judicial review. A private suit or regulatory inquiry alone does not satisfy those conditions. No qualifying proceeding is identified here.

The Beaches' federal right expired in 1989, before the 1992 foreclosure. Reich bars withdrawal of a government remedy previously held out; it does not extend this private substantive right. Brockamp and Bay Area Laundry likewise require attention to each enacted clock and consequence, not a universal deadline rule. The Court does not adopt the trial court's alternative residential-mortgage classification or decide timely notice, enforcement of timely rescission, tender, overall loan validity or independent State rescission law. Section 1635(i)'s foreclosure provisions remain subject to §1635(f); no new application of them is decided.

##### Precedent Treatment

- Reiter and Bull retain their same-transaction recoupment rule against an affirmative-action limitation; they are distinguished from termination of the underlying right.
- Reich retains its protection against a State's withdrawal of a held-out tax remedy; it supplies no extension of this private statutory right.
- Brockamp is applied for attention to an integrated timing scheme, while its reservation of separate recoupment theories remains intact.
- Bay Area Laundry supports distinguishing different statutory clocks and their consequences; its collection-accrual rule is unchanged.

##### Law After Decision

An expired §1635 federal right supplies no rescission defense in a later foreclosure. The express agency extension remains available on its own conditions. Defensive damages under §1640(e), including its State-law qualification, and independent state-law remedies retain their separate governing rules; the Court does not establish that any additional remedy belongs to the Beaches. No general jurisdictional classification or ruling about all repose, tolling or defense provisions follows.

##### Separate Writings

No separate writing is filed.

##### Procedure After Action

The Florida Supreme Court's rejection of the federal rescission defense stands, with the existing damages offsets undisturbed. Foreclosure proceeds under the otherwise governing law. The Court orders no new monetary relief or evidentiary proceeding and leaves unpresented remedies unresolved.

##### Source Notes

The statutory provisions, transaction chronology, lower-court grounds and offsets appear in [523 U.S. 410](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/97-5310.ZO.html), including the agency extension in §1635(f), §1640(e)'s State-law qualification and §1635(i)'s retained time limit. The Florida judgment is reported at 692 So. 2d 146. The review concerns federal rescission, not an adjudication of independent state-law relief.



END EXACT COPIED TEXT


## Published prior positions: C & A Carbone

Source: `terms/OT1993/output/OT_1993CHUNK4.md`

Source SHA256: `bafc31cad917d30329e478d8275913e1a1e845b0f4e63daa03157a0e1f6fc1f2`

BEGIN EXACT COPIED TEXT

### C & A Carbone, Inc. v. Town of Clarkstown, No. 92-1402

Merits decision, May 16, 1994

#### Chronology and Posture

Argued December 7, 1993; decided May 16, 1994. On writ of certiorari to the New York Appellate Division, Second Department, 182 App. Div.2d 213, 587 N.Y.S.2d 681. The reviewed order maintained injunctive enforcement of the Town's exclusive waste route. The question is whether compulsory processing through the designated privately operated transfer station violates the Commerce Clause. Independent zoning, environmental-review, taking and state-law rulings are outside this decision.

To replace its landfill under a consent-decree obligation, the Town contracted for private construction and operation of a transfer station for five years, guaranteed a minimum waste stream and bore the contractual shortfall risk. It could then acquire the station for one dollar; the purchase was an option, not a mandatory transfer. The station's tipping charge exceeded competing disposal costs. Local Law No. 9 required the designated route, including for Carbone's already sorted unrecycled residue, which Carbone instead shipped to out-of-state destinations. The ordinance regulates private waste transactions beyond the Town's own purchases.

Section 3(C) permits source-separated recyclables to go to facilities inside or outside the Town. Acceptable waste may initially go to specially permitted recycling centers, but unrecycled residue must follow the Town-facility route; this is not unconditional permission to operate a recycling center. Section 3(B)'s licensed-carter requirement separately excepts construction and demolition debris and exceptions supplied by the chapter and its rules. Acceptable waste excludes hazardous waste, pathological waste and sludge, but §4 permits a Town resolution for sewer sludge from a municipal sewer system or the Rockland County sewer district. Section 5(A)'s outside-waste exception requires an agreement with the Town for acceptable waste at a Town facility and separately permits recyclables at specially permitted centers. Section 5(B)'s restriction on importing, dumping and mixing outside waste so as to conceal its contents or origin has a different exception for Town composting of acceptable waste. Those provisions are not independently invalidated here; §8 preserves severable portions.

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg participate at argument and decision.

#### Judgment

The Court reverses and remands the judgment enforcing the challenged exclusive-routing requirement, 7–2.

| Component | Disposition and vote | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Remedy |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Commerce Clause basis of the maintained flow-control enforcement judgment | Reversed and remanded, 7–2 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg | Blackmun, Souter | Set aside enforcement of the challenged compulsory route against Carbone and adjust the injunction consistently on remand. Preserve independent valid rules and severability; no damages, refund, unconditional operating injunction or contract rescission. |

Six Justices support discrimination analysis; O'Connor supplies a seventh judgment vote under Pike. Blackmun and Souter would affirm the Commerce Clause ruling on the municipal-service and insufficient-burden ground.

#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Exact scope |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Thomas, Ginsburg | Reverse and remand | Discrimination in this private processing market, failure of justification, regulatory/proprietary distinction and bounded remedy. |
| Concurrence in the judgment | O'Connor | No other Justice | Reverse and remand | Pike excessive-burden analysis; rejects the discrimination classification and separately rejects the claimed congressional authorization. |
| Dissent | Souter | Blackmun | Would affirm | Evenhanded municipal-service characterization and inadequate showing of a clearly excessive interstate burden under Pike; no categorical proprietary immunity. |

#### Controlling Propositions and Authority

##### Compulsory use of this local private processor discriminates in the service market

**Controlling proposition:** A municipality discriminates against interstate commerce when it compels private waste transactions to use a selected local private processing service and excludes competing interstate services, even though competing local processors are excluded too and the material may later leave the State. This build-and-operate financing arrangement, including the Town's tonnage guarantee and option to purchase later, does not turn the present regulatory command into a purchase of disposal for the Town's own waste; the Court decides no materially different genuinely public system.

**Authority:** Kennedy's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Thomas and Ginsburg; six Justices adopt the discrimination classification and its stated private-service boundary. O'Connor agrees only with the judgment on a distinct Pike ground.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the relevant commerce includes processing services, not only the movement of discarded material. Dean Milk rejects a local processing reservation even when it also burdens nearby competitors, and Fort Gratiot prevents exclusion of other intrastate interests from curing discrimination against interstate commerce. Clarkstown compels waste holders to buy the designated private operator's service, including an additional stop for Carbone's already sorted residue. That command forecloses competing processors from serving the demand. New Energy makes the number of favored businesses immaterial; Exxon does not protect an ordinance reserving this service market merely because other local firms also lose business. The Town's public responsibility, financing risk and purchase option deserve consideration, but the operator presently receives the protected processing business through coercive regulation of others' transactions. Boston Harbor preserves genuine project purchasing, not a procurement label for that command. The Court leaves ordinary government purchasing and materially different public provision unresolved rather than announcing a rule against every municipal franchise.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Dean Milk Co. v. Madison, 340 U.S. 349 (1951): applied to compulsory local processing despite burdens on other local competitors.
- Fort Gratiot Sanitary Landfill v. Michigan Department of Natural Resources, 504 U.S. 353 (1992): applied to geographic private-service reservation and the distinction between planning and proprietary conduct.
- New Energy Co. of Indiana v. Limbach, 486 U.S. 269 (1988): applied; discrimination does not require a minimum number of favored businesses.
- Exxon Corp. v. Governor of Maryland, 437 U.S. 117 (1978): distinguished; this ordinance reserves a processing market rather than merely changing its business structure without a local service preference.
- Building & Construction Trades Council v. Associated Builders & Contractors (Boston Harbor), 507 U.S. 218 (1993): preserved for genuine proprietary project purchases; its labor-preemption holding does not immunize coercive regulation of unrelated private transactions.

##### Sanitary service and financing do not justify this discriminatory route

**Controlling proposition:** A discriminatory local processing requirement can survive only if the municipality establishes a legitimate local purpose that reasonable nondiscriminatory alternatives cannot adequately serve. Clarkstown's interests in safe, reliable disposal and financing the station do not justify the challenged compulsory private-service reservation on this record; neutral safety regulation and lawful nondiscriminatory financing remain available, without a new proof quantum, perfect-certainty demand, or requirement to disprove every imaginable alternative.

**Authority:** Kennedy's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Thomas and Ginsburg; six Justices adopt this application of the discrimination rule. No independent Pike holding is adopted by that coalition.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court accepts the Town's duty to provide sanitary disposal and its need for dependable financing. Those ends do not establish that competing processing services must be excluded. Chemical Waste and Oregon Waste require a legitimate purpose that adequate reasonable nondiscriminatory means cannot serve; they do not authorize ordinary balancing to replace that inquiry after discrimination is identified. Safety standards, inspection and appropriate capacity requirements can address the quality of processing without reserving purchases to one private operator. Lawful financing can address public costs without this particular commercial exclusion. The Court directs no tax, bond, subsidy or substitute fee, and does not treat the price difference alone as proof of constitutional invalidity. The strongest contrary argument is that the guaranteed stream makes the promised service financially dependable. A contractual revenue commitment nevertheless cannot by itself justify discriminatory regulation of private transactions. Relief therefore removes the challenged enforcement ground while leaving independent valid rules, the ordinance's severability provision and lawful public financing for ordinary implementation below.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Chemical Waste Management v. Hunt, 504 U.S. 334 (1992): applied for the legitimate-purpose and adequate-nondiscriminatory-alternatives inquiry, with its proof and remedial limits preserved.
- Oregon Waste Systems v. Department of Environmental Quality, April 4, 1994: applied to keep discrimination, justification and public/private capacity distinct; no compensatory-tax issue is decided here.
- Philadelphia v. New Jersey, 437 U.S. 617 (1978): applied to the Commerce Clause's protection of waste commerce despite valid environmental objectives.
- Pike v. Bruce Church, Inc., 397 U.S. 137 (1970): reserved for genuinely evenhanded incidental burdens; it supplies O'Connor's separate ground, not an independent holding of the Court.

#### Precedent Treatment and Current-Law Effect

The waste-commerce decisions remain effective, and the Court applies their discrimination and justification rules to this compulsory private processing service. Boston Harbor's project-purchasing rule remains confined to its proprietary setting. No precedent is overruled. O'Connor's Pike and congressional-authorization discussion and the dissent's municipal-service analysis are separate positions, not additional controlling rules.

Effective May 16, 1994, the Town may not enforce the challenged exclusive private-processing route against Carbone. The rule covers the present commercial reservation, despite exclusion of other local processors and the Town's financial responsibility and option to acquire the station. It does not decide a materially different genuinely public system, ordinary purchasing for government waste, or every municipal franchise.

Neutral health, inspection and capacity requirements and lawful nondiscriminatory financing remain available under their own requirements. The Court neither orders a replacement program nor guarantees a competitor's profits. The ordinance's source-separated-recyclables provision, conditional outside-waste agreement provision, separate composting and municipal-sludge exceptions, licensing rules and severability clause retain the distinct scope stated above; their independent validity is not adjudicated. Chicago's ash-generation ruling addresses a different statutory question and supplies no authorization for the challenged route. No hazardousness finding, compensatory-tax determination, contract rescission or unconditional disposal entitlement follows.

#### Separate Positions Relevant to the Decision

Justice O'Connor concurs in the judgment. She does not regard selection of one municipal contractor as discrimination among geographic classes when other local processors and outside processors are excluded alike. Her joins in Fort Gratiot and Oregon Waste addressed express geographic preferences and do not compel that classification here. She instead finds a clearly excessive burden under Pike: already processed residue must make a mandated local stop, at the protected charge, to support the designated operation, although neutral safety standards and lawful financing can serve the Town's legitimate objectives. The price difference is not itself a complete social-cost calculation, and her analysis does not declare every expensive municipal service unconstitutional. No adjudicated unavoidable conflict with a New Jersey rule is assumed.

She also concludes that the cited RCRA provisions do not unmistakably authorize otherwise unconstitutional flow control. Sections 6941 and 6943 encourage sound planning; §6943(a)(5)'s removal of state or local impediments to long-term supply and operating contracts does not remove federal Commerce Clause limits. Section 6948(d)(3)(C) provides for EPA assistance concerning institutional obstacles, not an express delegation to suspend those limits. The committee report cannot enact the missing authorization, and the regulation encouraging unrestricted movement across local boundaries does not provide it. Congress retains power to authorize a different arrangement expressly. This remains her separate supporting analysis, not a new holding of the Court.

Justice Souter dissents, joined by Justice Blackmun. They view the arrangement as provision of a single municipally guaranteed service rather than preference for a geographic class of independent businesses. The Town must replace its landfill, bears the tonnage-shortfall risk and may acquire the station. They acknowledge present private operation, coercive routing and interstate movement; their conclusion does not depend on denying those facts or asserting automatic market-participant immunity. Under Pike they give weight to dependable sanitary service, financing and allocation of costs among waste generators. The loss of Carbone's processing business and the station's higher charge do not, in their view, establish a clearly excessive interstate burden on this record.

The dissent accepts Fort Gratiot's distinction between planning and proprietorship but differentiates this single contracted public service from reserving ordinary private landfill capacity by origin. Oregon Waste's origin surcharge does not, for them, resolve that classification. Blackmun's Reeves opinion and his waste-case dissents support attention to the public program, while his Exxon dissent requires confronting the service-market exclusion rather than relying merely on continued shipments. They would affirm on this contract and record without immunizing every private public-purpose contractor, treating future acquisition as certain, or finding that no outside processor could ever be injured.

#### Source Notes

[New York Appellate Division, 182 A.D.2d 213](https://static.case.law/ad2d/182/cases/0213-01.json), supplies the maintained reargument order, enforcement posture and separate rulings. [Official report, 511 U.S. 383–430](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-511/pdf/USREPORTS-511-383.pdf), supplies the docket, dates, published contract account and full Local Law No. 9 in its appendix. The reported purchase option is not converted into a mandatory acquisition. The complete contract, merits briefs and joint appendix were not independently recovered; no new bidding, investor-residence, cost-equivalence, unavoidable interjurisdictional-conflict or future-ownership finding is made.

#### Mandate, Remedy, and Stage

The state appellate judgment is reversed on the challenged Commerce Clause enforcement ground, and the case is remanded for proceedings consistent with this decision, including appropriate adjustment of the injunction. Other appeals dismissed below as superseded or terminated are not revived. Independent valid zoning, health, permitting, dumping, recycling and financing measures remain subject to their own law and the ordinance's severability clause. No damages, refund, compelled acceptance of a particular load, contract rescission, substitute tax or fee, or mandate deadline is fixed.

**End of entry: C & A Carbone, Inc. v. Town of Clarkstown, Merits decision, May 16, 1994.**

---



END EXACT COPIED TEXT


## Published prior positions: Plaut

Source: `terms/OT1994/output/OT_1994CHUNK4.md`

Source SHA256: `bbf430e5eb5575c15167985decf0570e40e83e4cf3a229f3e54fcf696554108c`

BEGIN EXACT COPIED TEXT

### Plaut v. Spendthrift Farm, Inc., No. 93-1121

Merits decision, April 18, 1995

#### Chronology and Posture

Argued November 30, 1994; decided April 18, 1995. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit, reviewing its August 3, 1993 judgment, 1 F.3d 1487. The question is whether §27A(b) may require reopening this final, unappealed private federal securities dismissal.

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer participated at argument and decision.

#### Judgment

**Reversed and remanded, 5–4.**

**Supporting:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer.
**Opposing:** Justices O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas.

The statutory conditions are satisfied, including the timely February 11, 1992 motion. Reinstatement is mandatory; the remand does not authorize discretionary refusal through an equitable balancing test. The underlying securities claims, defenses, and remedies remain for judicial adjudication. No finding of fraud, merits judgment, or damages award is entered.

#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Opinion of the Court | Chief Justice Stone-Zsela | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer | Reversal and remand | Entire opinion, including the statutory conditions, Article III rule, response to narrow retrospective coverage, limits, and remedy. |
| Dissent | Scalia | O'Connor, Kennedy, and Thomas | Would affirm denial of reinstatement | Entire dissent; no part of the Court's opinion. |

#### Controlling Propositions and Authority

##### Section 27A(b) permits mandatory reinstatement of this completed limitations dismissal

**Controlling proposition:** Under Morgan Stanley, Article III permits Congress to require reinstatement under §27A(b) of a qualifying private §10(b) action even though its limitations dismissal became final without appeal before the provision's December 19, 1991 enactment: the action must have commenced on or before June 19, 1991, been dismissed as time barred after that date, and been timely under the jurisdiction's law, including retroactivity principles, as it existed June 19, 1991, with a reinstatement motion filed within sixty days after enactment. The court independently determines those cumulative conditions and, when they are satisfied, must reinstate without discretionary balancing, retaining independent responsibility for all claims, defenses, and remedies; this expressly retrospective entitlement does not authorize legislative findings, dictated liability, or unrestricted reopening of completed merits judgments.

**Authority:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela's Opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Those five direct rationale joins control every proposition, qualification, and remedy stated here. O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas dissent; no combination of separate rationales is necessary.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Morgan Stanley governs this completed limitations dismissal. Finality matters: the unappealed judgment ended the ordinary review process. But Morgan Stanley distinguishes Congress's creation of a bounded entitlement to further adjudication from legislative adjudication of the controversy itself. Section 27A(b) supplies the governing rule; judges determine whether its conditions are satisfied and decide the securities claims.

The statute's narrow coverage and exclusively retrospective operation require attention, but do not make it an individualized legislative judgment. It reaches every action satisfying objective conditions concerning commencement, dismissal, governing limitations law, and the time for seeking relief. It does not identify parties for an award or assessment of liability. A closed temporal class is still a class defined by law. Congress has directed no finding of deception, reliance, causation, or damages. Independent judicial determination remains both at the eligibility stage and on the merits. Mandatory reinstatement after eligibility is established is the consequence of the new legal rule, not a legislative finding that the plaintiffs prevail on their claims.

Robertson distinguishes a genuine change in governing law from prescribed adjudication; Morgan Stanley supplies the completed-dismissal application that Robertson left open. Klein protects the judicial function without forbidding this statutory entitlement. McCullough's protection of completed judicial determinations remains: Congress may not dictate contrary findings or declare a defendant liable. Beam's finality limit on judicial retroactivity does not erase an express statutory remedy. Wheeling Bridge and Sioux Nation concern continuing equitable relief and sovereign waiver, respectively; neither supplies an unrestricted reopening power. Chase Securities addresses due-process revival, not the Article III issue resolved here.

The February 11, 1992 motion satisfied the statutory conditions. Reinstatement therefore follows, with the securities merits left for the courts. The dissent's objection treats completion of this limitations dismissal as an absolute bar despite the retained judicial work; Morgan Stanley forecloses that rule in this setting. Other completed merits judgments, individualized legislative commands, and independent constitutional protections remain outside this holding.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Congress may not command findings contrary to the record, impose liability on identified defendants by legislative declaration, or disguise an individualized award as a procedural reopening law. The Court decides no general power to set aside completed judgments adjudicating the underlying merits, no constitutionality of every retrospective statute, and no independent due-process question; applicable due-process protections and other constitutional restrictions remain. Section 27A(a), other applications of subsection (b), general severability, federal waiver, continuing prospective relief, and independently authorized judicial reopening are not decided. The decision neither adjudicates other parties' completed cases nor creates criminal or innocence remedies. Fraud, reliance, causation, liability, damages, and ordinary merits defenses remain for the courts; reinstatement establishes none of them.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Morgan Stanley & Co. v. Pacific Mutual Life Insurance Co., May 23, 1994: applies its five-Justice Article III holding sustaining §27A(b)'s bounded mandatory reinstatement procedure to this qualifying completed private limitations dismissal; preserves independent eligibility and merits adjudication and all constitutional limits.
- Robertson v. Seattle Audubon Society, March 25, 1992: applies its distinction between genuine legislative change and prescribed judicial adjudication; Morgan Stanley supplies the completed-dismissal rule that Robertson did not decide.
- United States v. Klein, 80 U.S. (13 Wall.) 128: applies its protection of the independent judicial function; a class-defined entitlement to renewed adjudication does not dictate findings or securities liability.
- McCullough v. Virginia, 172 U.S. 102: preserves protection of completed judicial determinations while distinguishing the statutory entitlement to litigate a limitations-dismissed claim from a legislative declaration of facts or liability contrary to an adjudication.
- James B. Beam Distilling Co. v. Georgia, 501 U.S. 529: preserves finality against reopening by later judicial doctrine alone; the entitlement here comes from an express statute whose Article III validity Morgan Stanley establishes.
- Pennsylvania v. Wheeling & Belmont Bridge Co., 59 U.S. (18 How.) 421: distinguishes the continuing operation of prospective equitable relief; that setting does not itself decide this completed limitations dismissal.
- United States v. Sioux Nation of Indians, 448 U.S. 371: distinguishes the Government's waiver of its own defense; no governmental waiver of a private defendant's defense supplies the holding here.
