BEGIN EXACT COPIED SOURCE

### Camps Newfound/Owatonna, Inc. v. Town of Harrison, No. 94-1988

Merits decision, May 19, 1997

#### Chronology and Posture

On review of the Supreme Judicial Court of Maine, 655 A.2d 876, which on April 21, 1995 reversed summary judgment for the camp and sustained the challenged property tax. Argued October 9, 1996. The question is whether the nonresident-beneficiary condition in Maine's charitable-property exemption discriminates against interstate commerce and whether a subsidy, market-participant or resident-benefit justification sustains it. The Maine nonprofit's 180-acre Christian Science camp recruits nationally; about 95 percent of campers come from outside Maine. Its reported charge is roughly $400 per week and annual property taxes exceed $20,000. It timely pursued state refund and prospective-exemption procedures.

Section 652(1)(A) generally covers real and personal property owned and occupied or used solely for the purposes of benevolent and charitable institutions incorporated by Maine, without loss of exemption merely because of the source of funds or limitations in beneficiary classes, subject to the express nonresident subparagraph. An institution operated principally for nonresidents may exempt no more than $50,000 of current just property value only if the statutory average weekly charge per person does not exceed $30; above that rate it receives no exemption under that subparagraph. The computation divides all tax-year stipends or charges for services, benefits or advantages by all persons receiving them during that year, then divides by the weeks during that tax year when the institution is actually operated principally for nonresidents; §502 defines the tax year. The reported weekly price is not substituted for that statutory computation.

The nonresident subparagraph does not apply to a nonprofit corporation incorporated solely to conduct medical research; that exception does not waive the general exemption's other requirements and is not found applicable to this camp. The paragraph also includes nonprofit nursing, boarding and boarding-care facilities licensed under Title 22, chapter 1665 or its successor; nonprofit community mental-health facilities licensed under Title 34-B, chapter 3; and nonprofit child-care centers incorporated by Maine as benevolent and charitable institutions. Nonprofit means exempt under Internal Revenue Code §501(c)(3). No independent challenge to Maine incorporation, neutral qualification, religious liberty, equal protection or travel rights is decided.

All nine Justices participated at argument and decision.

#### Judgment

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Remedy or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Federal validity of the beneficiary-residence condition in this application | Reversed and remanded, 6–3 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Breyer | Scalia, Thomas and Ginsburg | The condition cannot sustain the challenged unequal treatment; the state judgment's federal validation is reversed |
| Scope of affirmative remedial directions | State-law severability and constitutionally adequate relief without automatic extension of the exemption, 5–4 | Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Breyer | Stone-Zsela would require the exemption on this scheme; Scalia, Thomas and Ginsburg would affirm and award no relief | State courts apply neutral qualifications and lawful remedial choices, including any required backward-looking correction; no specified refund, interest or new assessment |

Stone-Zsela's disagreement over the affirmative remedy does not change his vote for reversal of the residence condition. The three affirming Justices' conditional acceptance of ordinary remedial limits is not a remand vote.

#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy and Souter | Reverses federal validation and remands for lawful state relief | Parts I–III in full |
| Same opinion | Breyer | Stone-Zsela | Reverses the residence condition but requires a more specific exemption remedy | Parts I–II; no join in Part III's refusal to select extension of the exemption |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Joins invalidation; would require extension of the exemption under this scheme | Case-specific exemption remedy, with refund and prospective relief subject to governing law |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas and Ginsburg | Would affirm the Maine judgment | Resident-charitable-assistance comparison and alternative justification; no actual-purchase or successful compensatory-tax finding |
| Dissent | Thomas | Scalia | Would affirm on an additional constitutional ground | Proposed abandonment of the negative Commerce Clause doctrine and tentative Import-Export Clause alternative; Ginsburg does not join |

The controlling merits have six direct votes; the complete state-remedy holding has five. No separate ground is aggregated under Marks.

#### Controlling Propositions and Authority

##### I. Paid interstate camp services remain protected against discriminatory taxation

**Holding and operative rule:** A charitable camp that recruits nationally and sells services to visitors crossing state lines participates in interstate commerce, although its property and performance are local and it is nonprofit. Where the same otherwise qualifying private camp offering receives worse tax treatment because its principal beneficiaries are nonresidents, that express geographic condition discriminates; a claimed resident-service distinction must rest on the actual relationship or offering, not beneficiary residence alone.

**Authority:** Breyer's Part I, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy and Souter, controls by six direct votes.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court examines the transaction and the comparison. *Heart of Atlanta* establishes that interstate travel to obtain locally consumed services is commerce. *Fulton* establishes that a local taxpayer and local property do not remove a tax condition keyed to an interstate relationship. Nonprofit organization does not eliminate the paid camp service and national clientele, though the Court decides no rule for every charitable act or public benefit.

*Tracy* requires attention to actual service obligations. Its regulated rate, reliability and service duties materially distinguished the offering there; it created no categorical public-utility or welfare exception. Here the asserted difference is that resident-serving charity benefits Maine's own community. That is a legitimate objective, but the present condition identifies recipients rather than a materially different private offering or supported public-service obligation. No actual service-segment uncertainty comparable to *Tracy* requires a new evidentiary hearing. The Court neither makes utility-style regulation indispensable in every case nor presumes all charitable services identical. It rejects this particular attempt to make the challenged residence criterion establish its own immunity. The same camp's tax treatment depends on changing the geographic mix of its clients. The low-charge property-value cap and medical-research exception do not make that applied condition neutral; the Court decides no hypothetical claimant's qualification under either provision.

##### II. Resident benefit, subsidy and public-capacity labels do not justify this tax condition

**Holding and operative rule:** This discriminatory private-clientele tax condition can survive only upon a legitimate local purpose that reasonable nondiscriminatory alternatives cannot adequately serve; support for resident charity does not establish that showing here. Forgone taxes do not make Maine a purchaser of the camp's services or remove the discriminatory tax condition from review, and a compensatory-tax defense requires an identified intrastate burden, a roughly equivalent nonexcessive charge and substantially equivalent taxable events, none established by general resident contributions or asserted charitable savings.

**Authority:** Breyer's Part II has the same six direct joins as Part I.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court applies *Oregon Waste*'s discrimination-and-alternatives inquiry and *Fulton*'s refusal to let a deduction label cleanse geographic favoritism. Maine may support local welfare through direct resident programs, ordinary general-revenue assistance or neutral charitable-property criteria; it has not shown that resident support requires worsening a private provider's tax treatment because outsiders buy its services. The contrary argument treats the exemption itself as resident-directed aid and stresses that direct payments can also select residents. But permissible public assistance does not confer immunity on taxation controlling independent private clientele. This decision neither invalidates direct resident benefits nor requires charitable support everywhere.

*SSC* protects actual purchased performance, while no purchase, contract for camp services or government operation is established here. *West Lynn* examines practical fiscal operation and preserves ordinary assistance; Maine need not replicate its dedicated commercial assessment for a different discriminatory tax condition to fail. The tax/subsidy comparison is therefore not merely an economic equivalence inquiry. Compensation is independently governed by *Fulton* and *Oregon Waste*: no matching burden, equivalence or equivalent taxable event follows from generalized contribution or expenditure-saving claims. The Court finds no invented numerical disproportion, no litigant default and no duty to disprove every imaginable alternative. It applies the existing rule to the actual grounds supporting this condition.

##### III. Reversal requires lawful state relief, not an automatic exemption or fixed refund

**Holding and operative rule:** The judgment sustaining this discriminatory condition must be reversed and the case remanded for state-law severability, remaining neutral exemption conditions and constitutionally adequate relief. If payment was compelled before meaningful review, adequate backward-looking correction is required; independently, a clearly held-out postpayment remedy may not be withdrawn after payment and replaced by an unpursued prepayment route, even where payment was voluntary, subject to reasonable preannounced conditions and fair prospective changes.

**Authority:** Breyer's Part III, joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy and Souter, has five direct votes. Stone-Zsela would require the exemption under the presented scheme and does not join this complete remedial choice; Scalia, Thomas and Ginsburg would affirm.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court applies *Fulton*'s separation of constitutional invalidity from the State's lawful remedial choices. *McKesson* supplies the conditional backward-looking duty when payment precedes meaningful review; *Reich* separately prevents withdrawal of the promised postpayment route. Where correction is required, lawful refunds, constitutionally permissible retrospective equalization or a lawful combination may suffice. The camp's timely pursuit of state procedures preserves its claim but does not establish the amount due or every neutral exemption qualification. Reversal therefore does not automatically reinstate every feature of the trial-level judgment. The state courts must address their law and the actual procedural predicates without enforcing the invalid residence preference. The Court orders no particular refund, interest, assessment against absent charities, unconditional enlarged exemption or substitute subsidy, and calculates no statutory weekly average.

#### Precedent Treatment

- *Heart of Atlanta Motel, Inc. v. United States*: Applies the interstate-travel and local-service principle without making all charity commerce.
- *General Motors Corp. v. Tracy*: Applies the actual-service comparison and distinguishes the supported regulated obligations there; its record-specific holding remains, with no public-utility immunity or reduced Commerce Clause scrutiny.
- *Fulton Corp. v. Faulkner*: Applies geographic-tax scrutiny despite a local taxpayer or exemption form, its cumulative compensatory conditions, and its conditional state-remedy framework.
- *Oregon Waste Systems v. Department of Environmental Quality*: Applies demanding justification after discrimination and the distinct compensation requirements; ordinary incidental-burden balancing is insufficient.
- *C & A Carbone, Inc. v. Town of Clarkstown*: Applies the private-service comparison and public/regulatory distinction while preserving genuinely different public systems.
- *West Lynn Creamery, Inc. v. Healy*: Applies attention to practical fiscal operation without requiring its dedicated-fund facts or condemning ordinary general-revenue assistance.
- *SSC Corp. v. Town of Smithtown*: Preserves protection for actual government-purchased performance; no purchase arises merely from a tax exemption.
- *McKesson Corp. v. Division of Alcoholic Beverages and Tobacco* and *Reich v. Collins*: Preserve their distinct backward-looking and promised-postpayment-remedy rules; neither supplies an automatic refund amount here.

#### Law After Decision

Effective May 19, 1997, the beneficiary-residence condition cannot sustain the unequal charitable-property treatment of these paid interstate camp services. Nonprofit status and local property do not remove the actual interstate transaction. Actual service differences, genuine public purchases and lawful ordinary assistance remain distinct; no new categorical utility or charity immunity is recognized. The statute's neutral qualifications, state severability and lawful remedial choices remain for the state courts, subject to the controlling constitutional limits. No independent holding invalidates the medical-research exception or determines a hypothetical low-charge claimant's capped exemption.

#### Separate Positions Relevant to the Decision

**Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and dissenting in part:** The Chief Justice joins the nondiscrimination holding but would direct the Maine courts to provide the exemption under this presented statutory scheme and any refund or prospective relief authorized by state remedial law. His exemption direction is a selected remedy for this case, not a universal federal requirement to extend every discriminatory benefit. Refund remains subject to proper state-law and due-process analysis, with no amount fixed. Maine remains free to replace the scheme with a nondiscriminatory charitable policy.

**Scalia, joined by Thomas and Ginsburg, dissenting:** They regard the relevant comparison as eligibility for the State's resident-charitable assistance, rather than physical similarity of camp services alone. Property restricted to charitable work serving Maine residents contributes to the social-welfare function the State supports. They do not deny the interstate travel or declare that nonprofits never conduct commerce. Their primary ground distinguishes this limited assistance from commercial protectionism; alternatively, resident welfare justifies the geographic limitation here. They answer the proposed alternatives by observing that resident-targeted cash payments still select residents: changing the fiscal form does not establish an adequate nondiscriminatory substitute for resident-only assistance.

They preserve the ordinary commercial tax rules in *Fulton* and *Oregon Waste*, and *West Lynn*'s distinction between dedicated commercial assessments and ordinary aid. They find no successful compensatory tax, quantified state savings, compulsory utility duties or actual *SSC* purchase. *Tracy* supplies no categorical utility exception to extend; their charity-assistance distinction is their independent proposed ground. They would affirm and award no exemption, refund or new hearing. Ginsburg joins this ground alone, retaining the existing constitutional framework.

**Thomas, joined by Scalia, dissenting:** In addition to the charity ground, they would abandon the judicial negative Commerce Clause doctrine. The affirmative commerce power supplies no textual prohibition of this kind; exclusivity and congressional-silence theories do not, in their view, justify indeterminate judicial policy balancing. Thomas would reconsider *Woodruff*'s interstate/foreign distinction when examining the textual Import-Export Clause as a possible check on certain interstate taxes. That alternative is tentative: the parties have not specifically presented it, and a final Clause ruling is reserved. A value-based real-property tax appears unlike an impost at entry or a duty on imported goods, so he would likely sustain it under that alternative as well. This is no Court overruling, definitive Import-Export holding or join by Ginsburg.

#### Source Notes

The facts and state proceedings appear in *Camps Newfound/Owatonna v. Harrison*, 655 A.2d 876, and the public record recited in [520 U.S. 564](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/520bv.pdf). The exact statutory source is Me. Rev. Stat. Ann., title 36, §652(1)(A) (Supp. 1996), as quoted at 520 U.S. 568–569 n.2. The statutory calculation is not replaced by the reported approximately $400 charge; no tax-year beneficiary count or precise refund is established. The published statutory recitation does not itself establish satisfaction of every neutral exemption condition.

#### Mandate, Remedy, and Stage

The case returns to the Maine courts for severability and lawful relief from the unconstitutional residence condition, with remaining neutral qualifications and actual remedial predicates examined under the controlling rules. No statutory average, refund amount, interest award, assessment against another charity, replacement financing program or universal extension of the exemption is ordered.

**End of entry: Camps Newfound/Owatonna, Inc. v. Town of Harrison, merits decision, May 19, 1997.**

---


END EXACT COPIED SOURCE

#### Camps Newfound/Owatonna, Inc. v. Town of Harrison

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-1988.
**Decided:** 1997-05-19.

##### Paid interstate camp services remain protected against discriminatory taxation

**Holding:** A charitable camp that recruits nationally and sells services to visitors crossing state lines participates in interstate commerce, although its property and performance are local and it is nonprofit. Where the same otherwise qualifying private camp offering receives worse tax treatment because its principal beneficiaries are nonresidents, that express geographic condition discriminates; a claimed resident-service distinction must rest on the actual relationship or offering, not beneficiary residence alone.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer's Parts I–II, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy and Souter; six direct joins.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The nonresident subparagraph grants at most $50,000 in exempt current just property value only if the statutory average weekly charge is no more than $30; higher charges receive no exemption under that subparagraph. The average divides tax-year charges by all recipients and by the weeks operated principally for nonresidents; the advertised weekly price is not that calculation. Nonprofit corporations incorporated solely for medical research are excepted from this subparagraph, not from neutral general qualifications. Neither hypothetical exception eligibility, Maine-incorporation validity, all charitable acts, religious liberty, equal protection nor travel rights is decided.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Heart of Atlanta treats interstate travel for local services as commerce; Fulton scrutinizes geographic tax conditions despite local property or an exemption form. Tracy requires actual offering/service obligations; its regulated rate, reliability and service duties are absent here, but no utility-style regulation is made universally indispensable. Carbone preserves comparison of the actual private service and distinct genuine public systems.

##### Resident benefit, subsidy and public-capacity labels do not justify this tax condition

**Holding:** This discriminatory private-clientele tax condition can survive only upon a legitimate local purpose that reasonable nondiscriminatory alternatives cannot adequately serve; support for resident charity does not establish that showing here. Forgone taxes do not make Maine a purchaser of the camp's services or remove the discriminatory tax condition from review, and a compensatory-tax defense requires an identified intrastate burden, a roughly equivalent nonexcessive charge and substantially equivalent taxable events, none established by general resident contributions or asserted charitable savings.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer's Parts I–II, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy and Souter; six direct joins.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The decision leaves direct resident benefits, ordinary general-revenue assistance, neutral charitable criteria and actual purchased services available. It requires no worldwide charitable support, presumes no numerical disproportion or default, and imposes no duty to disprove every imagined alternative.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Oregon Waste supplies demanding local-purpose/nondiscriminatory-alternative scrutiny. Fulton and Oregon Waste require the identified intrastate burden, rough equivalent nonexcessive charge and substantially equivalent taxable events for compensation. SSC protects actual purchased performance; forgone revenue establishes none. West Lynn examines practical fiscal operation without requiring its dedicated-fund facts.

##### Reversal requires lawful state relief, not an automatic exemption or fixed refund

**Holding:** The judgment sustaining this discriminatory condition must be reversed and the case remanded for state-law severability, remaining neutral exemption conditions and constitutionally adequate relief. If payment was compelled before meaningful review, adequate backward-looking correction is required; independently, a clearly held-out postpayment remedy may not be withdrawn after payment and replaced by an unpursued prepayment route, even where payment was voluntary, subject to reasonable preannounced conditions and fair prospective changes.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer's Part III, joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy and Souter; five direct votes. Stone-Zsela requires the exemption and does not join this open remedial choice; Scalia, Thomas and Ginsburg would affirm.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the residence-condition validation, 6–3, and remand under the five-Justice state-law remedy instruction. Required correction may take a lawful refund, constitutionally permissible retrospective equalization or combination; no fixed refund, interest, new assessment, substitute subsidy or unconditional exemption is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Fulton separates invalidity from lawful state remedial choices. McKesson requires backward correction when payment preceded meaningful review; Reich independently protects a clearly held-out postpayment route even after voluntary payment, subject to reasonable preannounced conditions and fair prospective change.


END EXACT COPIED SOURCE


### Inherited Tracy Commerce Clause holding

Source: `terms/OT1997/entering-law/OT_1997CHUNK9.md`

BEGIN EXACT COPIED SOURCE

#### General Motors Corp. v. Tracy

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-1232.
**Decided:** February 18, 1997.

For the separate questions in General Motors Corp. v. Tracy, No. 95-1232 decided February 18, 1997, see Federal Courts — Standing and personal injury (The assessed purchaser may bring this challenge); Civil Rights — Rational basis for economic classifications (The economic classification has a rational basis).

##### This record does not establish the asserted discrimination

**Holding:** Ohio's exemption for sales by regulated local distribution companies does not violate the Commerce Clause on this record, because their compulsory rate, reliability and service obligations materially distinguish their regulated supply business from independent marketers' commodity sales. Commodity overlap alone does not establish equivalence of those offerings where the record does not establish a distinct industrial-sales category separated from the obligations relied upon; the holding confers no general immunity on utilities or all of their competitive transactions.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II of Souter's opinion, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; seven Justices. Stone-Zsela and Stevens do not join.

**Material application:** The industrial-market record does not establish a distinct negotiated, unregulated sales category detached from the local distribution companies' compulsory service obligations during the 1986–1990 assessment period. Later legislation supplies no finding about those purchases.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Neither utility labeling nor equal taxation of local outsiders alone is sufficient. No finding attributes each sale to a particular public duty or presumes alternative taxation would endanger service. No compensatory-tax defense, incidental-burden claim or congressional authorization is decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The assessment judgment is affirmed, 7–2, without a refund, replacement rate, payment finding or new tax against utilities. Independent state classification and penalty-remission issues are unreviewed.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** West Lynn requires examination of integrated fiscal operation and Carbone of the actual service market. Oregon Waste's demanding justification and Fulton's cumulative compensation requirements remain; justification is not reached because this record does not establish the necessary comparison.


END EXACT COPIED SOURCE


### Inherited Fulton holding

Source: `terms/OT1997/entering-law/OT_1997CHUNK9.md`

SHA256 of source: `0ccf978132798bdec6ec2299d0b2287843c0e61cc31f525d54c154b1909fe088`

BEGIN EXACT COPIED SOURCE

#### Fulton Corp. v. Faulkner, 516 U.S. 325

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-1239.

**Decided:** February 21, 1996.

##### The issuer-based deduction discriminates against interstate investment

**Holding:** A State discriminates against interstate commerce when the tax on a resident's otherwise comparable corporate shares increases as the issuer's local income-tax exposure decreases. Neither the shareholder's local residence nor drafting the geographic advantage as a deduction removes that discrimination, and proof that a particular issuer already lost an investment is unnecessary.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Thomas), unanimous.

**Material application:** The tax is twenty-five cents per $100 of share value, reduced according to the issuer's North Carolina-apportioned income. For corporate shareholders the linked dividend-deduction proportion applies without the ordinary $15,000 dividend-deduction cap.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Oregon Waste's express-geographic-discrimination rule and West Lynn's examination of linked operation apply; a deduction label or shareholder residence does not remove discrimination.

##### Corporate income and share ownership are not shown to be equivalent taxable events

**Holding:** A compensatory-tax defense requires the State to identify the actual intrastate burden being offset, show a roughly equivalent and nonexcessive interstate charge, and establish substantially equivalent taxable events reasonably treated as mutually exclusive proxies. North Carolina's corporate-income and shareholder-property taxes do not satisfy those requirements merely because corporate profits may influence share value; the failure to establish equivalent events independently defeats the defense.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Thomas), unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Different nominal taxpayers do not invariably prevent compensation, and income and property taxes may coexist. This State failed to establish equivalent events.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Oregon Waste supplies the cumulative compensatory conditions, Maryland v. Louisiana the actual-local-burden requirement and Armco the independently fatal equivalent-events defect. Henneford preserves genuinely complementary taxation.

**Independent alternative holding:** A State does not establish the compensatory defense's actual-burden and approximate-equivalence requirements merely by invoking typical price/earnings ratios or aggregate corporate tax contributions. North Carolina's comparison fails to demonstrate the required matching burden on this record, an additional ground for rejecting its defense.

**Proposition-level authority for alternative holding:** Opinion of the Court (Thomas), unanimous.

**Limits, remedy, transition, or precedent treatment:** The 1990 claim is not governed by a new universal calculation using the later 7.75% rate; the state discussion also identifies the earlier 7% rate. Its general price/earnings reasoning is neither a statutory command nor an adopted equality finding for every taxpayer. Associated Industries requires the relevant actual matching burden, rather than aggregate or other-transaction offsets.

##### Darnell's contrary Commerce Clause permission is overruled

**Holding:** Darnell v. Indiana is overruled to the extent it permits discriminatory taxation of residents' shares in out-of-state corporations under the Commerce Clause merely by reference to a different corporate-level local tax and an asserted substantial equality. Its distinct equal-protection and standing determinations are not erased.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Thomas), unanimous.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Darnell v. Indiana, 226 U.S. 390, is expressly overruled only on its incompatible Commerce Clause permission. Oregon Waste and Associated Industries require the cumulative inquiry; Darnell's separate standing and equal-protection determinations remain.

##### Invalidity requires lawful remedial proceedings, not a predetermined refund

**Holding:** Reversal of the tax's constitutional justification requires remand for state-law severability and constitutionally adequate relief. When a State compelled payment before a meaningful opportunity to contest the tax, McKesson requires adequate backward-looking correction; independently, Reich forbids withdrawing a clearly held-out postpayment remedy after payment and substituting an unpursued prepayment route.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Thomas), unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** McKesson permits lawful refunds, constitutionally permissible equalizing retrospective treatment or a lawful combination where backward-looking relief is required. Reich's protection applies to a clearly held-out postpayment remedy even after voluntary payment; reasonable previously announced conditions and prospective changes with fair notice remain permissible.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse and remand for severability and constitutionally adequate relief according to actual payments, preservation, procedures and lawful state choices. No fixed refund, fee award, interest, assessment against others, blanket stock exemption, deduction change or automatic reinstatement of the intermediate appellate remedy follows.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** McKesson's conditional backward-looking duty and Reich's independent no-withdrawal protection govern relief without selecting a universal refund formula.


END EXACT COPIED SOURCE

# Florence / Stemler — neutral dependency refresh after Lewis

This immutable preparation replaces only the Florence section of `OT_1997CHUNK9_NEUTRAL_LATER.md` for future modeling. It changes no completed adjudication and contains no vote or provisional commitment. The earlier packet remains preserved. The new substantive input is the validated, public-only May 26 *County of Sacramento v. Lewis* projection, copied exactly in the annex below. No current commitment, reconciliation, private position, raw Record or audit annex was opened.

**Event cutoff:** June 1, 1998. **Admitted preparation baseline:** the frozen May 21 current-term public law, now supplemented by the May 26 Lewis decision. Original lower-source research and this refresh occurred October 24, 2026. Use the [original entering-law slice](../entering-law/OT_1997CHUNK9.md), [May 21 neutral projection](OT_1997CHUNK9_ENTERING_NEUTRAL_PROJECTION.md), and [Lewis public law](../entering-law/OT_1997CHUNK9_LEWIS_PUBLIC.md). The copied source annex, not a historical same-matter comparator, supplies the new law.

**Participation and authority:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer remain seated. No case-specific nonparticipation or argument date is established for this added matter. Its authorized June 1 setting and inventory caption supply no invented Supreme Court docket, intervening grant, party substitution, new filing or waiver. The actual federal appellate channel is §1254(1). Six qualified Justices are required for a quorum; the applicable judgment threshold is a majority participating. No participation change is inferred from missing materials.

**Readiness:** The Lewis dependency is now incorporated, and this complete packet is ready for independent neutral modeling on the currently supplied public law. The May 28 DeCarlo event has not yet been supplied as committed public law. Under Engine §3G, before the June 1 event the operator must compare its eventual public decision and any other earlier effective event with this packet. If it materially changes immunity, personal responsibility, attribution, proof or remedy, replace the affected neutral portion and rerun the affected downstream stages. A DeCarlo proposal, commitment or expected result supplies no law. The residual chronological check is not a fabricated source gap and does not predetermine a change.

## City of Florence v. Chipman / Stemler v. City of Florence — June 1, 1998

### Actual channel, parties, and review scope

Sixth Circuit Nos. 96-5993 and 96-5996, **126 F.3d 856 (October 8, 1997), rehearing denied November 13, 1997**. The consolidated appeals were brought by **the plaintiffs** after individual-officer dismissals under Rule 12(b)(6) and municipal summary judgments. This is **not** an officers' interlocutory appeal from denial of immunity. The source warning about Johnson factual-sufficiency jurisdiction must not misdescribe the channel. Ordinary pleading and summary-judgment standards still prohibit resolving disputed facts against the nonmovant.

The City and official-capacity defendants **won below**: their summary judgments were affirmed. Black's individual-officer substantive-due-process claim and Stemler's individual-officer orientation-based equal-protection claim were reinstated. All other challenged dismissals remained affirmed. “City of Florence” is the inventory caption, not proof that the city lost or may claim personal qualified immunity. The review opposing reinstatement belongs to the adversely affected individual-officer components; no new petition number or unnoticed party substitution is fabricated. The authorized questions concern the compelled dangerous placement, discriminatory selection despite probable cause, and conduct-time immunity. They do not independently reopen municipal liability, false arrest, malicious prosecution, abandoned excessive force or an unpleaded fabrication claim.

### Supported facts and distinction between claims

**Conni Black died; Susan Stemler did not.** The opinion recites evidence in the plaintiffs' favor unless otherwise indicated. Black and Steve Kritis had been drinking; witnesses described his assaults, threats and dangerous pursuit after Black asked Stemler to drive her away. These are reported allegations and evidence, not this Court's findings. Barker warned Lieutenant Dusing that Kritis threatened the women's safety. Stemler described intoxication, assaults and threats. Other Florence and Boone officers arrived. Kritis called Stemler a lesbian and accused her of taking Black; the label spread among the officers. Stemler denies being a lesbian, which does not eliminate a claim based on their perception.

The account supports knowledge of Kritis's intoxication and violence, but the officers' roles and knowledge differ. Reuthe told Dusing that Kritis smelled of alcohol; Dusing's later claim conflicts with other evidence. Dusing directed Dolan to tell Black she would be arrested for public intoxication if she did not leave with Kritis. Dolan and Alsip lifted her from Stemler's car; Alsip placed her in Kritis's truck. The estate alleges this was against her will; Dolan's contrary account remains disputed. Alsip said he never heard a request to leave and would have acted differently if he had known the preceding events. No collective inference supplies every officer's personal knowledge, culpability or causal role.

The crash occurred about five minutes after departure. Kritis reported a renewed struggle; reconstruction could not determine what caused the steering movement. He was later convicted of state offenses. Causation is not automatically established by proximity, nor erased merely because an officer did not drive the truck. Avoid gratuitous injury detail; the legally material facts are fatal injury, timing and causal dispute.

Stemler was arrested for DUI, prosecuted twice and acquitted at the second trial. A separate state civil judgment found probable cause; Kentucky treated it as final for issue preclusion despite a pending appeal. The circuit used that predicate to affirm false-arrest and ordinary malicious-prosecution dismissal, while preserving reconsideration if the state judgment later reversed. No such later reversal is established here. A fabrication theory raised after the officers had been dismissed was not pleaded against them and did not support reversal. A separate excessive-force claim was voluntarily dismissed. Equal protection remains distinct: the opinion described a supported comparison between Stemler's arrest and the nonarrest of visibly intoxicated Kritis, plus alleged purposeful group-based animus.

### Lower reasoning and material concurrence

The circuit rejected a general inference that uncertainty at the edges of substantive due process defeats every claim. It characterized Black as actually restrained by threats and physical placement, distinguished a general failure to protect, and found the alleged knowing dangerous transfer sufficient under the custody/deliberate-indifference authorities. It declined to decide noncustodial state-created danger. Its formulation sometimes says “knew or reasonably should have known.” Lewis now requires a contextual constitutional culpability inquiry and preserves the actual-awareness and reasonable-response limits of the distinct custodial authorities. The lower shorthand therefore cannot by itself establish the required mental state or turn constructive notice into actual awareness.

On Stemler, the court held probable cause did not foreclose selective enforcement, used effect and purpose plus an actually comparable unarrested person, and rejected a blanket claim that orientation-based animus could never violate equal protection. It found no need for heightened scrutiny. Its references to historical Armstrong, Whren, Romer and Bryan County must be replaced by their actual simulated scope; in particular Armstrong's federal-prosecutor discovery rule does not automatically supply a merits or police-stop discovery rule here. The supplied entering module identifies Bryan County's hiring/fault issue as reserved, not a historical holding to import.

Judge Wellford agreed with the disposition but would avoid declaring facts, broadening culpability or equating classifications. He found sufficient allegations of intentional arbitrary conduct or conscious disregard toward a person in custody and would leave actual constitutional tort proof below. At rehearing he stressed actual awareness and actual drawing of the serious-risk inference under Farmer. He also preserved questions concerning later Kentucky appellate rulings and claim splitting rather than adjudicating them. This separate lower opinion is a legal alternative, not a Supreme Court vote predictor.

### Entering law and open paths

*DeShaney*, 489 U.S. 189 (1989), distinguishes nonprotection from affirmative restraint; *Youngberg*, 457 U.S. 307 (1982), supplies custody-related protection; *Daniels*, 474 U.S. 327 (1986), excludes mere negligence. Simulated *Collins* (February 26, 1992) denies a general safe-workplace duty while expressly reserving compulsion, direct bodily injury and different affirmative-danger claims. Its separate attribution and substantive-right inquiries remain intact. Simulated *Pinder* (May 21, 1996) rejects custody from knowledge, promises or detention of an assailant, but leaves distinct affirmative deprivation analytically open. Its 5–4 immunity holding concerns a noncoercive 1989 undertaking, not this alleged forced 1994 transfer. O'Connor's conduct-time application has Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas with Stone; Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer dissented. The precise public positions matter without compelling a vote here.

### Lewis's now-effective rule and its exact boundary

On May 26 the Court adopted a **six-Justice contextual constitutional rule** in Kennedy's opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. In the supported rapidly evolving high-speed pursuit, instant choices amid competing enforcement and safety obligations required a purpose to cause harm unrelated to a legitimate law-enforcement objective; reckless risk-taking or deliberate indifference alone did not suffice there. That proposition governs at its stated setting and level of generality. The record's unintended collision and lack of an improper harmful purpose defeated the federal individual claim in that setting. Neither reversal's nine votes nor the words “unintended collision” enlarge it into a purpose-to-harm rule for every injury involving a vehicle.

Lewis expressly preserves the importance of **actual opportunity for deliberation**, the distinct duties and proof rules of custody cases, and analysis of purposeful collisions, deliberate terrorizing, contrived encounters and materially different opportunities for considered action. It establishes no seconds-based cutoff, no broad noncustodial immunity, and no automatic deliberate-indifference rule whenever an official could stop acting. Constitutional culpability must be grounded in precedent, constitutional tradition and the actual circumstances, rather than a judge's personal shock. Negligence remains insufficient. It does not license an official to manufacture the emergency on which a heightened threshold would depend.

The present allegations concern a **roadside decision after the private pursuit had stopped**: warnings, conversations, a threatened arrest, and officers' physical removal and placement of Black with a reportedly intoxicated and abusive driver. The complaint concerns the officers' own compelled transfer and the resulting danger; they were not the pursuing driver who unintentionally struck a passenger during a rapid chase. The warnings, successive choices and division of tasks are pertinent to a real opportunity to assess and respond. They do not establish any invented minimum deliberation time, identical knowledge among the officers, or a final finding that an opportunity existed for every defendant. A model must evaluate the supported situation and each actor, not transplant Lewis merely because a private chase preceded the stop or a fatal crash followed it.

The inquiry keeps separate: **(1)** the protected interest and actual deprivation or custodial restraint, **(2)** the culpability appropriate to the supported opportunity and function, **(3)** the particular officer's awareness, authority, action and causal responsibility, and **(4)** the separate conduct-time immunity defense. In a path relying on the preserved custodial deliberate-indifference authorities, the model must identify an actually known serious risk, a reasonable opportunity and authority to respond, a culpable disregard rather than a reasonable protective response, and attributable injury. Circumstantial evidence may establish awareness; “should have known,” official title, an unsuccessful response or the fatal outcome alone cannot replace it. Doe's supervisory-abuse framework is an instructive existing boundary, not a mechanically transplanted roadside checklist. Lewis creates no new independent state-law-to-constitutional conversion and does not resolve municipal attribution or state tort liability.

The lower court treated Black as actually in the officers' custody through threatened arrest and physical control and did not reach noncustodial state-created danger. The supported motion-to-dismiss account allows that question to be examined without declaring the conflicting evidence true. Pinder's reservation of a distinct affirmative-deprivation theory remains; it supplies neither a categorical duty in all danger cases nor a prohibition here. Where a specific constitutional provision actually covers a presented claim, Lanier's specific-provision principle remains. Lewis also preserves Albright's lack of a controlling universal displacement rationale. No new Fourth Amendment claim is silently substituted for the due-process claim actually reinstated below.

**Published Lewis positions, with their authority limits:** Stevens joined only the judgment on the particular May 22, 1990 qualified-immunity ground and reserved constitutional merits; his position supplies no seventh vote for the Court's standard and no automatic immunity for February 1994 coercion. Scalia and Thomas joined only the judgment on a distinct text/history/precedent ground concerning the carefully described reckless-pursuit claim; they did not join the Court's affirmative purpose-to-harm definition, and their writing does not categorically exclude intentional official assaults outside custody. Breyer joined the Court in full and separately preserved flexibility to resolve a suitable case on immunity first. These writings are publicly available individual positions for modeling, not controlling substitutes for the six-Justice rule or commitments in Florence.

### Conduct-time law remains a separate question

*Harlow*, *Anderson*, *Elder* and simulated *Lanier* require particularized apparent unlawfulness at the conduct date without a factual twin. **The event occurred overnight February 18–19, 1994.** Elder's February 23 opinion, Farmer later in 1994, and all later decisions may clarify present analysis but do not themselves give earlier notice. The conduct-date inquiry must rely on DeShaney, Youngberg, Daniels, relevant pre-event circuit authority and other law then effective. The lower opinion's *Nishiyama*, *Walton*, *Meador*, *Heflin* and similar authorities remain evidence of their actual holdings; later *Foy*, *Kneipp*, *Lewellen* or *Gazette* cannot be treated as earlier notice. No mandatory merits-first sequence exists.

Lewis is present law for the constitutional analysis but supplies **no retroactive notice in February 1994**. Its rule also imposes no mandatory merits-first sequence. A suitable disposition may rest on the particular conduct-time clarity question without resolving a difficult constitutional extension; conversely, present merits may be resolved when properly reached. The model must state which ground does the work and must not label an immunity-only judgment a holding that the challenged conduct was constitutional.

### Supported alternatives after the refresh

One Black path preserves the reinstatement of the individual claim because the favorable allegations identify an actual coercive deprivation with an opportunity for considered action, sufficient constitutional culpability under the applicable custodial authorities, and conduct that pre-event law made obviously unlawful. Its strongest counterargument is that the custody, each officer's knowledge and available response, causal relationship, and particular clarity must be established rather than inferred from the tragic result or generalized bodily-security language. The pleading posture permits testing legal sufficiency, not findings against the plaintiff from the conflicting testimony.

A competing path resolves a genuinely unclear application on qualified immunity, or narrowly corrects the lower judgment where it used a “should have known” formulation or undifferentiated attribution that cannot sustain the actual constitutional inquiry. Its strongest counterargument is that threatening and physically transferring a vulnerable person into a known danger is materially different from Pinder's noncoercive assurance or Lewis's rapid pursuit; uncertainty at the margins does not erase the obvious core of an established protection. A notice-based resolution must examine all actual pre-February-1994 authority without demanding a factual twin or using a tally of judicial disagreement. A narrower remand may clarify a legal standard or defendant-specific claim without deciding factual truth, finding liability, or mandating new discovery.

A constitutional path dismissing the claim under Lewis must independently justify why the supported restraint and opportunity fall within an applicable no-violation rule. Merely asserting no purpose to cause the eventual crash, calling all police work an emergency, or invoking every officer's ability to desist is insufficient under Lewis's express boundaries. Likewise, the claimant cannot obtain automatic liability by showing a deliberate act, a known risk in the abstract, noncompliance with local policy, or time to pause. The present rule does not select among these paths or predetermine how any Justice resolves the different facts.

Lewis decides no equal-protection rule. Stemler's selective-enforcement claim therefore remains separately analyzed under the authorities and allegations below, with its own effect, purpose, comparator, individual participation, causation and conduct-time notice requirements.

On equal protection, *Yick Wo*, *Oyler*, *Wayte*, and *Moreno* provide received nonarbitrary-enforcement and purpose/effect principles. Simulated *Whren* (June 10, 1996, unanimous Thomas opinion) preserves independent equal-protection objections despite ordinary traffic probable cause; *Romer* (May 20, 1996, seven-Justice Souter opinion) preserves ordinary review's limits on bare hostility without an orientation tier. Walmer/Thomasson's military conduct and rebuttable-inference holdings do not authorize civilian animus. Neither postdates conduct to create notice retroactively. The supported path allowing the claim rests on alleged arbitrary group-based purpose, differential treatment and the actual comparator under already existing principles; the competing immunity path must test their particular conduct-time clarity, not assert that no protection exists absent a suspect class. No intentional discrimination is found merely because the officers discussed orientation. A ground rejecting the comparison must respect the pleading posture and plaintiff-favorable account.

*Crawford-El*, *Clanton*, *Valot*, *Smith* and *Ricci* preserve separate elements, personal responsibility, ordinary proof, manner/scope limits, and conduct-date immunity. Ricci's additional civil-custody rule does not itself decide this compelled transfer or supply notice in 1994. The municipal defendants' favorable judgment is not reopened by finding an individual claim viable. A remand would authorize ordinary claim-specific proceedings, not liability, damages, a mandatory trial, specified discovery or automatic action on separate state cases.

## Exact new-law annex

# County of Sacramento v. Lewis: effective May26 public law

### Event

County of Sacramento v. Lewis, No. 96-1337. Plenary merits decision, May 26, 1998. Argued December 9, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit, 98 F.3d 434. The case concerns the constitutional culpability required when a rapid police pursuit ends in an unintended fatal collision. After final summary judgment, the court of appeals reversed Deputy Smith's individual qualified-immunity judgment using a deliberate-indifference or reckless-disregard standard. Its affirmance of the municipal judgments and the unappealed state-law dispositions are outside this Court's review. No Fourth or Eighth Amendment claim is presented.

### Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate.

### Public Action

The reviewed judgment against Deputy Smith is reversed.

### Judgment & Remedy

**Reversed, 9–0.** **Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. The case returns for proceedings consistent with termination of the reviewed federal individual claim, leaving the district court's favorable individual judgment in effect. Six Justices adopt the Court's contextual constitutional ground; Scalia and Thomas rely on a distinct constitutional ground, and Stevens relies only on qualified immunity. No damages, trial, new evidentiary hearing, municipal retrial or state-law judgment is directed.

### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal | Complete rapid-pursuit culpability rule, application, qualifications and remedy |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stevens | No other Justice | Supports reversal | Individual immunity because May 22, 1990 law did not make this conduct's federal unlawfulness sufficiently apparent; reserves constitutional merits |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas | Supports reversal | Distinct constitutional ground; no join in the Court's conscience-based purpose-to-harm standard |
| Concurrence | Breyer | No other Justice | Fully joins the Court and supports reversal | Preserves flexibility to decide an appropriate case on immunity before difficult constitutional merits |

### Holdings

**Holding and operative rule:** In the rapidly evolving high-speed pursuit presented here, where officers must make instant choices amid competing enforcement and safety obligations, reckless or deliberately indifferent risk-taking alone does not establish a substantive-due-process violation. A purpose to cause harm unrelated to a legitimate law-enforcement objective is required; because this plaintiff-favorable record does not establish that purpose, the reviewed federal individual claim fails.

**Authority:** Kennedy's opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, supplies six direct votes for the complete rule and application. Neither the two distinct constitutional votes nor Stevens's immunity vote supplies additional support for that rule. No combination of separate rationales is needed.

**Controlling explanation:** Rochin identifies the constitutional boundary against arbitrary executive abuse; its conscience-shocking standard must be applied through precedent, constitutional tradition and the actual setting, not a judge's personal reaction. Daniels excludes negligence but left intermediate culpability unresolved. Whitley explains why competing obligations and an emergency matter to fault, while reserving the civilian due-process question. Farmer's actual-awareness standard concerns prison safety with a materially different opportunity to respond; Doe likewise requires knowledge, authority and a reasonable opportunity for protection against deliberate abuse. Those holdings do not mechanically determine culpability in a rapid pursuit.

The officer chose to pursue, and the favorable account includes dangerous speed, close following distance, danger to others, inadequate communication and alleged policy violations. The lack of a serious predicate offense and the ability to abandon pursuit are substantial criticisms. They do not, in this rapidly developing encounter, establish the improper harmful purpose required for constitutional liability. The collision was unintended, and the supported record supplies no purpose to injure unrelated to enforcement. The Court finds neither wise police judgment nor an adjudicated benevolent motive.

Actual opportunity for deliberation remains important. This rule is not a universal intent requirement, an automatic deliberate-indifference rule whenever an official could desist, or permission to manufacture an emergency. Purposeful collisions, deliberate terrorizing conduct, contrived encounters and materially different opportunities for considered action require their own facts and analysis. The decision creates no seconds-based cutoff, broad noncustodial immunity or mandatory order between constitutional merits and qualified immunity. It resolves neither the excluded municipal and state claims nor a procedural-due-process challenge to state remedies.

### Precedent Treatment

- **Rochin v. California, 342 U.S. 165:** Applied as the executive-abuse boundary, with the required culpability identified through the actual emergency setting.
- **Daniels v. Williams, 474 U.S. 327:** Its negligence exclusion remains; the Court resolves the previously open intermediate-culpability question only for this rapid pursuit.
- **Whitley v. Albers, 475 U.S. 312:** Its competing-obligation analysis informs the rule, but its prison-security holding is not treated as already deciding civilian pursuit liability.
- **Farmer v. Brennan, June 6, 1994:** Distinguished by the custodial duty and actual opportunity for response. Its awareness, reasonable-response and permissible-inference rules remain intact.
- **Doe v. Taylor Independent School District, October 16, 1995:** Distinguished because knowing supervisory acquiescence in deliberate abuse requires authority, opportunity and causal participation not supplied merely by continuing this pursuit.
- **Collins v. City of Harker Heights, February 26, 1992:** Its bounded workplace-omission holdings do not automatically resolve affirmative police conduct or merge the underlying right with municipal attribution.
- **Pinder v. Johnson, May 21, 1996:** Its distinction between nonprotection and affirmative deprivation remains; it did not decide this culpability question.
- **Uhlrig v. Harder, February 18, 1997:** Its conduct-time immunity disposition is preserved without importing the lower court's unadopted merits test or a mandatory merits-first sequence.
- **United States v. Lanier, March 31, 1997:** Its rejection of categorical immunity for noncustodial official wrongdoing remains. A governing specific constitutional provision must be used where it covers a claim; that principle does not itself dispose of every due-process claim.
- **Albright v. Oliver, January 24, 1994:** Its fractured judgment supplies no controlling general displacement rationale for this case.
- **Washington v. Glucksberg, June 26, 1997:** Its assistance-in-suicide holding remains limited; no exclusive most-specific-history methodology governing all liberties is established or displaced here.

### Law After Decision

The decision establishes a purpose-to-harm requirement for the supported rapid-pursuit setting and rejects the circuit's use of reckless disregard or deliberate indifference alone there. Existing deliberate-indifference rules retain their distinct duties, opportunities for response and proof requirements. Constitutional wrongdoing remains separate from qualified immunity, municipal attribution and state-law liability. A purposeful or materially more deliberative encounter is not resolved merely by calling it a pursuit.

### Separate Writings

Stevens concurs only in the judgment. He would resolve the individual claim on qualified immunity without deciding the difficult constitutional standard. All relevant law existing on May 22, 1990 must be considered under the particularized-notice approach. Garner and Brower concern intentional force or intentionally applied stopping means; Checki concerns intentional misuse of police power to terrorize; Wood II concerns affirmative interference with protective circumstances. The reported pursuit decisions did not establish this claim's constitutional boundary merely by recognizing a possible egregious case, and Daniels reserved intermediate culpability. Neither the absence of an identical case nor a tally of judicial disagreement decides immunity. On the complete favorable account, the existing law did not make this pursuit's federal unlawfulness sufficiently apparent. Later decisions cannot supply retrospective notice. He makes no finding that the conduct was constitutional.

Scalia, joined by Thomas, concurs in the judgment on a different constitutional ground. The carefully described asserted right against this reckless pursuit and unintended collision lacks sufficient textual, historical or precedential support. A general protection of life or bodily security does not by itself define the constitutional tort. They reject the Court's reliance on an open-ended conscience standard and do not join its affirmative purpose-to-harm definition, although they agree that these facts establish no constitutional violation. Glucksberg does not impose a universally exclusive history method; their objection rests on the absence of adequate support for this particular asserted right and on the limits of judicially defined constitutional tort liability. Their position does not categorically exclude direct intentional assaults outside custody.

Breyer fully joins the Court. He separately preserves flexibility to decide a suitable case on qualified immunity before resolving a difficult or poorly presented constitutional question. That approach is consistent with Uhlrig and the distinction between actual wrongdoing and conduct-time notice. Today's choice to resolve the pursuit standard is not a compulsory merits-first sequence for every later case.

### Procedure After Action

The Ninth Circuit must give effect to reversal of its ruling against Smith and termination of the reviewed federal individual claim. The district court's favorable individual judgment remains in effect. The municipal judgments and unappealed state-law dispositions are not reopened, and no additional evidentiary proceeding or relief is ordered.

### Source Notes

[Lewis v. Sacramento County, 98 F.3d 434](https://static.case.law/f3d/98/html/0434-01.html) supplies the final-judgment posture, favorable pursuit facts and the undisputed absence of an intended collision. Alleged departmental violations remain distinct from federal constitutional culpability. The lower account does not establish that the riders were dangerous felons or that Smith had a purpose to injure. The immunity notice date is May 22, 1990.
